Algorithm Design & Analysis · part 7 of 7

NP-Completeness & Approximation

NP-completeness is the theory of where efficient algorithms stop: a precise sense in which thousands of natural problems — satisfiability, the travelling salesman, vertex cover, subset sum, graph colouring — are equally hard, so that a polynomial-time algorithm for any one of them would give one for all, and the widely-held belief that none exists. This page defines P, NP, NP-hard and NP-complete from the notion of a checkable certificate, builds polynomial-time reductions as the tool that transfers hardness, works the classic chain from satisfiability through clique, vertex cover, Hamiltonian cycle, the travelling salesman, subset sum and colouring with every gadget drawn and every small instance brute-forced, and then turns to what one does next: approximation algorithms with proven ratios (vertex cover, metric TSP, set cover, MAX-3-SAT, the knapsack FPTAS), exact exponential search with pruning, parameterised algorithms, local search and the population heuristics that give up the guarantee, and the SAT and integer-programming solvers that make hard problems routine in practice — every count measured by running the real routine, every answer cross-checked against exhaustive search.

Data Structures & Algorithms P · NP · certificates · reductions Cook–Levin · 3-SAT · Karp reductions Clique · vertex cover · Hamiltonian · subset sum · colouring Approximation ratios · PTAS · FPTAS Branch & bound · FPT · local search · GA SAT solvers · ILP · phase transition 28 visualizations

01 What NP-completeness is

NP-completeness is a proved equivalence among thousands of apparently unrelated computational problems: each of them can be converted into each of the others by a polynomial-time translation of instances, so that a polynomial-time algorithm for any single one of them would yield a polynomial-time algorithm for every one of them — and despite fifty years of effort on hundreds of these problems, no such algorithm has been found. The statement is not "these problems are hard"; nobody has proved that. The statement is "these problems are exactly as hard as one another, and the class of problems they are hardest in contains everything whose solutions can be checked quickly." That is a theorem, and it is the single most useful theorem an engineer can know about the limits of algorithms: it converts the private question "am I not clever enough to solve this?" into the public question "is this problem NP-complete?", which has an answer, a proof technique, and a well-stocked catalogue.

the four definitions the whole page turns onP the decision problems a deterministic algorithm SOLVES in time polynomial in the number of BITS of the input (§04) NP the decision problems whose YES-answers have a certificate that a deterministic algorithm CHECKS in polynomial time (§05) NP-hard a problem X such that EVERY L ∈ NP reduces to it in polynomial time: ∀ L ∈ NP, L ≤ₚ X — X need NOT be in NP, need not even be decidable (§07) NP-complete a problem that is NP-hard AND in NP (§07) P ⊆ NP is immediate (an algorithm that decides L ignores the certificate). Whether the inclusion is STRICT is open. the transfer rule — the one mechanism behind every hardness proof on this pageA ≤ₚ B ("A reduces to B") means: a polynomial-time computable map f with x ∈ A ⟺ f(x) ∈ B (§06) then B ∈ P ⟹ A ∈ P (solve A by translating and calling B's algorithm) — the USEFUL direction and A ∉ P ⟹ B ∉ P (contrapositive) — the HARDNESS direction so A NP-hard and A ≤ₚ B ⟹ B NP-hard (transitivity) — the CATALOGUE direction the consequence that gives the class its nameif ANY NP-complete problem X has a polynomial-time algorithm, then P = NP: take any L ∈ NP; by X's NP-hardness, L ≤ₚ X by some f computable in time O(nᶜ); X decides in O(mᵏ); the composite decides L in O(nᶜ) + O((nᶜ)ᵏ) = O(nᶜᵏ) — polynomial, because polynomials compose. conversely if P ≠ NP then NO NP-complete problem has one. So the thousands of problems stand or fall together.

Where it sits

Everything earlier in this series assumed the problem in front of it was solvable, and asked how fast. Algorithm Analysis built the language — O, Θ, Ω, recurrences, the growth hierarchy — and §04 and §26 of this page are that language applied to the gap between nᵏ and 2ⁿ; its §10 table of how large an input each growth rate admits is the practical content of the word "intractable". Dynamic Programming gave O(nW) for the 0-1 knapsack and O(n²·2ⁿ) time — over Θ(n·2ⁿ) subset states — for the travelling salesman; §02 explains why the first of those is not a polynomial-time algorithm and §14 and §26 explain exactly what it is instead. Greedy Algorithms showed where a local rule provably succeeds — matroids, the cut property, Huffman — and where it provably fails, and its §22 on set cover and §23 on load balancing are the first two approximation guarantees in the series — set cover is re-proved and measured here in §23, and both are placed in the taxonomy of §20. Shortest Paths, MST & Flow is the largest collection of problems in the series that are in P, and §16 uses it as the far side of the boundary: shortest path is easy and longest simple path is NP-hard on the same graph, minimum cut is easy and maximum cut is NP-hard, 2-colouring is easy and 3-colouring is NP-hard. Graph Representations & Traversal supplies the objects almost every hard problem on this page is stated over, and §02 uses its §08 cost table to make the point that "the size of the input" is a matter of representation. Sorting & Searching contains the series' only unconditional lower bound — Ω(n log n) comparisons, proved by a decision-tree counting argument — and §07 contrasts it sharply with what this page can and cannot prove: there is no unconditional lower bound for any NP-complete problem, and producing one would settle P vs NP.

The thesis: hardness is a relation, not a property

The organising idea is that the page never proves a problem hard in absolute terms. It proves that one problem is at least as hard as another, by exhibiting a translation, and then chains those statements. Cook and Levin supplied the root of the chain independently in 1971 and 1973: satisfiability is at least as hard as every problem in NP, proved not by a translation from one particular problem but by a translation from an arbitrary polynomial-time verifier, whose computation is encoded as a Boolean formula (§08). Karp supplied the next twenty branches in 1972 — his list of twenty-one NP-complete problems counts satisfiability itself — and the catalogue has grown to thousands since. Each branch is a gadget: a local construction that turns a variable into a triangle, a clause into a widget, a choice into a pair of parallel edges, in such a way that solutions correspond. So the skill this page teaches is not "recognising hard problems by their look"; it is (a) recognising the catalogue entry your problem resembles, (b) constructing the map and proving both directions of the ⟺, and (c) knowing what to do afterwards — which is the second half of the page, and in working life much the larger half.

The second organising idea is that "NP-complete" is not a verdict of impossibility. It is a statement about worst-case, exact, general-instance solving of the decision version. Every one of those four qualifiers is a door: worst case opens onto instances that are easy in practice and onto the phase transition that explains why (§30); exact opens onto approximation algorithms with proved ratios (§20–§25); general instance opens onto parameterised algorithms that are polynomial once a structural parameter is bounded (§27); and decision version reminds you that the optimisation problem you actually face may be attacked by branch and bound, by a solver, or by local search that carries no guarantee at all and works (§26, §28–§30). A proof of NP-completeness is therefore best read as a routing decision: it tells you to stop hunting for an exact polynomial algorithm and start choosing among four well-understood responses (§19, §32).

What is and is not claimed

  • Claimed, and proved: that every problem in NP reduces to 3-SAT, to clique, to vertex cover, to Hamiltonian cycle, to subset sum, to 3-colouring, and so on down the catalogue; that a polynomial algorithm for any of them collapses the whole class into P; that certain approximation ratios are achievable, with the proofs given here; and that certain ratios are not achievable unless P = NP.
  • Believed, and unproved: that P ≠ NP. This page never writes "no polynomial-time algorithm exists" for an NP-complete problem. It writes "none is known, and its existence would imply P = NP", which is what the mathematics supports. The belief is strong — it is the standard working hypothesis of cryptography, of complexity theory, and of the whole literature of approximation lower bounds — but it is a hypothesis.
  • Not claimed: that NP-complete instances are hard in practice. Modern SAT solvers dispatch industrial formulas with millions of variables (§30), integer programming solves scheduling problems with hundreds of thousands of binaries, and the instances that defeat them are usually engineered. NP-completeness is a statement about the worst case over all instances, and the worst case is a small and peculiar corner of instance space.
  • Frequently confused, and separated here: NP-hard is not NP-complete (an NP-hard problem need not be in NP — the halting problem is NP-hard and not even decidable, §18); "NP" does not abbreviate "non-polynomial" (it abbreviates nondeterministic polynomial, §05); an optimisation problem is normally NP-hard rather than NP-complete, while its decision version is NP-complete (§03); and O(nW) knapsack is pseudo-polynomial, not polynomial (§02, §14).

The catalogue, and how this page walks it

  • The machinery — what an encoding is, why "polynomial in the input" means "polynomial in the bit length", and why that one distinction decides whether knapsack's O(nW) counts (§02); decision, search and optimisation as three faces of one problem, and self-reducibility, which lets a decision oracle build an actual solution with n extra queries (§03); the class P and the three reasons it is the right robustness notion, with the honest caveats about n¹⁰⁰ and galactic constants (§04); the class NP through certificates and verifiers, written out for three problems (§05); polynomial-time reductions, both directions of the correctness condition, and the direction trap that ruins half of all first attempts (§06); NP-hard against NP-complete, the two worlds P = NP and P ≠ NP, Ladner's theorem, and what a collapse would and would not mean (§07).
  • The root and the first branch — circuit satisfiability, and the Cook–Levin theorem with the tableau, the cell variables and the four clause families given in full rather than waved at (§08); SAT, CNF-SAT and 3-SAT, with the clause-splitting gadget and its proof of equisatisfiability in both directions, why DNF-SAT is easy and why that is not a contradiction (§09).
  • The reduction catalogue — the map of which classic reduces to which, and why the chain is shaped the way it is (§10); 3-SAT ≤ₚ Clique, gadget by gadget (§11); Clique ≤ₚ Vertex Cover ≤ₚ Independent Set, the complementation triangle (§12); Hamiltonian cycle, and Hamiltonian cycle ≤ₚ TSP (§13); subset sum, partition and knapsack, and what "pseudo-polynomial" and "strongly NP-hard" mean (§14); graph colouring — 3-colouring hard, 2-colouring easy — with the gadget (§15); where exactly the boundary between easy and hard runs, problem pair by problem pair (§16); co-NP, NP ∩ co-NP, and the problems whose status is genuinely unknown (§17); PSPACE, EXPTIME, the provably intractable, and the undecidable (§18).
  • Coping — the four responses to a hardness proof, stated as a decision (§19); approximation ratios, the conventions, and the APX / PTAS / FPTAS taxonomy (§20); vertex cover's 2-approximation from a maximal matching, with the tight instance (§21); the travelling salesman — double-tree 2, Christofides 3/2, and why the general problem admits no constant ratio unless P = NP (§22); set cover's greedy algorithm and the H(d) ≤ 1 + ln d bound (§23); MAX-3-SAT at 7/8 by coin flip, then derandomised by conditional expectations (§24); and the knapsack FPTAS, where rounding the values buys 1 − ε at a price polynomial in 1/ε (§25).
  • Solving anyway — exact exponential algorithms: backtracking, branch and bound, and dynamic programming over subsets (§26); parameterised complexity: FPT, kernelisation, bounded search trees (§27); local search, 2-opt and simulated annealing (§28); population heuristics and genetic algorithms (§29); SAT solvers, integer programming, and the phase transition that explains why random instances are usually easy (§30); NP-hardness where it actually bites in machine learning (§31); choosing a response as a decision procedure (§32); pitfalls (§33); and the cheat sheet, with the load-time audit that re-checks every routine on this page against exhaustive search (§34).
How to read the figures. Every number this page displays is measured, not asserted. Counts of assignments tried, clauses checked, search-tree nodes, oracle queries, reduction sizes and approximation ratios are produced by running the routine under a counter and reading the counter back; every answer a figure reports is recomputed in the same figure by an independent exhaustive search over all assignments, subsets, tours, colourings or covers, and the two are compared on screen with an ✓ agree or ✗ DISAGREE marker. Where a claim in the prose is a count — "the conversion of §09 turns 5 clauses into 12", "the Tseitin encoding of the circuit in §08 has 15 clauses", "the self-reduction of §03 needs 3 oracle queries" — the figure under it re-derives that count when the page loads, so a stale number announces itself.

02 Problems, encodings and the size of an input

A concrete problem is a set of binary strings — the encodings of its yes-instances — and "polynomial time" means polynomial in the number of bits of that string, never in the numeric value of anything written on it. That single sentence carries more weight on this page than any other, because it is what makes O(nW) knapsack not a polynomial algorithm, what makes trial-division primality an exponential one, and what makes the whole theory encoding-independent in the first place — provided the encodings you allow are reasonable. Get it wrong and half the catalogue collapses: subset sum looks polynomial, partition looks polynomial, and NP-completeness looks like a mistake. Get it right and the theory has a stable foundation on which every later claim can be stated exactly.

the three-step formalisationABSTRACT PROBLEM a relation between a set I of instances and a set S of solutions e.g. SHORTEST-PATH relates ⟨G, u, v⟩ to a lightest u ⇝ v path DECISION PROBLEM an abstract problem whose solution set is {0, 1}: a function Q : I → {0, 1} e.g. PATH(⟨G, u, v, k⟩) = 1 iff some u ⇝ v path uses at most k edges ENCODING a map e : I → {0,1}* turning each instance into a bit string CONCRETE PROBLEM the induced set of strings. Identify it with the LANGUAGE of its yes-instances: L = { e(i) : i ∈ I and Q(i) = 1 } ⊆ {0,1}* "solving the problem" = deciding membership in L; "input size" n = |e(i)|, in bits the definition that everything rests onan algorithm SOLVES a concrete problem in time O(T(n)) if on every instance string of length n it answers in O(T(n)) steps P = { L ⊆ {0,1}* : some algorithm decides L in time O(nᵏ) for a constant k } DECIDES ≠ ACCEPTS. An accepter must output 1 on every x ∈ L (and may run forever on x ∉ L); a decider must halt on every input with the right answer. For polynomial time the two coincide — simulate the accepter for its own c·nᵏ step bound and output 0 if it has not accepted by then.

Why the encoding is not a technicality

Take an algorithm whose only input is a positive integer k and whose running time is Θ(k) — a loop that counts down. Written in unary, k is a string of k ones, so the input length is n = k and the algorithm runs in Θ(n): linear, polynomial, tractable. Written in binary, k is n = ⌊log₂ k⌋ + 1 bits, so the same loop runs in Θ(k) = Θ(2ⁿ): exponential. Nothing about the algorithm changed. The problem changed, because the problem is a set of strings and the strings are different. Unary is a pathological encoding precisely because it is exponentially longer than it needs to be: it buys the algorithm a budget proportional to the value it is reading, which is the whole of its apparent efficiency.

Rule out that pathology and the definition becomes stable. Two encodings e₁, e₂ of the same instance set are polynomially related when polynomial-time computable functions translate each into the other. The lemma is then one paragraph: if e₁(Q) ∈ P and |e₁(i)| is computable from e₂(i) in time O(nᶜ), then on input e₂(i) convert (cost O(nᶜ), so the converted string has length O(nᶜ) — an algorithm cannot write more than it has time for) and run the O(mᵏ) decider on it, for a total of O(nᶜ) + O(nᶜᵏ) = O(nᶜᵏ). Polynomially related encodings therefore agree about membership in P. Base 2, base 3, base 10, ASCII decimal, an adjacency matrix against an adjacency list, a list of edges against a list of neighbours — all polynomially related, all interchangeable for this purpose. Unary against binary is not, and that is the only common encoding that is not.

the standard encodings this page assumes, and the input size each givesINTEGER k binary (or any base ≥ 2): ⌈log₂(k + 1)⌉ bits. UNARY (k ones) is banned except where §14 needs it on purpose. A LIST OF n INTEGERS n·(bits per integer) + separators — so "n items of weight ≤ W" has size Θ(n log W), NOT Θ(nW) GRAPH, adjacency matrix n² bits — size Θ(n²) whatever m is (Graphs, §04) GRAPH, adjacency list Θ((n + m)·log n) bits — size Θ(n + m) up to the log (Graphs, §05) CNF FORMULA Θ(L·log n) bits for total literal count L over n vars "POLYNOMIAL IN n" for a graph: polynomial in n + m. The two representations are polynomially related (m ≤ n², and the matrix is built from the list in O(n²)), so P is the same class either way. the three standard traps, each an algorithm that is polynomial in a VALUE and exponential in a SIZE running time input size n time in terms of n verdict 0-1 KNAPSACK DP Θ(n·W) Θ(n log W) Θ(n·2^(n bits of W)) PSEUDO-polynomial, not polynomial (§14, §25) SUBSET SUM DP Θ(n·T) Θ(n log T) same shape PSEUDO-polynomial (§14) PRIMALITY by trial / Θ(√N) Θ(log N) = b bits Θ(2^(b/2)) EXPONENTIAL in the input size division (primality IS in P — by AKS, 2002 — but not by this algorithm)

Pseudo-polynomial, stated exactly

An algorithm is pseudo-polynomial when its running time is polynomial in the input size and in the largest numeric value appearing in the input — equivalently, when it would be polynomial if the numbers were written in unary. The knapsack table of Dynamic Programming, §10 has (n + 1)(W + 1) entries, of which the n(W + 1) outside the initial row are each computed in constant time. On this page's five-item instance of §25, with n = 5 and W = 15, that is 6 × 16 = 96 entries and 80 computed cells — the same 96 / 80 split that §25's value-indexed table shows at ε = 1, where the scaled values also total 15 — and W = 15 occupies four bits. Double the capacity to W = 31: the input grows by one bit, and the table doubles to 6 × 32 = 192 entries, 160 of them computed. At W = 1023, ten bits, it is 6 144 entries and 5 120 computed cells. The work is proportional to 2^(bits of W), which is the definition of exponential in the input size. The algorithm is genuinely useful — when W is small it is the best thing available, and §25 turns it into an approximation scheme by making the numbers small on purpose — but it is not a polynomial-time algorithm and its existence does not put knapsack in P.

The contrast that makes the notion precise is strong NP-hardness (§14): a problem is strongly NP-hard when it remains NP-hard even if all the numbers in the instance are bounded by a polynomial in the instance size — that is, even under unary encoding. Subset sum, partition and knapsack are not strongly NP-hard, which is exactly why they have pseudo-polynomial algorithms and why knapsack has a fully polynomial-time approximation scheme (§25). The travelling salesman with arbitrary distances, graph colouring, 3-SAT and clique are strongly NP-hard: their difficulty has nothing to do with the magnitude of any number, so no pseudo-polynomial algorithm can exist for them unless P = NP — and, for an optimisation problem whose optimum is bounded by a polynomial in the instance size and the largest number in it, no FPTAS either, since an FPTAS run with ε small enough would be exactly such an algorithm (§20).

Where "the size of the input" bites in graph problems

A graph on n vertices and m edges has input size Θ(n²) if you hand it over as an adjacency matrix and Θ(n + m) — up to a log n per identifier — if you hand it over as adjacency lists, as Graph Representations & Traversal, §08 tabulates. For dense graphs the two agree to within a constant. For sparse ones they differ by a factor of n, and an algorithm that is linear in one may be sub-linear in the other: reading a graph out of a matrix takes Θ(n²) whatever m is, which is why the dense/sparse distinction of that page matters even here. None of this changes membership in P — the representations are polynomially related — but it changes what the word "linear" refers to, and a claimed "linear-time" algorithm should always be read with its representation attached. The same caution applies to the weighted problems of Shortest Paths, MST & Flow: Dijkstra's O((n + m) log n) is polynomial in n + m and says nothing about the magnitude of the weights, while Dial's bucket queue on that page's §11 is O(m + n·C) for integer weights bounded by C — a pseudo-polynomial bound of exactly the knapsack shape, fast when C is small and exponential in the bits of C.

Unary against binary: the length of the input, and the measured cost of a loop that is linear in the value both axes are logarithmic; the slider picks a value k and the readout reports the measured step counts. The loop's cost tracks the unary length exactly and the binary length exponentially — that gap is the whole content of "polynomial in the size of the input"
37

Decision problems as languages, and why that is convenient

Once a decision problem is a set of strings, the whole vocabulary of formal languages is available for free and costs nothing. An alphabet Σ is a finite symbol set; a language over Σ is any subset of Σ*; complement, union, intersection, concatenation and Kleene star are set operations; and a complexity class is a set of languages singled out by a resource bound on a machine that decides them. P is one such set. NP (§05) is another. co-NP (§17) is the set of complements of NP languages, and the fact that P is closed under complement while NP is not known to be is a statement that only makes sense once problems are languages. The framework also makes the convention about malformed inputs harmless: some bit strings encode no instance at all, and by fiat they are mapped to 0, so L is a perfectly well-defined subset of {0,1}* and a decider is required to reject the garbage as well as the no-instances.

The one place this abstraction must be handled carefully is in reading an algorithm's time bound. Θ(n²) for an algorithm on a graph normally means n = |V|, not n = the length of the encoding; O(nW) for knapsack means n = the number of items; O(2ⁿ) for SAT means n = the number of variables. These are all legitimate and all standard, and none of them is the bit length. Translating into bit length is a step you must do explicitly, and it is exactly where the pseudo-polynomial algorithms reveal themselves: n items and a capacity of W take Θ(n log W) bits, so O(nW) is O(n·2^(bits of W)). For graph problems the translation is benign — n vertices take at least n bits to name, so anything polynomial in n + m is polynomial in the bit length and conversely — which is why the graph chapters of this series can be sloppy about it and this page cannot.

The rule to carry forward. Whenever a running time mentions a number from the input rather than a count of items in the input — W, T, C, N, the largest weight, the target sum, the maximum value — the bound is pseudo-polynomial until proved otherwise, and to check it you write that number in binary and ask what the time is as a function of its bit length. Whenever a running time mentions only counts — n items, m edges, L literals, k sets — a polynomial in those counts is a polynomial in the input size, because the input must at minimum name each of them. §14 returns to this with subset sum's table drawn twice, once against T and once against log T, and §25 turns the weakness into a technique.

03 Decision, search and optimisation — and self-reducibility

Every optimisation problem on this page comes in three forms — decide whether a solution of a given quality exists, find one, and find the best one — and the three are polynomially equivalent for all of the classic problems, so complexity theory may work exclusively with the yes/no form without giving anything up. The equivalence is not a definition; it is a pair of constructions. Optimisation reduces to decision by binary search over the objective value. Search reduces to decision by self-reducibility: a decision oracle, asked n carefully chosen questions, hands back an actual satisfying assignment or an actual clique. Because those constructions add only a polynomial overhead, the sentence "SAT is NP-complete" carries the full practical content "if you could decide satisfiability quickly you could also produce the assignment quickly", which is what anyone building on the result actually wants.

three faces of one problem, in the case of cliqueDECISION CLIQUE = { ⟨G, k⟩ : G has a clique of size ≥ k } → yes / no SEARCH given ⟨G, k⟩, OUTPUT a clique of size k, or report that none exists → a witness OPTIMISATION given G, OUTPUT a clique of MAXIMUM size ω(G) → the best witness and in the case of the travelling salesman: DECISION TSP = { ⟨G, c, k⟩ : G has a Hamiltonian cycle of cost ≤ k } — NP-COMPLETE (in NP: the tour is the certificate) OPTIMISATION TSP-OPT = find the cheapest Hamiltonian cycle — NP-HARD, and NOT known to be in NP (the answer is a number; certifying that no cheaper tour exists is a co-NP-flavoured claim, §17) the standard reductions between the three, with their costsDECISION ≤ₚ SEARCH ≤ₚ OPTIMISATION trivial: a better oracle answers a weaker question in one call OPTIMISATION ≤ₚ DECISION binary search on the objective. If the optimum is an integer in [0, V], ⌈log₂(V + 1)⌉ calls pin it down; V ≤ 2^(input bits), so that is ≤ (input bits) + 1 calls — POLYNOMIAL. (V must be bounded by an exponential in the input size — it always is: the answer is written on the input.) SEARCH ≤ₚ DECISION SELF-REDUCIBILITY. Fix one bit of the solution at a time and ask the oracle whether the remaining problem is still a yes-instance. n calls for n bits.

Why theory chooses the decision form

Three reasons, all structural. First, a decision problem is a language (§02), so the entire apparatus of formal-language theory — complement, closure, classes defined by machines — applies without adaptation, whereas an optimisation problem is a relation and has no complement in any useful sense. Second, the certificate definition of NP (§05) only makes sense for yes/no questions: a certificate is evidence for a yes, and there is nothing for it to be evidence of in "the maximum clique has size 3". Third, the decision form is never harder than the optimisation form, so a hardness proof for the decision form is the stronger statement — and it is hardness we are usually proving. Saying "TSP-DECISION is NP-complete" already implies that nobody will solve TSP-OPT in polynomial time unless P = NP, because the decision version reduces to the optimisation version in one call.

The price is a vocabulary trap this page takes care over. The optimisation problem is NP-hard and is normally not NP-complete, because NP-completeness requires membership in NP and an optimisation problem is not a decision problem at all. Written correctly: TSP-DECISION is NP-complete; TSP-OPT is NP-hard. The same holds for maximum clique, minimum vertex cover, minimum colouring, maximum cut, and every other optimisation problem in the catalogue. Loose usage — "TSP is NP-complete" — is everywhere in the literature and is harmless shorthand among people who know what they mean, but it is exactly the confusion §07 is written to prevent, so this page always names which version it means.

Self-reducibility, made concrete: a SAT oracle that hands back an assignment

Suppose a black box answers, in one step, "is this CNF formula satisfiable?" — and nothing else. It never shows an assignment. Here is how to extract one with n queries for n variables. Ask the box about φ itself; if the answer is no, stop and report unsatisfiable. Otherwise ask about φ|x₁ = TRUE, the formula with x₁ substituted (clauses containing the literal x₁ are deleted, occurrences of ¬x₁ are struck out of their clauses). If the answer is yes, fix x₁ = TRUE; if no, then because φ was satisfiable every satisfying assignment must have x₁ = FALSE, so fix that instead. Repeat for x₂ under the value now fixed for x₁, and so on. After n rounds every variable has a value, and the invariant maintained throughout — the formula restricted to the values fixed so far is satisfiable — is true at the end, when no free variables remain, which means the fixed assignment satisfies φ.

On the page's running formula φ = (x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ ¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) ∧ (x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) — which has exactly 5 satisfying assignments out of 8, namely 001, 010, 101, 110 and 111 in the bit order x₁x₂x₃ — the procedure runs like this. A preliminary query establishes that φ is satisfiable at all — yes. Then, one query per variable: is φ|x₁=T satisfiable? Yes (101, 110, 111 survive), so x₁ = TRUE. Is φ|x₁=T, x₂=T satisfiable? Yes (110, 111), so x₂ = TRUE. Is φ|x₁=T, x₂=T, x₃=T satisfiable? Yes (111), so x₃ = TRUE. Four queries in all — one preliminary plus n = 3 — and the assignment 111 comes out; substituting it gives (1∨0∨0) ∧ (0∨1∨1) ∧ (1∨1∨1) = TRUE, which the figure re-checks against the exhaustive list of satisfying assignments. Note what the greedy "try TRUE first" rule does: it always lands on the lexicographically largest satisfying assignment under that ordering, and the oracle's three answers are exactly the branch decisions that a backtracking search would make without ever having to backtrack — which is the whole point. The decision oracle removes the search.

The same trick for clique, and why it is not universal

For clique the natural self-reduction deletes vertices instead of fixing bits. First find the optimum size k = ω(G) by binary search on the decision oracle (or, since k ≤ n, by n queries). Then walk the vertices in any fixed order and, for each v, ask the oracle whether G − v still has a clique of size k: if yes, delete v permanently, because some k-clique avoids it; if no, keep v, because every k-clique uses it. The invariant is the current graph still contains a clique of size k, and when the walk ends the surviving vertex set cannot be shrunk further, so it is exactly a k-clique. On the seven-vertex demonstration graph of §12 the maximum clique has size 3 and there are exactly 2 triangles, {c, d, e} and {d, e, f}; the deletion walk in alphabetical order deletes a, b and c — each time a triangle survives without it — and then keeps d, e and f, returning {d, e, f} after exactly 7 further queries, one per vertex, on top of whatever the search for k cost. Which triangle you get depends on the vertex order, and the figure lets you change it.

Self-reducibility is a property of a problem, not a theorem about NP. It holds for every NP-complete problem in the standard catalogue, because for each of them there is an obvious "fix one bit / delete one element and ask again" step whose sub-instance is an instance of the same problem. It is not known to hold for every problem in NP — that is a genuine open question, tangled with whether P = NP — and there are artificial NP languages for which no such procedure is known. The honest statement is: for every problem this page names, a polynomial-time decision algorithm would yield a polynomial-time search algorithm, by the explicit construction above; so nothing is lost by studying only the decision versions.

Self-reducibility: recovering a whole solution from a decision oracle, one query per variable each step is one oracle call; the tree shows the branch taken and the branch the oracle ruled out. The readout counts the queries, counts the work the brute-force oracle did inside each call, and checks the recovered solution against the exhaustive list

Binary search on the objective, with the one condition it needs

The reduction from optimisation to decision is binary search, and it is worth stating its condition because it is the only thing that can go wrong. If the objective takes integer values in [0, V], then ⌈log₂(V + 1)⌉ decision queries locate the optimum exactly. That is polynomial in the input size provided V ≤ 2^poly(n) — provided, in other words, that the optimum is not astronomically larger than anything written on the input. It never is: for clique and vertex cover the optimum is at most n, so ⌈log₂(n+1)⌉ queries suffice; for TSP it is at most the sum of all the edge costs, whose binary length is at most the length of the input, so the number of queries is at most the number of input bits. If the objective is not integral — a Euclidean tour length, say — the search returns an interval rather than a value, and one falls back on the self-reduction to produce the tour and then evaluates it, which is what the figures of §22 do. The pattern "decide, then binary-search, then self-reduce" is the standard way a theoretical result about a decision problem is cashed out as a program that prints an answer, and it costs a factor of O(n + log V) oracle calls in total.

A fourth face: counting, which is harder than all three

There is one more version of every problem on this page, and it does not join the equivalence. Counting asks not whether a solution exists but how many there are: how many satisfying assignments does φ have (5, out of 8, for the page's running formula), how many 3-cliques does a graph contain (2 in the seven-vertex graph of §12, and 12 in the nine-vertex graph the reduction of §11 builds from φ), how many subsets of {3, 7, 11, 14, 19, 23} total 37 (exactly 3). Those numbers are what the figures on this page use for cross-checking, and computing them is a strictly different problem: the counting versions live in the class #P, and #SAT is #P-complete just as SAT is NP-complete.

The reason counting deserves a separate paragraph rather than a footnote is that it is hard even when the decision problem is easy. Deciding whether a bipartite graph has a perfect matching is in P — it is a flow problem, solved on Shortest Paths, MST & Flow, §25 — but counting its perfect matchings is #P-complete, which is Valiant's 1979 theorem about the permanent. So the easy/hard boundary of §16 is drawn for decision problems and does not transfer to counting; a self-reduction that produces one solution says nothing about how many there are. Toda's theorem (1991) makes the gap precise in the other direction: the whole polynomial hierarchy (§18) reduces to #P with a single query, so a #P oracle is at least as useful as an oracle for any fixed number of alternating quantifiers. When a problem on this page is described as solved by "sampling solutions" or "estimating how many" — which is what several machine-learning applications in §31 actually need — the relevant hardness is #P's, not NP's, and the approximation theory is a different one (approximate counting, and the Markov-chain methods that support it).

The phrase to get right. "X is NP-complete" is a statement about a decision problem. "X is NP-hard" is a statement about any problem — decision, search, optimisation, function, even undecidable ones — that is at least as hard as everything in NP. The optimisation versions of clique, vertex cover, TSP, colouring, set cover, subset sum and knapsack in this page's catalogue are NP-hard; their decision versions are NP-complete; and the two facts are linked by exactly the constructions above. §07 gives the formal definitions and §33 lists the ways the pair is misquoted.

04 P: polynomial time, and why the class is robust

P is the set of decision problems some algorithm decides in O(nᵏ) steps for a fixed k, with n the input length in bits — and the reason this coarse, constant-blind, exponent-blind definition has survived sixty years is that it is the smallest natural class that is invariant under everything an engineer would consider an implementation detail. Change the machine model, change the encoding, change the programming language, pipe one algorithm's output into another's input, call a subroutine a constant number of times: the class does not move. That invariance is what lets a theorem proved about Turing machines in 1971 say something about a Python program in 2026. The identification of P with "tractable" — the Cobham–Edmonds thesis — is a separate and much weaker claim, and this section is careful to state where it holds and where it visibly does not.

the definition, and the two equivalent phrasingsP = { L ⊆ {0,1}* : ∃ algorithm A, ∃ constant k, A decides L, and A on every input of length n halts within O(nᵏ) steps } = { L : L is ACCEPTED by some polynomial-time algorithm } — the two coincide: simulate the accepter for its own step bound (§02) = { L : L is decided by a polynomial-time deterministic Turing machine } — model-independence, below note the quantifier order: ∃k ∀n, not ∀n ∃k. ONE exponent must work for all input lengths. note what is NOT bounded: the constant factor, the exponent's size, the memory used (bounded only by the time: space ≤ time). the three robustness facts — why the class does not depend on the machineMODEL INVARIANCE a t-step MULTI-TAPE Turing machine computation is simulated by a single-tape machine in O(t²) steps; a t-step RANDOM-ACCESS machine computation (unit cost on O(log n)-bit words) is simulated by a Turing machine in time polynomial in t, the exponent depending on the accounting; and a parallel machine with a POLYNOMIAL number of processors buys only a polynomial factor. Every one of these is a polynomial of a polynomial, which is a polynomial. So P is the same class in all of them. (The caveat: unit-cost arithmetic on UNBOUNDED integers is NOT a reasonable model — repeated squaring builds a 2ⁿ-bit number in n steps. Charge O(bits) per arithmetic operation and the invariance returns.) CLOSURE UNDER f, g polynomial-time computable ⟹ g ∘ f polynomial-time computable: COMPOSITION |f(x)| ≤ time to write it = O(nᶜ), so g costs O((nᶜ)ᵈ) = O(nᶜᵈ). This is the fact §06 needs for transitivity. A CONSTANT number of calls to polynomial subroutines, plus polynomial glue, is polynomial. A POLYNOMIAL number of calls is still polynomial. An EXPONENTIAL number is not — which is why §26 exists. CLOSURE UNDER L ∈ P ⟹ complement of L ∈ P: run the decider and flip the bit. P = co-P. COMPLEMENT NP is NOT known to have this property — that asymmetry is the whole of §17.

Why "polynomial" and not something tighter

The honest answer is that no tighter class is robust. "Linear time" depends on the machine model — what is linear on a random-access machine is quadratic on a single-tape Turing machine. "O(n log n)" is not closed under composition. "O(n³)" is not closed under composition either; feed one cubic algorithm's output into another and you get O(n⁹). The polynomials are the smallest family of growth rates closed under addition, multiplication and composition, which is precisely the algebra of "build a program out of parts", so they are the smallest family in which the phrase "and then call the subroutine" is free. Everything else in the definition follows from wanting that one closure property. Edmonds in 1965 and Cobham in 1964 arrived at the class from opposite directions — Edmonds wanting a name for what his matching algorithm achieved and brute force did not, Cobham wanting a machine-independent characterisation of feasible functions — and the coincidence is the usual sign that a definition is the right one.

The complement of the argument is also worth stating. The reason "exponential" is the other side of the line is not that 2ⁿ is a magic function but that exponentials are closed under the same operations in the wrong direction: compose two exponentials and you leave the class entirely, and multiply an exponential by a polynomial and you are still exponential, so the constant factors and the exponent tuning that dominate practical performance inside P are invisible at that scale. A 1 000-fold faster machine multiplies the largest tractable n by 1 000 for a linear algorithm and by 10 for a cubic one, but adds about log₂ 1000 ≈ 10 to it for a 2ⁿ algorithm: measured against the same budget the figure uses, a cubic routine's reach goes from n = 1 000 to n = 10 000 while a 2ⁿ routine's goes from n = 29 to n = 39. That asymmetry — hardware multiplies polynomial reach and merely adds to exponential reach — is the practical content of the distinction, and §26 returns to it when a 1.3ⁿ algorithm turns out to be worth building.

How large an input fits in a fixed budget

The table below fixes a budget of 10⁹ elementary operations — roughly one second on one core of a contemporary machine, which is the only piece of hardware folklore in it — and asks, for each growth rate, the largest n whose operation count stays inside the budget. The counts themselves are not assumed: the figure runs routines that actually perform n, n², n³, 2ⁿ and n! units of work, counts them, checks the measured counts against the closed forms, and only then extrapolates the closed forms to the budget.

growthlargest n in 10⁹ opslargest n in 3.6×10¹² ops (an hour)what an extra hour buysexample on this page
n1 000 000 0003.6 × 10¹²× 3 600reading the input; BFS on an adjacency list
n log₂ n39 620 07798 574 774 544 ≈ 9.9 × 10¹⁰× ≈ 2 500sorting (Sorting & Searching, §08)
n²31 6221 897 366× 60all pairs of items; the 2-approximation of §21 on a dense graph
n³1 00015 326× ≈ 15Floyd–Warshall; matrix multiplication as usually written

One caution the figure enforces and the table cannot: a crossover is a point beyond which one curve stays above the other, not the first point at which it is larger. Measured, 2ⁿ is already larger than n² at n = 1 (2 against 1), exactly equal at n = 2 (4 = 4) and again at n = 4 (16 = 16), and only from n = 5 onward is it permanently larger; 2ⁿ overtakes n³ for good at n = 10, where 1 024 beats 1 000 by 24 operations; and n! overtakes 2ⁿ at n = 4 (24 against 16), having trailed it at n = 1, 2, 3 (1 against 2, 2 against 4, 6 against 8) — the two never tie for n ≥ 1. Every one of those numbers is read off the figure's measured arrays.

Read the last column as the real lesson. Inside P, waiting or buying hardware moves the frontier multiplicatively; outside it, additively. An exact exponential algorithm is not thereby useless — a 1.5ⁿ routine reaching n = 51 solves plenty of real instances, and §26 and §27 are about pushing exactly that base down — but no amount of engineering turns + 12 into × 3 600.

Measured growth: real routines run at increasing n, with every plotted point a counted operation each curve is the counter's value after running a routine that enumerates n items, all n² pairs, all n³ triples, all 2ⁿ subsets or all n! permutations; the crossovers are read off the measured arrays, not asserted, and the readout checks each measurement against its closed form
14

The caveats, stated plainly

  • n¹⁰⁰ is in P and is useless. The definition puts no bound on k, and an algorithm running in n¹⁰⁰ steps is unusable at n = 2. The empirical defence is that this almost never happens: the polynomial-time algorithms that arise in practice have small exponents, and once any polynomial algorithm is known for a problem, better ones have historically followed quickly. That is an observation about the history of the field, not a theorem, and it should be quoted as such.
  • Galactic algorithms. Some polynomial algorithms have constants so large that no instance in this universe is big enough for them to win. The graph-minor theorem yields O(n³) — later O(n²) — recognition algorithms for every minor-closed graph property, with constants that depend on the excluded minors and are beyond astronomical, and the proof is non-constructive: it shows the algorithm exists without exhibiting its obstruction set. The fast matrix-multiplication exponents below 2.372 are similar: the improvements over Strassen's n^2.807 are real theorems with constants that rule out use. A membership proof in P is a statement about asymptotics and nothing else.
  • The constant factor is where real engineering lives. Two O(n log n) sorts can differ tenfold on cache behaviour alone, as Algorithm Analysis, §11 shows and Sorting & Searching, §17 measures. None of that is visible at this page's resolution, and none of it is the point here; the distinction this page draws is between nᵏ and cⁿ, which dwarfs every constant.
  • Polynomial in P does not mean "known to be polynomial". Linear programming was suspected polynomial for decades before the ellipsoid method (1979) proved it, while the simplex method in practice was and is far faster than either polynomial algorithm on real instances and still has exponential worst-case behaviour on Klee–Minty cubes. Primality testing entered P only in 2002, by an algorithm whose original bound was about Õ(b¹²) in the number of bits b, later improved to about Õ(b⁶) — and in practice probabilistic tests are used instead. Membership in P is a mathematical fact with an uneven relationship to what anyone runs.
  • Space is bounded by time. An algorithm that runs in O(nᵏ) steps touches at most O(nᵏ) cells, so P ⊆ PSPACE (§18) with no further argument. The converse is not known, and §18 explains what PSPACE contains that NP is not known to.

What P measures, and the neighbouring resources it does not

P fixes one resource — deterministic time — and holds everything else free. Vary the resource and you get the neighbouring classes, each of which stands in an interesting and mostly unresolved relation to P, and §18 returns to the ones that matter.

  • Space. L is logarithmic work space, PSPACE polynomial space. L ⊆ P ⊆ NP ⊆ PSPACE, and all four could conceivably be equal — the only separation known in that chain is L ≠ PSPACE, which follows from the space hierarchy theorem. A polynomial-time algorithm uses at most polynomial space for free, since it cannot touch more cells than it has steps; the converse is exactly what is open.
  • Randomness. BPP is what a polynomial-time algorithm with coin flips decides with error probability at most 1/3 on every input (and 1/3 can be driven to 2^−n by repetition and majority vote). P ⊆ BPP trivially. Randomised primality tests, randomised quicksort's pivot choice in Sorting & Searching, §14, and the MAX-3-SAT coin flip of §24 all live here. The modern expectation is that P = BPP — that randomness buys no asymptotic power for decision problems — on the strength of derandomisation results conditional on circuit lower bounds, and the historical trend supports it: primality was famously the flagship BPP problem until 2002 put it in P.
  • Quantum. BQP is polynomial time on a quantum machine. P ⊆ BQP, and factoring is in BQP by Shor's algorithm while not known to be in P — which is why "quantum computers break RSA" is a true sentence and "quantum computers solve NP-complete problems" is not one. BQP is not believed to contain NP: the best general quantum speed-up for unstructured search is Grover's, which turns 2ⁿ into 2^(n/2) — a square root, not a polynomial — and that is provably optimal for a black-box search. §18 says where BQP is thought to sit.
  • Parallelism. NC is what polylogarithmic time on polynomially many processors decides. NC ⊆ P, and whether the inclusion is strict is the "is everything parallelisable?" question; the P-complete problems, of which circuit evaluation is the canonical one, are the ones believed not to be.

None of these changes anything about this page's subject. An NP-complete problem is not put into P by randomising, by parallelising with polynomially many processors, or (as far as anyone knows) by going quantum; each of those buys a polynomial factor or a square root, and the gap this page is about is exponential.

Where this page uses P. Every "easy" problem named from §16 onward is a member of P by an algorithm built earlier in this series: BFS and DFS reachability, bipartiteness and 2-colouring, topological order and strongly connected components (Graph Representations & Traversal, §11–§23); shortest paths, minimum spanning trees, maximum flow, minimum cut and bipartite matching (Shortest Paths, MST & Flow); sorting, selection and binary search (Sorting & Searching); every dynamic program whose state space is polynomial (Dynamic Programming, §07); 2-SAT and Horn satisfiability (§09, §16). The catalogue of P is exactly the rest of this series, and this page is what is left over.

05 NP: certificates and verification

A problem is in NP when every yes-instance has a short piece of evidence — a certificate — that a polynomial-time algorithm can check; the algorithm never has to find the evidence, only to be convinced by it when handed one, and it must refuse to be convinced by anything when the answer is no. That is the whole definition, and it is strikingly weak: it asks for nothing more than that solutions be recognisable. Nearly every combinatorial problem anyone poses has this property, which is why NP is enormous and why "is it in NP?" is almost never the interesting question. The interesting question is the gap between recognising a solution and producing one, and NP is the class in which that gap is exactly the thing being measured.

the certificate definitionA two-argument algorithm V VERIFIES a language L if L = { x ∈ {0,1}* : ∃ y ∈ {0,1}* with |y| = O(|x|ᶜ) and V(x, y) = 1 } read both halves, because both are needed: COMPLETENESS x ∈ L ⟹ SOME certificate y makes V accept ("the evidence exists") SOUNDNESS x ∉ L ⟹ NO certificate y makes V accept ("the evidence cannot be forged") NP = { L : some V verifies L in time polynomial in |x| } the length bound on y is not decoration: without it, "guess the whole run of an exponential search" would be a certificate and NP would swallow EXPTIME. Since V runs in poly(|x|) time it cannot read more than poly(|x|) bits of y anyway, so |y| = O(|x|ᶜ) is the natural bound and is equivalent to imposing none at all beyond V's own running time. the equivalent nondeterministic definition — and where the letters come fromNP = the languages decided in polynomial time by a NONDETERMINISTIC machine: one allowed to branch, accepting x iff SOME branch of its (exponentially large) computation tree accepts within poly(|x|) steps. the two definitions are the same class, and the translation is mechanical: certificate → nondeterminism the machine nondeterministically writes y bit by bit, then runs V nondeterminism → certificate the certificate is the list of branch choices; V replays them deterministically NP abbreviates NONDETERMINISTIC POLYNOMIAL. It does NOT abbreviate "non-polynomial"; P ⊆ NP, so NP contains every problem with a fast algorithm. "Guess and check" is the right mental model; "not solvable quickly" is not.

Three verifiers, written out

The definition is abstract; the verifiers are not. Each of the following is five lines of code, and each is exercised, counted and cross-checked in the figure.

problem (decision form)certificatewhat the verifier doesits costexhaustive search instead
3-SAT — is the CNF formula φ satisfiable?one truth assignment, n bitsfor each clause, scan its literals until one is true; reject if a clause has noneO(L), L = total literals — at most 9 literal tests on φ2ⁿ assignments — 8 here, 2¹⁰⁰ at n = 100
CLIQUE — has G a clique of size k?the vertex set, k namescheck |S| = k, then look up all C(k,2) pairs in the adjacency matrixO(k²) — 3 lookups for k = 3C(n,k) subsets — 35 triples on 7 vertices
SUBSET-SUM — does some subset of S total T?the subsetadd the listed elements; compare with TO(|S'|) additions — 2 for {14, 23}2^|S| subsets — 64 here
HAM-CYCLE — has G a Hamiltonian cycle?the cyclic vertex ordercheck it is a permutation of V and every consecutive pair is an edgeO(n) after an O(n) permutation check(n−1)!/2 tours (§13)
COMPOSITE — is N composite?a non-trivial factorone division, and two range checksO(b²) on b-bit numbers√N = 2^(b/2) trial divisions (§02)

Notice what the verifier is not doing in any row. It is not searching. It is not clever. It performs the same work a referee does reading a proof: local, mechanical, linear in the size of what it is shown. The exponential in each right-hand column is the cost of playing the role of prover instead, and NP is the class of problems where that asymmetry is all there is.

Soundness is the half that is usually forgotten

It is easy to write a "verifier" that accepts the right certificates and misses the fact that it also accepts wrong ones. A verifier for CLIQUE that checks only that |S| = k and that some pair in S is an edge satisfies completeness — every real clique passes — and fails soundness, since a non-clique passes too, and the language it verifies is not CLIQUE but something much larger. The two conditions must be checked separately, and the second is a statement about all strings y, not about the intended one. Put the other way round: a verifier defines a language, namely {x : ∃y V(x,y) = 1}, and the obligation is to show that this language is the one you meant. Every membership proof "X ∈ NP" on this page is that two-line argument, and §17's co-NP is precisely the observation that for some problems the no-answers are the ones with short evidence and the yes-answers are not.

P ⊆ NP, and what would have to be true for the inclusion to be strict

If L ∈ P then L ∈ NP: take the verifier that ignores its certificate and runs the decider on x. Completeness holds because the decider accepts every x ∈ L whatever y is (the empty string will do); soundness holds because it accepts no x ∉ L whatever y is. So P ⊆ NP, with a one-line proof and no hidden content. The reverse inclusion is the open problem. To prove P ≠ NP one must exhibit a problem in NP and prove that no polynomial algorithm whatsoever decides it — a statement quantified over all possible algorithms, which is the kind of statement complexity theory is worst at proving. Sixty years have produced exactly one class of such results for natural problems, the hierarchy theorems (§18), which separate classes only when one is given strictly more of the same resource; §07 collects the three barriers that explain why nothing else has worked. Sorting & Searching, §08 contains the only unconditional lower bound in this series, Ω(n log n) comparisons, and it is instructive about why: it holds in a restricted model where the algorithm may only compare, and counting leaves of a decision tree bounds its depth. No such restriction is available for SAT, where the algorithm may do anything at all.

The certificate length bound, and why it costs nothing

The definition requires |y| = O(|x|ᶜ), and it is worth seeing that this is not an extra assumption but a consequence of the time bound. A verifier running in p(|x|) steps cannot read more than p(|x|) bits of its certificate, so any certificate longer than that has a prefix that works just as well; truncating loses nothing. Dropping the bound entirely would be a different and much larger class — a certificate allowed to be exponentially long could simply be the transcript of an exponential-time computation, and "NP" would swallow EXPTIME. Keeping it gives the class its other important property for free: NP ⊆ EXPTIME. Enumerate all 2^O(nᶜ) candidate certificates, run the polynomial verifier on each, and accept if any accepts; that is 2^O(nᶜ) · poly(n) = 2^O(nᶜ) time, so every NP problem is solvable in exponential time by brute force, and the entire content of this page is whether anything better is possible. §26 is about making that brute force as fast as brute force can be made.

A certificate can also be surprisingly clever, and the classic case is worth knowing because it is the one place where "obviously in NP" is wrong. COMPOSITE — is N composite? — is in NP by an obvious certificate, a non-trivial factor. PRIMES — is N prime? — has no obvious certificate at all: the natural evidence for primality is the absence of a factor, which is a universal statement and therefore a co-NP-flavoured one. It was not obvious that PRIMES ∈ NP until Pratt exhibited, in 1975, a recursive certificate of primality: a generator of the multiplicative group modulo N together with the factorisation of N − 1 and, recursively, primality certificates for each of those factors, all checkable in polynomial time. So PRIMES sits in NP ∩ co-NP — which was already strong evidence that it was not NP-complete, since an NP-complete problem in co-NP would force NP = co-NP (§17) — and in 2002 it was shown to be in P outright. Factoring's decision version occupies the same NP ∩ co-NP position today, and is the standard example of a problem widely believed to be neither in P nor NP-complete (§07).

What NP is closed under

NP is closed under union and intersection (run both verifiers on a pair of certificates), under concatenation and Kleene star (the certificate names the split points as well), and under polynomial-time many-one reductions in the sense that A ≤ₚ B and B ∈ NP give A ∈ NP. It is not known to be closed under complement — that is precisely the NP versus co-NP question of §17 — and this single missing closure is what separates NP from P, which is closed under complement trivially. It is the reason the definition is asymmetric in the first place: a certificate is evidence for a yes, and nothing in the definition offers evidence for a no.

A verifier at work: checking a certificate step by step, against the cost of finding one by exhaustive search the left panel steps through the verifier's checks on the offered certificate; the right bar compares the measured verifier cost with the measured cost of the exhaustive search that would have produced it. Flip to a forged certificate to watch soundness reject it

What the certificate view buys later on

  • It makes NP-hardness provable at all. The Cook–Levin theorem (§08) reduces an arbitrary language in NP to circuit satisfiability by taking the verifier V, which the certificate definition guarantees exists, and compiling its computation into a circuit whose free inputs are the certificate's bits. Without a uniform object to compile — a polynomial-time machine, given by the definition — there would be nothing to reduce from.
  • It explains the shape of every gadget. Each reduction in §11–§15 arranges its output instance so that its solutions are in correspondence with the certificates of the input instance: a satisfying assignment becomes a clique, becomes a cover, becomes a subset, becomes a colouring. Reductions map certificates to certificates, and checking a reduction is checking that correspondence in both directions.
  • It draws the line at co-NP. "This formula is satisfiable" has a one-line certificate; "this formula is unsatisfiable" appears not to, and no short certificate for unsatisfiability is known. That asymmetry is not an artefact of how the question is phrased — it is the difference between NP and co-NP (§17), and whether the two classes coincide is open and is implied by, but not known to imply, P = NP.
  • It is the definition the approximation lower bounds are stated against. The PCP theorem, which §20 and §24 use to state the inapproximability results, is a re-characterisation of NP: every NP language has a proof format such that a verifier reading a constant number of randomly chosen bits of the certificate accepts correct proofs always and incorrect proofs with probability at most ½. It is a statement about certificates from beginning to end.
The misreading to avoid. "NP" names a class that contains P. A problem being in NP says nothing about it being hard — sorting is in NP, shortest paths are in NP, every problem in this series is in NP. The hardness claims on this page are always of the form "NP-hard" or "NP-complete", never "in NP". And a problem in NP is guaranteed only that the yes-answers are short-certifiable; a problem whose no-answers are short-certifiable is in co-NP, and the two need not coincide (§17).

06 Polynomial-time reductions: the tool that transfers hardness

A polynomial-time reduction from A to B is a single function f, computable in polynomial time, that turns every instance of A into an instance of B with the same answer — yes to yes and no to no — and its whole purpose is to move a fact about B onto A, or a fact about A onto B, depending on which way the fact points. Written A ≤ₚ B and read "A is no harder than B", it is the only technique this page uses to prove anything hard, and it is the technique that most first attempts get backwards. The arrow points from the problem you already know about to the problem you are asking about, and which of those is "already known" depends entirely on whether you are proving hardness or building an algorithm.

the definition, with both obligations namedA ≤ₚ B iff ∃ f : {0,1}* → {0,1}* such that (i) f is computable by a polynomial-time algorithm — the REDUCTION ALGORITHM (ii) for EVERY x: x ∈ A ⟺ f(x) ∈ B — the CORRECTNESS CONDITION (ii) is two implications and BOTH must be proved: x ∈ A ⟹ f(x) ∈ B "a solution of x becomes a solution of f(x)" — the easy half, usually a construction f(x) ∈ B ⟹ x ∈ A "a solution of f(x) comes from a solution of x" — the half that fails, and must be checked equivalently x ∉ A ⟹ f(x) ∉ B: the map must not manufacture yes-instances out of no-instances. a free consequence of (i): |f(x)| = O(|x|ᶜ) — an algorithm cannot write more output than it has steps. this is also the correct notion of "the reduction blows the instance up by a polynomial", which every gadget in §11–§15 does. the two uses, which are the SAME theorem read in opposite directionsTO SOLVE A NEW PROBLEM you have A; B is solved by a library routine; build f; now A is solved. B ∈ P ⟹ A ∈ P. e.g. bipartite matching ≤ₚ max flow (Shortest Paths, MST & Flow §25) Here B is the KNOWN-EASY problem and A is yours. TO PROVE A PROBLEM HARD A is known NP-hard; B is yours; build f : A → B; now B is NP-hard. A NP-hard and A ≤ₚ B ⟹ B NP-hard. e.g. 3-SAT ≤ₚ CLIQUE (§11) Here A is the KNOWN-HARD problem and B is yours. ← THE ARROW STILL POINTS THE SAME WAY. TRANSITIVITY A ≤ₚ B and B ≤ₚ C ⟹ A ≤ₚ C: the composite map g ∘ f is polynomial-time (§04's composition closure) and x ∈ A ⟺ f(x) ∈ B ⟺ g(f(x)) ∈ C. This is what lets the catalogue grow from one root.

The direction trap, stated as loudly as it deserves

To prove that your problem B is hard, you must reduce a known-hard problem A to B. You take an arbitrary instance of 3-SAT and build from it an instance of your problem. You do not take an instance of your problem and build a formula from it; that proves B ≤ₚ 3-SAT, which says your problem is no harder than 3-SAT — true of every problem in NP and therefore worth nothing as a hardness proof. The mnemonic that survives contact with a whiteboard: the hard problem is the input, your problem is the output. Your reduction algorithm must be able to handle any 3-SAT formula, however large and however structured, and must emit an instance of your problem; it may not choose which formulas to accept.

Two further traps ride along with the first. The no-direction: having built a construction that turns satisfying assignments into cliques, it is tempting to declare victory, but the theorem also requires that a clique in the constructed graph can only have come from a satisfying assignment. Most broken reductions in the wild are broken exactly there — the gadget admits a spurious solution that corresponds to nothing. The size trap: a map may be perfectly correct and still not be a reduction, if computing it takes exponential time or produces an exponentially large output. Converting a CNF formula to an equivalent DNF formula is a correct transformation and not a polynomial reduction, because the output can be exponentially larger (§09); Tseitin's trick in §08 exists precisely to avoid that blow-up at the price of new variables.

A reduction in full, on every instance of a small family

The cleanest possible example, and the one the figure runs: INDEPENDENT-SET ≤ₚ CLIQUE. An independent set in G is a vertex set with no edge inside it; a clique is a vertex set with every edge inside it. Those are the same condition in complementary graphs, so define f(⟨G, k⟩) = ⟨Ḡ, k⟩, where Ḡ has the same vertices and exactly the non-edges of G. Computing Ḡ takes Θ(n²) — read every pair once — so f is polynomial-time. Correctness, both halves at once: a set S has no edge of G inside it precisely when every pair inside it is a non-edge of G, that is, an edge of Ḡ; so S is independent in G ⟺ S is a clique in Ḡ. Taking |S| ≥ k on both sides gives the ⟺ the definition demands, and because the equivalence is about the same set S, the reduction also converts a witness into a witness with no work at all.

The figure enumerates the entire family of graphs on four vertices — 2⁶ = 64 of them, one per subset of the six possible edges — decides "has an independent set of size 3" on each by brute force, decides "has a clique of size 3" on each image by brute force, and shades the two columns accordingly. 23 of the 64 graphs are yes-instances of the independent-set question and 41 are no-instances, and the map agrees on all 64: every yes maps to a yes and every no to a no. Switching the control to the bogus map f(G) = G — a map that is polynomial-time and utterly wrong — makes the picture disagree on 46 of the 64 instances, the very first of them being the empty graph, which has an independent set of size 3 (any three vertices) and no clique of size 3 at all. That is what a failed reduction looks like: not a subtle inefficiency, but pairs of instances whose answers disagree, and finding one such pair is how a reduction is refuted.

The mapping picture, drawn over a real reduction: all 64 graphs on four vertices, mapped and decided on both sides left column = instances of INDEPENDENT-SET (k = 3), right column = their images under the chosen map, decided as instances of CLIQUE (k = 3); green = yes, grey = no, and a red link marks an instance whose answer the map fails to preserve. Both sides are decided by exhaustive search, and the readout counts the disagreements
0

Many-one reductions against Turing reductions

The reduction defined above is a many-one or Karp reduction: one call, at the end, with no post-processing — the answer to f(x) is the answer to x. The more permissive notion is a Turing or Cook reduction: A ≤ᵀ B when a polynomial-time algorithm decides A given free access to an oracle for B, calling it as often as it likes and doing what it likes with the answers. Every many-one reduction is a Turing reduction. The converse fails in an important way, and the reason the theory standardises on many-one is exactly that failure: a Turing reduction may negate the oracle's answer, so L ≤ᵀ complement of L always holds, and under Turing reductions NP and co-NP would become indistinguishable, collapsing the distinction §17 is built on. Cook's original 1971 paper used Turing reductions; Karp's 1972 paper introduced the many-one restriction, and it is Karp's version that the word "reduction" means on this page unless stated otherwise.

Turing reductions are still the right tool in two places, and both appear later. The optimisation-to-decision binary search of §03 is a Turing reduction — it calls the oracle O(log V) times and post-processes — and so is the self-reduction that recovers a witness. And the statement "TSP-OPT is NP-hard" is a statement about Turing reducibility, since an optimisation problem cannot be the target of a many-one reduction at all: what a many-one reduction maps to is a yes/no question. So the precise version of the definition in §07 is: X is NP-hard if every L ∈ NP satisfies L ≤ₚ X for decision X, and L ≤ᵀ X in general. This page uses many-one whenever the target is a decision problem, which is everywhere except the optimisation statements.

What a reduction preserves, and what it destroys

A polynomial-time reduction preserves membership in P, membership in NP, and NP-hardness. It does not preserve running time beyond a polynomial — a reduction that squares the instance size turns an O(m²) algorithm for B into an O(n⁴) algorithm for A, which is fine for the theory and can be fatal in practice; this is why reducing your problem to SAT and calling a solver (§30) is an engineering decision with a real cost, not a free lunch. It does not preserve approximability either, which is the reason §20 needs its own machinery: a reduction that maps optimal solutions to optimal solutions may map near-optimal ones to arbitrarily bad ones, so a 2-approximation for B gives nothing for A unless the reduction was built to be approximation-preserving. And it does not preserve the parameter that §27's fixed-parameter algorithms exploit — the standard reductions blow the parameter up polynomially, which is exactly why parameterised complexity needs its own notion of reduction.

Reductions already used in this series, in the other direction

Nearly every algorithm in the earlier parts of this series was obtained by a reduction used the easy way — translating a new problem into one with a known polynomial algorithm. Collecting them makes the technique feel less exotic and shows what an approximation- or structure-preserving translation looks like when it works.

problem Areduced to Bthe mapwhere
maximum bipartite matchingmaximum flowadd a source into one side, a sink out of the other, unit capacities everywhereShortest Paths, MST & Flow, §25
a system of difference constraints xⱼ − xᵢ ≤ bsingle-source shortest pathsone vertex per variable, one edge per constraint of weight b, a super-source to all§10
image segmentation, project selectionminimum cutthe cut's two sides are the two labels; capacities encode the penalties§26
longest path in a DAG, critical pathsshortest paths with negated weightsnegate every weight; the DAG's topological order keeps it well defined§09

The same machinery also runs forwards in industrial practice, at a scale the theory did not anticipate. Because 3-SAT and integer programming have solvers that are extraordinarily effective on structured instances (§30), "reduce my problem to SAT and call a solver" is a serious engineering strategy rather than a theoretical curiosity: hardware equivalence checking, bounded model checking, package dependency resolution, timetabling and configuration are all routinely solved this way. Both halves of §06 are then in play at once — the reduction must be correct (or the answers are wrong) and it must be small (or the solver drowns), and the second requirement is a much sharper constraint than "polynomial". A reduction that squares the instance is a proof-theoretic success and an engineering failure.

The checklist for a reduction you have just written. (1) Which problem is the input and which is the output — is the known-hard one the input? (2) Does the construction run in polynomial time, and is the output polynomially bounded? (3) Yes to yes: take an arbitrary solution of the input instance and exhibit the corresponding solution of the output instance. (4) No to no, in its contrapositive form: take an arbitrary solution of the output instance and show it must have come from a solution of the input. (5) Test it on a small family by brute force, both sides, and compare — which is exactly what the figure above does, and what every reduction figure in §11–§15 repeats.

07 NP-hard, NP-complete, and what P = NP would mean

A problem is NP-hard when everything in NP reduces to it — it is at least as hard as the whole class — and NP-complete when it is NP-hard and itself in NP, so that it is simultaneously a hardest member and an ordinary member. The second condition is the one that gets dropped in conversation, and dropping it changes the meaning: NP-hard problems need not be in NP, need not be decision problems, and need not be decidable at all. The NP-complete problems are the ones that sit exactly at the boundary, and because a reduction chain can only move hardness upward, they are all equivalent: any one of them in P puts all of them in P, and all of NP with them.

the two definitions, and the three things that distinguish themX is NP-HARD ⟺ for every L ∈ NP, L ≤ₚ X ("at least as hard as everything in NP") X is NP-COMPLETE ⟺ X is NP-hard AND X ∈ NP ("a hardest problem that is itself in the class") so the class of NP-hard problems is a superset, and it is much bigger: NOT NECESSARILY IN NP TSP-OPT (an optimisation problem: the answer is a number, not a bit) NOT NECESSARILY A DECISION any search or function problem; "output a maximum clique" NOT NECESSARILY DECIDABLE the halting problem is NP-hard — every L ∈ NP many-one reduces to it — and is not PROBLEM AT ALL decidable at all, so it is NP-hard and emphatically NOT NP-complete (§18) the standard sentence pattern this page uses: "TSP-DECISION is NP-complete; TSP-OPT is NP-hard." "CLIQUE (decision) is NP-complete; MAX-CLIQUE is NP-hard." the collapse theorem, and its contrapositiveTHEOREM If any NP-complete problem is in P, then P = NP. PROOF Let X be NP-complete and X ∈ P, decided in O(mᵏ). Take any L ∈ NP. X's NP-hardness gives f with x ∈ L ⟺ f(x) ∈ X, computable in O(nᶜ), hence |f(x)| = O(nᶜ). Decide L by computing f(x) then running X's decider: O(nᶜ) + O((nᶜ)ᵏ) = O(nᶜᵏ). So L ∈ P. Since L was arbitrary, NP ⊆ P, and P ⊆ NP always. ∎ CONTRAPOSITIVE If P ≠ NP then no NP-complete problem is in P — i.e. P ∩ NP-complete = ∅. COROLLARY If any NP-complete problem is NOT in P, then P ≠ NP and NO NP-complete problem is in P. The thousands of problems in the catalogue therefore stand or fall together, as one object.

The two worlds

Exactly one of the following pictures is true, and nobody knows which.

if P ≠ NP (the consensus view)if P = NP
shapeP is a proper subset of NP; the NP-complete problems sit at the top of NP and outside P; a middle region existsP, NP and NP-complete are all the same set of problems; there is no middle and no top
the middle regionnon-empty, by Ladner's theorem (1975): if P ≠ NP there are languages in NP that are neither in P nor NP-complete — NP-intermediate. The theorem's construction is artificial, by diagonalisation, but the conclusion is unconditional given P ≠ NPempty — everything collapses into one class
candidates for the middlegraph isomorphism (no polynomial algorithm known; a quasipolynomial 2^O((log n)^c) algorithm is known since 2015–17; not known NP-complete); integer factoring, whose decision form lies in NP ∩ co-NP, so its NP-completeness would force NP = co-NP (§17); discrete logarithm; some lattice problemsall in P, like everything else
cryptographypossible — but not implied. One-way functions require average-case hardness, which P ≠ NP (a worst-case statement) does not give. P ≠ NP is necessary for public-key cryptography, not sufficientevery computational cryptosystem in use falls, given an efficient enough algorithm; only information-theoretic security survives (one-time pad, secret sharing)
this page§08–§18 are a map of the boundary; §19–§30 are the response§08–§18 remain true as theorems about reductions; §19–§30 become unnecessary
The two worlds, side by side: what the class diagram looks like under each hypothesis switch the hypothesis to watch the regions merge; each placed problem carries a measured cost, computed at load by running the best routine this page has for it on a small instance and counting its steps

What P = NP would and would not mean

The headline is genuine: if P = NP by a practical algorithm, then finding is as easy as checking, everywhere. Every problem whose solutions can be recognised quickly could be solved quickly — optimal schedules, optimal circuit layouts, optimal protein foldings in their combinatorial idealisations, and, most strikingly, proofs: "does this statement have a proof of at most n symbols in this formal system?" is in NP, because a proof is a certificate a proof-checker verifies in linear time, so mathematics itself would be mechanised up to proof length. That last consequence is the strongest informal argument people offer for believing P ≠ NP.

The cryptographic consequence deserves stating exactly, because it is routinely overstated in both directions. Overstatement one: "P = NP would break all cryptography." Almost right, with two qualifications. Information-theoretically secure schemes — the one-time pad, Shamir secret sharing — are unaffected, because their security is a counting argument and not a computational assumption. And the break requires an efficient algorithm: a proof that P = NP by a non-constructive argument, or a constructive one running in n¹⁰⁰ with galactic constants (§04), would leave every deployed system running exactly as before, while demolishing the theoretical foundation under it. With those two caveats, the statement holds and holds broadly: given an efficient, practical algorithm, factoring, discrete logarithm and lattice problems — all with decision versions in NP — would fall, taking RSA, Diffie–Hellman, elliptic-curve cryptography and the post-quantum lattice schemes with them; and recovering a symmetric key from a known plaintext–ciphertext pair is an NP search problem too — the key is the certificate — so AES would fall as well. The second caveat cuts the same way for both kinds of scheme: RSA-2048 and AES-128 are each a single fixed-size instance, so an impractical polynomial, or a proof with no algorithm in it, leaves both exactly as secure in practice as before, while a practical algorithm breaks both. There is no version of P = NP that breaks the public-key schemes and spares the symmetric ones. Overstatement two: "P ≠ NP means cryptography is safe." False, and in a way worth internalising. P ≠ NP says some problem in NP has no polynomial algorithm on its worst-case instances. Cryptography needs problems that are hard on random instances drawn from a distribution you can sample, which is a strictly stronger requirement; one-way functions imply P ≠ NP, but nobody can prove the converse, and it is entirely consistent with current knowledge that P ≠ NP and no one-way functions exist. The average-case theory is a different theory, and the phase-transition results of §30 are the practical face of the same gap: random SAT instances away from the critical clause ratio are easy, and worst-case hardness says nothing about them.

The evidence, and the three barriers

Nobody has proved either side, so the belief that P ≠ NP rests on evidence of three kinds. Failure at scale: thousands of NP-complete problems, attacked independently for fifty years by people with every incentive to succeed, and not one polynomial algorithm.
