Structural coincidence: those thousands of problems arose separately — in logic, scheduling, graph theory, number theory, statistical physics, biology — and turned out to be a single problem in disguise, which is the kind of unexpected unification that usually indicates something real. The absence of a proof is itself explained: three theorems show that broad, natural families of proof technique cannot settle the question, which is why fifty years of effort have produced so little.

  • Relativisation (Baker, Gill and Solovay, 1975). Give every machine access to the same oracle A and ask the question again. There is an oracle A with P^A = NP^A and an oracle B with P^B ≠ NP^B. Any proof technique that still works when both sides are given an arbitrary oracle — which includes simulation and diagonalisation, the techniques behind the hierarchy theorems of §18 — therefore cannot decide P versus NP, because it would have to prove both a statement and its negation.
  • Natural proofs (Razborov and Rudich, 1994). Most known lower-bound arguments for circuits are "natural": they exhibit a property of Boolean functions that is easy to check, is shared by most functions, and is not shared by any function with small circuits. Razborov and Rudich showed that a natural proof of a super-polynomial circuit lower bound would itself break the pseudo-random generators that cryptography is built on — so if strong one-way functions exist, no natural proof can separate the classes.
  • Algebrisation (Aaronson and Wigderson, 2008). Interactive-proof results such as IP = PSPACE do not relativise, and for a while looked like the technique that might break through; algebrisation is the extension of relativisation that captures them, and it too is shown to be insufficient for P versus NP.

Two smaller facts complete the picture. First, an exact consequence of a collapse that is easy to state and easy to misstate: if P = NP, then every language in P except the two trivial ones — the empty language and the set of all strings — is NP-complete. The reason is that with P = NP a reduction from any L' ∈ NP to such an L is trivial: decide x ∈ L' in polynomial time and output a fixed string known to be in L, or a fixed string known not to be, which is possible exactly when L has at least one of each. The two trivial languages are excluded because they have no such pair of witnesses, and they are the reason the statement carries an exception at all. Second, the question carries a Clay Millennium Prize, informal polls of the research community have consistently found a large majority expecting P ≠ NP, and the minority view is not that P = NP is likely but that the question may be independent of the standard axioms — a possibility nobody has been able to rule out either.

Proving NP-completeness in practice: the four-step recipe

Every completeness proof from §09 onward has the same four steps, and it is worth having them in one place.

  • 1. Show X ∈ NP. Name the certificate, name the verifier, argue it runs in polynomial time, and check soundness as well as completeness (§05). This step is usually three lines and is usually the one omitted, which is how "NP-complete" gets attached to problems that are merely NP-hard.
  • 2. Choose the known-hard problem A. Pick the catalogue entry whose structure resembles yours: 3-SAT when your problem has independent binary choices with local constraints; clique or independent set when it is about mutually compatible selections; Hamiltonian cycle when it is about ordering or routing; subset sum or partition when numbers are involved; 3-colouring when it is about conflicting labels. §10's map exists to make this choice fast.
  • 3. Build f and argue it is polynomial. Describe the construction locally — per variable, per clause, per vertex — and count: how many vertices, edges, numbers or clauses does one input symbol generate?
  • 4. Prove x ∈ A ⟺ f(x) ∈ X, both directions. Forward: convert a solution of x into a solution of f(x), explicitly. Backward: take an arbitrary solution of f(x) and show it induces a solution of x. The backward direction is where gadgets earn their keep, because it is where you must rule out solutions the construction did not intend.

And the standard hedge, which this page uses without further comment from here on: results of the form "no polynomial-time algorithm for X" are always stated as "unless P = NP", and results of the form "no (1 + ε)-approximation" as "unless P = NP" too. That is not timidity; it is the exact strength of what is proved. The unconditional statement — that no polynomial algorithm exists — has never been proved for any problem in NP, and proving it for one would prove it for all.

Three sentences to keep straight. "X is in NP" — yes-instances have short checkable certificates; says nothing about hardness. "X is NP-hard" — everything in NP reduces to X; X may be an optimisation problem, may be undecidable, may lie far above NP. "X is NP-complete" — both of the above; X is a decision problem in NP and a hardest one. When a source says "the travelling salesman problem is NP-complete" it means the decision version; when it says "TSP is NP-hard" it means either version, and only the second phrasing is safe without further qualification.

08 Circuit satisfiability and the Cook–Levin theorem

Reductions can only move hardness from one problem to another, so the catalogue needs a root: one problem proved hard directly, from the definition of NP rather than from another problem — and the proof is that a polynomial-time verifier is a machine, its computation on an input of size n is a rectangular table of poly(n) cells, and the question "is there a certificate that makes this table end in acceptance?" is a Boolean satisfiability question about the cells. That is the Cook–Levin theorem, proved independently by Cook in 1971 and Levin in 1973, and it is the only theorem on this page whose proof reaches all the way down to the machine. Everything else is a gadget.

the two problems this section proves completeCIRCUIT-SAT = { ⟨C⟩ : C is a Boolean combinational circuit with one output, and some assignment to its inputs makes it output 1 } a circuit is a DAG of AND / OR / NOT gates over input wires; size = number of gates + wires; no cycles, no state SAT = { ⟨φ⟩ : φ is a Boolean formula and some assignment of its variables makes it TRUE } COOK–LEVIN SAT is NP-complete. (and, by the same argument, CIRCUIT-SAT is NP-complete) the route taken here: (a) CIRCUIT-SAT ∈ NP (b) every L ∈ NP ≤ₚ CIRCUIT-SAT (c) CIRCUIT-SAT ≤ₚ SAT (d) SAT ≤ₚ 3-SAT (§09) and from 3-SAT the whole catalogue follows by gadgets (§10–§15) step (a): CIRCUIT-SAT ∈ NP — the easy halfcertificate the value of every WIRE in C (or, more economically, just the input wires) verifier for each gate in topological order, check that the certified output value is what the gate computes from its certified input values; accept iff every gate checks out and the output wire is 1 cost O(size of C) — one constant-time check per gate. COMPLETENESS: a satisfying input gives consistent wire values. SOUNDNESS: on an unsatisfiable C no wire assignment can be both gate-consistent and output 1, because gate-consistency forces the wire values to be the ones the circuit actually computes.

Step (b): the tableau, in full

Let L ∈ NP. By the certificate definition (§05) there is a verifier V and constants such that x ∈ L iff some certificate y with |y| = O(nᵏ) makes V(x, y) accept within T(n) = O(nᵏ) steps. Model V as a single-tape machine — the model costs at most a polynomial (§04) — with state set Q, tape alphabet Γ, and a transition function δ. The reduction must produce, from x alone and in polynomial time, an object that is satisfiable exactly when such a y exists.

The object is the tableau: a table whose i-th row is the machine's configuration after i steps, written as the tape contents with the current state symbol inserted immediately to the left of the head's cell, and a delimiter # at each end. Two facts bound its size. The machine runs for at most T(n) steps, so the start row plus one row per step — T(n) + 1 rows — suffice; and in T(n) steps the head can visit at most T(n) + 1 tape cells, so those cells plus the inserted state symbol and the two delimiters — T(n) + 4 columns — suffice. The table is therefore (T(n) + 1) × (T(n) + 4), which is O(T(n)²) cells; textbooks usually tidy this to a square T × T by assuming the machine halts within T(n) − 3 steps, and nothing below depends on the exact shape. Every cell holds one symbol from the finite set C = Q ∪ Γ ∪ {#}, whose size is a constant depending on the machine and not on n.

the variablesfor each row i, column j, and symbol s ∈ C: a Boolean variable x[i, j, s] with intended meaning "cell (i, j) contains s" count: |C| · T(n)² = O(n^2k) variables — POLYNOMIAL in n, which is the whole reason this works. the four clause families — φ = φcell ∧ φstart ∧ φaccept ∧ φmoveφcell "every cell holds exactly one symbol" ⋀ᵢⱼ [ ( ⋁ₛ∈C x[i,j,s] ) ∧ ⋀ₛ≠ₜ ( ¬x[i,j,s] ∨ ¬x[i,j,t] ) ] at least one, and no two size: T² clauses of width |C|, plus T²·C(|C|,2) clauses of width 2 = O(n^2k) φstart "row 0 is the start configuration on input x, with the certificate cells FREE" x[0,0,#] ∧ x[0,1,q₀] ∧ x[0,2,x₁] ∧ … ∧ x[0,n+1,xₙ] ∧ ( nothing at all about the y cells ) ∧ x[0,·,␣] … ∧ x[0,T−1,#] size: one unit clause per FIXED cell = O(n^k). Leaving the y cells unconstrained is exactly how "∃ certificate" becomes "∃ satisfying assignment" — the y cells ARE the free choice. φaccept "the machine accepts somewhere in the tableau" ⋁ᵢⱼ x[i, j, q_accept] one clause of width O(n^2k) φmove "every row follows legally from the row above it" stated LOCALLY, as a condition on every 2 × 3 window of adjacent cells: ⋀ over window positions (i, j): "the window at (i, j) is legal" a window is LEGAL iff it can occur in some valid pair of consecutive configurations. Two equivalent CNF renderings: (1) ⋁ over legal 6-tuples (a₁…a₆) of ( x[i,j−1,a₁] ∧ … ∧ x[i+1,j+1,a₆] ) — a DNF per window, needs distributing (2) ⋀ over ILLEGAL 6-tuples of ( ¬x[i,j−1,a₁] ∨ … ∨ ¬x[i+1,j+1,a₆] ) — already CNF, 6 literals per clause either way the number of clauses per window is a CONSTANT (at most |C|⁶) depending only on the machine, and there are (T−1)(T−2) windows, so φmove has O(n^2k) clauses.

Why locality is enough — the one claim that carries the proof

The whole construction turns on a claim that looks too strong: if row 0 is the start configuration and every 2 × 3 window is legal, then every row is the configuration that legally follows the row above it. The reason it holds is that a machine step is itself local. Away from the head, a cell's symbol is simply copied down, and a window entirely away from the head sees three symbols above and the same three below — legal. At the head, the transition function determines what the three cells around the head become, and any 2 × 3 window that straddles the head position sees enough of the neighbourhood to certify that what happened is what δ prescribes. Since a configuration row contains exactly one state symbol — which φcell and φstart together force, and which the legal windows preserve — there is exactly one place in each row where anything non-trivial can happen, and the windows covering it pin it down. The 3 in "2 × 3" is chosen to be wide enough to see a head moving one cell in either direction; a 2 × 2 window is too narrow to distinguish some illegal head motions.

The figure builds this for a real, if tiny, machine: a verifier that sweeps right along a tape holding a 2-bit input x followed by a 2-bit certificate y, accepting as soon as it reads a 1. On x = 00 the accepting certificates are exactly those containing a 1, so the constructed formula ought to be satisfiable — and the tableau for y = 10 reaches the accepting state in three steps while the tableau for y = 00 never does. The counts the figure measures for this machine: |C| = 7 symbols and a tableau of 6 rows by 9 columns — not square, because the illustration fixes a six-cell tape and six rows, and the two # delimiters plus the inserted state symbol add the same three columns the general count above carries, 378 cell variables, 1 188 clauses in φcell, 35 window positions, and — by enumerating all 13 122 configurations of the machine over a six-cell tape, stepping each one, and collecting the windows that actually appear — exactly 249 legal windows out of 7⁶ = 117 649 possible ones, hence 117 400 illegal ones and 35 × 117 400 = 4 109 000 clauses in φmove under rendering (2). Four million clauses for a six-cell tape is a startling number, and it is the honest shape of the theorem: the per-window cost is a constant — it depends only on |C|, that is, only on the machine — while the number of windows is polynomial in n. Polynomial-size, enormous constant. No one ever runs this construction; it exists to be a theorem.

Correctness of the reduction, and why it is polynomial

Forward. If x ∈ L, take an accepting certificate y, run V(x, y), and read the tableau off the run; setting x[i,j,s] = TRUE exactly for the symbol actually in each cell satisfies all four families by construction. Backward. If φ is satisfiable, take a satisfying assignment. φcell makes it a well-defined table of symbols; φstart makes row 0 the start configuration on x, with some bits in the certificate cells — call them y; φmove plus the locality claim makes every subsequent row the genuine next configuration, so the table is the actual run of V(x, y); and φaccept says that run accepts. Hence a certificate exists and x ∈ L. Polynomial time. The reduction algorithm never simulates anything: it emits O(n^2k) variables and O(n^2k) clauses by a mechanical loop over (i, j), using only n, the constants of V, and the bits of x. Writing that output is the entire cost.

Step (c): a circuit into CNF, without the exponential blow-up

Compiling a circuit into a formula naively — substitute each gate's definition into its consumers, recursively — duplicates a shared sub-circuit once per consumer, and a chain of k fan-out-2 gates produces a formula of size 2^k. That is a correct transformation and not a polynomial reduction (§06's size trap). Tseitin's encoding avoids it by refusing to eliminate anything: introduce a fresh variable for every gate's output wire and write down, for each gate, the constant number of clauses asserting that the new variable equals the gate's function of its inputs. Nothing is duplicated, so the output is linear in the circuit size.

the Tseitin clauses, gate by gatez = a ∧ b (¬z ∨ a) ∧ (¬z ∨ b) ∧ (z ∨ ¬a ∨ ¬b) 3 clauses [z→a, z→b, (a∧b)→z] z = a ∨ b (z ∨ ¬a) ∧ (z ∨ ¬b) ∧ (¬z ∨ a ∨ b) 3 clauses z = ¬a (z ∨ a) ∧ (¬z ∨ ¬a) 2 clauses output (z_out) 1 unit clause — "the circuit outputs 1" SIZE one new variable per gate; ≤ 3 clauses per gate + 1; every clause has ≤ 3 literals — so the output is ALREADY in 3-CNF, and §09's splitting step is not even needed for circuits. EQUISATISFIABLE, not equivalent: the CNF has MORE variables, so it is a different Boolean function on a different space. What is preserved is the projection: the satisfying assignments of the CNF, restricted to the circuit's input wires, are EXACTLY the circuit's satisfying inputs — and each extends in exactly ONE way, because the gate variables are forced bottom-up. Hence the two have the same NUMBER of solutions too.

The figure runs this on a five-gate circuit over three inputs: g₁ = x₁ ∧ x₂, g₂ = x₂ ∨ x₃, g₃ = ¬x₁, g₄ = g₁ ∨ g₃, g₅ = g₄ ∧ g₂, with g₅ the output. Evaluated on all eight inputs it is satisfied by exactly 5 of them — 001, 010, 011, 110, 111 in the order x₁x₂x₃ — and the Tseitin encoding has 8 variables and 15 clauses (3 + 3 + 2 + 3 + 3 for the gates, plus the unit clause). Brute-forcing the CNF over all 2⁸ = 256 assignments finds exactly 5 satisfying ones, and their projections onto x₁x₂x₃ are exactly the circuit's five satisfying inputs: five solutions upstairs, five downstairs, the unique-extension property visible as an equality of counts rather than merely of emptiness. The figure recomputes all of this at load and prints the comparison with an agreement marker.

Circuit to CNF, and machine to tableau: the two compilations the Cook–Levin proof is made of in Tseitin mode, every gate contributes its clauses and both the circuit and the formula are evaluated on every assignment, with the projection compared; in tableau mode, a tiny verifier is run on a chosen certificate and its computation is drawn as the grid of cells the proof turns into variables, with one 2 × 3 window highlighted and the clause counts measured

What the theorem actually established, historically and logically

  • It created the field. Before 1971 there was no way to say "this problem is hard" beyond "nobody has solved it". Cook's paper — and Levin's, written independently and published in 1973 in a different research tradition — gave the notion of completeness and the first complete problem. Karp's 1972 paper then listed twenty-one NP-complete problems — satisfiability and twenty more reached from it by reduction — and by doing so demonstrated that the phenomenon was not a curiosity of logic but a property of combinatorics, graph theory, scheduling and number theory alike.
  • It is the only proof on this page that mentions a machine. Every subsequent hardness proof reduces from a problem already known complete, so the machine model never reappears. That is the division of labour the theorem buys: one proof pays the cost of talking about computation in general, and every later proof talks only about gadgets.
  • It is robust to the machine model. Single-tape, multi-tape, random-access: each is simulated by the others with polynomial overhead (§04), and the tableau construction goes through for each with a different, still constant, window size. Nothing in the theorem depends on the choice.
  • Circuits are the more natural root. The tableau construction is really building a circuit — one copy of the "one step of the machine" circuit per time step, wired in series, with the certificate bits left as free inputs. Stating the root as CIRCUIT-SAT makes that explicit and makes step (c) a separate, clean, linear-size compilation. It also connects directly to circuit complexity, where the hope of proving P ≠ NP by exhibiting a problem needing super-polynomial circuits has been pursued (and blocked by the natural-proofs barrier) for forty years.
  • Practical descendants. Tseitin's encoding is not a museum piece: it is how every modern toolchain hands a hardware or software verification query to a SAT solver (§30). Bounded model checking unrolls a transition relation for k steps and Tseitin-encodes the result — which is the Cook–Levin tableau, built deliberately, and then solved.
The one-sentence version. A polynomial-time verifier is a finite machine whose run on a fixed input is a polynomial-size grid of symbols; the grid is legal exactly when every little window in it is legal; "legal window" is a finite condition; so "does a certificate exist?" is "is this polynomial-size Boolean formula satisfiable?" — and therefore satisfiability is at least as hard as everything in NP.

09 SAT, CNF-SAT and 3-SAT

3-SAT is satisfiability restricted to formulas that are a conjunction of clauses of at most three literals each, and it is NP-complete — the restriction throws away none of the difficulty, because any CNF formula can be rewritten, in linear time, as a 3-CNF formula that is satisfiable exactly when the original is. That rewriting is one gadget, clause by clause, and it is the first gadget on the page: long clauses are cut into a chain with fresh variables acting as carry bits, short clauses are padded with fresh variables that cancel out. The payoff is that every reduction from §11 onward starts from a formula with a fixed, tiny clause shape, which is what makes the gadgets drawable.

vocabulary, fixed onceLITERAL a variable or its negation: x or ¬x CLAUSE a disjunction of literals: (x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ x₅) — its WIDTH is the number of literals CNF a conjunction of clauses: C₁ ∧ C₂ ∧ … ∧ C_m — "conjunctive normal form", an AND of ORs k-CNF every clause has width ≤ k (some texts say EXACTLY k; padding converts between them, below) DNF an OR of ANDs: (x₁ ∧ ¬x₂) ∨ (x₃ ∧ x₄) — the dual form, and a completely different problem SAT is a given Boolean formula satisfiable? NP-complete (§08) CNF-SAT is a given CNF formula satisfiable? NP-complete 3-SAT is a given CNF formula of clause width ≤ 3 satisfiable? NP-COMPLETE — this section 2-SAT width ≤ 2 IN P — O(n + m) by the implication graph (§16) DNF-SAT is a given DNF formula satisfiable? IN P — O(size), below HORN-SAT ≤ 1 positive literal per clause IN P — unit propagation (Greedy Algorithms, §24) the chain of reductions, each link with its sizeL ∈ NP ≤ₚ CIRCUIT-SAT ≤ₚ CNF-SAT ≤ₚ 3-SAT §08 tableau §08 Tseitin this section: clause splitting O(n^2k) size linear, ≤3-wide linear: n' = n + O(L), m' ≤ 4m + L for total literal count L 3-SAT ∈ NP: the assignment is the certificate; the verifier scans every clause once, O(L) (§05). 3-SAT NP-hard: by the chain. Hence 3-SAT is NP-COMPLETE.

The gadget: cutting a long clause into a chain

Take a clause C = (ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂ ∨ … ∨ ℓₖ) with k ≥ 4. Introduce k − 3 fresh variables y₁, …, y_{k−3}, used nowhere else in the formula, and replace C by the chain

the splitting gadget, for k ≥ 4(ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂ ∨ y₁) ∧ (¬y₁ ∨ ℓ₃ ∨ y₂) ∧ (¬y₂ ∨ ℓ₄ ∨ y₃) ∧ … ∧ (¬y_{k−4} ∨ ℓ_{k−2} ∨ y_{k−3}) ∧ (¬y_{k−3} ∨ ℓ_{k−1} ∨ ℓ_k) k − 2 clauses, k − 3 fresh variables, every clause of width exactly 3 and for the short clauses, padding with fresh variables that cancel: width 2 (ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂) → (ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂ ∨ p) ∧ (ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂ ∨ ¬p) 2 clauses, 1 fresh var width 1 (ℓ₁) → (ℓ₁ ∨ p ∨ q) ∧ (ℓ₁ ∨ p ∨ ¬q) ∧ (ℓ₁ ∨ ¬p ∨ q) ∧ (ℓ₁ ∨ ¬p ∨ ¬q) 4 clauses, 2 fresh vars width 3 unchanged 1 clause, 0 fresh vars

Why it preserves satisfiability, forward. Suppose an assignment satisfies C, so some ℓᵢ is true. Set the carries to y₁ = … = y_{i−2} = TRUE and the rest FALSE. Every clause before the one containing ℓᵢ is satisfied by its positive carry y_j; the clause containing ℓᵢ is satisfied by ℓᵢ itself; every clause after it is satisfied by ¬y_{j−1}, whose carry is false. (The two boundary cases work out the same way: if i ∈ {1, 2} set every y false, and if i ∈ {k−1, k} set every y true.) Why it preserves satisfiability, backward — the half that matters. Suppose every ℓᵢ is false and ask whether any setting of the carries can satisfy the chain. The first clause becomes (F ∨ F ∨ y₁), forcing y₁ = TRUE. The second becomes (¬y₁ ∨ F ∨ y₂) = (F ∨ F ∨ y₂), forcing y₂ = TRUE. Inductively every carry is forced true, until the last clause becomes (¬y_{k−3} ∨ F ∨ F) = (F ∨ F ∨ F) — unsatisfiable. So the chain is satisfiable only when some original literal is true. The fresh variables can absorb a true literal somewhere but cannot manufacture one; that asymmetry is the whole gadget, and it is exactly the "no maps to no" obligation of §06.

The padding gadgets are the same argument in miniature. (ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂ ∨ p) ∧ (ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂ ∨ ¬p): whichever value p takes, one of the two clauses loses its third literal and demands ℓ₁ ∨ ℓ₂. The width-1 case uses all four combinations of two fresh variables, so no choice of p, q escapes a clause in which both are false. And the new variables are local — a distinct pair per clause — so the gadgets cannot interfere with one another, which is what lets the argument be made clause by clause and then conjoined.

Equisatisfiable is not equivalent, and the size accounting

The output formula is not logically equivalent to the input: it has more variables, so it is a function on a bigger space, and it is not even true that every satisfying assignment of the original extends to the new one uniquely — the carries have slack, and a long clause with several true literals has several valid carry settings. What is preserved is exactly what §06 requires: satisfiable ⟺ satisfiable, and more usefully, the set of assignments to the original variables that satisfy the original formula equals the set of restrictions to those variables of the assignments satisfying the new one. The figure checks that second, stronger statement by brute force on both formulas.

Sizes, for a CNF with n variables, m clauses and L total literals: a clause of width k ≥ 4 contributes k − 2 clauses and k − 3 variables, width 3 contributes 1 and 0, width 2 contributes 2 and 1, width 1 contributes 4 and 2. Summing, the output has at most 4m + L clauses and n + 2m + L variables — linear in the input either way, so the map is computable in linear time and is a legitimate polynomial-time reduction. The figure's worked instance has 5 variables and 5 clauses of widths 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 (total 15 literals) and comes out as 11 variables and 12 clauses, the per-clause contributions being 1 → 4, 2 → 2, 3 → 1, 4 → 2 and 5 → 3. Brute force over the original's 2⁵ = 32 assignments finds 10 satisfying; brute force over the 3-CNF's 2¹¹ = 2 048 assignments and projecting onto the first five variables yields the same 10, element for element.

CNF to 3-CNF: each clause split or padded, with both formulas brute-forced and their solution sets compared the chosen clause is expanded on the right with its fresh variables named; the grid below is every assignment to the original variables, green where it satisfies. The two grids — original, and the projection of the 3-CNF's solutions — must be identical, and the readout says so or does not

Why DNF-SAT being easy is not a contradiction

A DNF formula is a disjunction of terms, each term a conjunction of literals. It is satisfiable iff some term is satisfiable, and a single conjunction of literals is satisfiable iff it contains no variable together with its negation. So DNF-SAT is decided by one scan: for each term, check for a complementary pair; accept if any term has none. That is linear time, and the problem is in P.

The instinct that something must be wrong — "but every formula can be put in DNF, so convert the CNF and solve it" — is defused by §06's size trap. The conversion is correct and exponential. Distribute (x₁ ∨ y₁) ∧ (x₂ ∨ y₂) ∧ … ∧ (xₖ ∨ yₖ) and you get every way of choosing one literal from each clause: 2ᵏ terms, and no smaller DNF for this function exists, since the function has 2ᵏ minimal satisfying patterns. At k = 20 that is 1 048 576 terms from a formula of 40 literals. The hardness has not vanished; it has moved into the translation. The mirror image confirms the diagnosis: tautology checking — is this formula true under every assignment? — is trivial for CNF (check each clause for a complementary pair) and co-NP-complete for DNF. Each normal form makes one question easy and the dual question hard, and the two hard questions are hard in dual classes (§17).

Where the width boundary sits

  • 2-SAT is in P, in linear time. A clause (a ∨ b) is the pair of implications ¬a ⇒ b and ¬b ⇒ a, so a 2-CNF is a directed implication graph on the 2n literals, and the formula is unsatisfiable exactly when some variable x has x and ¬x in the same strongly connected component. Tarjan's algorithm from Graph Representations & Traversal, §22 settles it in O(n + m), and a satisfying assignment is read off the reverse topological order of the components. Greedy Algorithms, §24 makes the same point from the other side: two-literal clauses and Horn clauses are the two largest natural fragments on the easy side of the line. §16 draws the boundary in full.
  • 3-SAT is NP-complete, and stays complete under severe restrictions. Exactly three distinct literals per clause; each variable occurring at most three times, provided clauses of two and three literals are both allowed (impose both of the first two restrictions at once and every instance is satisfiable, by a Hall's-theorem matching of clauses to variables, so that particular combination is trivial); the formula's variable–clause incidence graph planar; monotone clauses (all-positive or all-negative) in the NAE variant — all still complete. That robustness is why 3-SAT is the workhorse source problem for the whole catalogue.
  • The optimisation versions cross the line earlier. MAX-2-SAT — satisfy as many 2-clauses as possible — is NP-hard even though deciding whether all of them can be satisfied is in P. Being able to answer "all or not all" in linear time tells you nothing about "how many", and §24 measures exactly that gap for MAX-3-SAT, where the best assignment on the eight-clause instance φ₈ satisfies 7 of 8 while the formula as a whole is unsatisfiable.
  • Practice runs the other way round. Modern solvers take CNF as their input format precisely because the Tseitin encoding of §08 puts everything into CNF cheaply, and they handle clauses of any width natively; the conversion to width 3 is a theoretical convenience for building gadgets, not something a solver wants (§30). A real toolchain encodes to CNF and stops.
The working formula. From here to §15 the page uses φ = (x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ ¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) ∧ (x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃): three variables, three clauses, nine literals, already in 3-CNF, and satisfied by exactly 5 of its 8 assignments — 001, 010, 101, 110, 111 in the bit order x₁x₂x₃. §11 turns it into a graph with 9 vertices — one per literal occurrence — whose 3-cliques are the consistent ways of choosing one satisfied literal from each clause (there are 12 of them, more than the 5 assignments, because an assignment can satisfy a clause in more than one way), §12 turns that into a vertex-cover instance, and §15 into a colouring instance; keeping one formula throughout means the reductions can be compared on the same object, and every figure re-derives its five satisfying assignments rather than trusting this sentence.

10 The reduction map: how the classic problems connect

The reduction map is the directed graph whose vertices are decision problems and whose arrow A → B means "there is a polynomial-time reduction A ≤ₚ B", so that hardness flows along the arrows: if A is NP-hard and A ≤ₚ B then B is NP-hard, and the whole catalogue of NP-complete problems is the set of vertices reachable from one root. That root is circuit satisfiability, which §08 proves NP-hard directly from the definition of a verifier; §09 pushes it into 3-CNF form; and every arrow after that is a construction — a gadget — that turns an instance of the problem at the tail into an instance of the problem at the head. Nobody proves a second problem hard from scratch. The whole discipline is: find the problem in the catalogue that most resembles yours, build the gadget, check both directions of the "if and only if", and check that the construction runs in polynomial time.

what an arrow means, and what it does notA → B means A ≤ₚ B : an instance x of A is transformed, in polynomial time, into an instance f(x) of B with x ∈ A ⟺ f(x) ∈ B reading it FORWARDS (the useful direction for proving hardness) A is NP-hard ⟹ B is NP-hard. "the tail's hardness transfers to the head" reading it BACKWARDS (the useful direction for solving) B ∈ P ⟹ A ∈ P. "the head's easiness transfers to the tail" so to prove YOUR problem B hard, you reduce a KNOWN-hard A TO B — A ≤ₚ B, arrow INTO B. Reducing B to SAT proves nothing about B's hardness (§06). the single most common error in this subject is drawing that arrow the wrong way round; §06 states the rule, this section's map is the rule drawn out. the spine of the map, and the gadget on each arrowCIRCUIT-SAT the root — NP-hard directly from the definition of a polynomial verifier (§08, Cook–Levin) ↓ one variable and 1–3 clauses per gate SAT a CNF formula; clauses of any width ↓ a fresh variable per split of a wide clause 3-SAT exactly three literals per clause — the source of every reduction below (§09) ├─→ CLIQUE (§11) a vertex per literal OCCURRENCE; edges between compatible literals in different clauses │ ↓ complement the graph │ VERTEX-COVER (§12) S is a clique in Ḡ ⟺ V∖S is a vertex cover of G │ ├─→ INDEPENDENT-SET (§12) the same set, read in the other graph — one problem in three costumes │ ├─→ SET-COVER an element per edge, a set per vertex (the greedy bound is §23) │ └─→ HAM-CYCLE (§13) a 12-vertex widget per edge, k selector vertices │ ↓ weight 1 on the edges of G, more elsewhere │ TSP (§13) the decision version; the sharper weights also kill approximation (§22) ├─→ SUBSET-SUM (§14) one number per (variable, polarity), slack numbers per clause, base 10 so no column carries │ ↓ add two numbers that force the split │ PARTITION (§14) ↓ weight = value = the number │ KNAPSACK (§14) decision version; the O(nT) table is PSEUDO-polynomial, not polynomial ├─→ 3-COLOURING (§15) a palette triangle T/F/B, a two-vertex gadget per variable, two OR-gadgets per clause └─→ 3-DIMENSIONAL-MATCHING and from there EXACT-COVER, and from SUBSET-SUM onward, BIN-PACKING and SCHEDULING

Why the map has one root

Cook–Levin does the only piece of work that cannot be delegated. To show that every language in NP reduces to circuit satisfiability, it does not look at any particular problem; it looks at the definition of NP itself — a polynomial-time verifier V(x, c) — and compiles that verifier, for the fixed input x and the fixed length bound on c, into a Boolean circuit whose inputs are the bits of the certificate and whose single output is V's verdict. The circuit is satisfiable exactly when some certificate is accepted. Because the verifier runs in polynomial time, the circuit has polynomially many gates, and building it is a mechanical transcription of the verifier's computation. That is the root, and it is the only vertex of the map whose hardness is established without reference to another vertex.

Everything downstream is comparison. Once CIRCUIT-SAT is NP-hard and 3-SAT inherits it, a new problem joins the catalogue by exhibiting one gadget. This is why the subject feels like a catalogue: the hard intellectual work happened once, in 1971, and the thousands of NP-completeness proofs since are each a single arrow added to a graph whose root was fixed then. It is also why a practitioner's job is pattern-matching. You do not prove your scheduling problem hard from the definition of NP; you notice that it contains 3-PARTITION, or that its objective is a disguised set cover, and you write the arrow.

Reading the map when you are trying to solve something

The backwards reading is the one that changes what you do on a Tuesday. If your problem sits at the head of an arrow whose tail is NP-hard, a polynomial algorithm for your problem would give a polynomial algorithm for the tail, hence for 3-SAT, hence — by §08 — for every problem in NP. That is not a proof that no such algorithm exists; §07 is explicit that P ≠ NP is unproven. It is a statement about company: your problem is now at least as hard as a problem that has resisted fifty years of attention by people who wanted very much to solve it. The correct inference is not "impossible" but "stop looking for an exact polynomial algorithm and read §19", which lists the four things to do instead.

The forwards reading matters too, in a way that is easy to miss. An arrow A → B also says that a good algorithm for B is a good algorithm for A. This is not idle: modern SAT solvers (§30) are fast enough that encoding a hard scheduling or verification problem into CNF and calling a solver is a standard engineering move. The reduction that proves your problem hard is often, run in the other direction, the reduction that lets you solve it. Hardness proofs and practical encodings are the same constructions with opposite intent.

The blow-up: reductions compose, and the sizes multiply

A reduction is only required to be polynomial, and polynomials compose: if f is O(nᵃ) and g is O(nᵇ) then g ∘ f is O(n^(ab)), still polynomial. That is all the theory needs. But the exponents are real, and a chain of four reductions applied to a tiny formula produces an instance nobody would want to hand a solver. The figure measures this by actually running the chain on the page's shared formula φ = (x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ ¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) ∧ (x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) — three variables, three clauses, nine literal occurrences — and reporting the size of every instance along the way.

stepinstance builtsize, measuredgrowth rule
—φ, a 3-CNF formulan = 3 variables, m = 3 clauses, 9 literal occurrencesthe input
§11CLIQUE9 vertices, 21 edges, k = 33m vertices, at most 9·C(m,2) edges
§12VERTEX-COVER (complement)9 vertices, 15 edges, k′ = 6same vertices, C(3m,2) − |E| edges, k′ = 3m − k
§12INDEPENDENT-SET9 vertices, 15 edges, k = 3the same graph, the complementary set
§13HAM-CYCLE186 vertices, 339 edges12|E| + k vertices, 16|E| + (2k − 1)|V| edges
§13TSP (decision)186 cities, 17 205 weights, budget 186the complete graph on the HAM-CYCLE vertices
§14SUBSET-SUM12 numbers of 6 decimal digits, target 1114442n + 2m numbers of n + m digits
§153-COLOURING27 vertices, 51 edges3 + 2n + 6m vertices, 3 + 3n + 13m edges

Nine literal occurrences become a 186-city travelling-salesman instance. Every one of those steps is polynomial, though not every one is linear: 3-SAT → CLIQUE can emit Θ(m²) edges; the complement step reads all C(3m,2) vertex pairs, Θ(V²) work; HAM-CYCLE is linear in |E| but its (2k − 1)|V| term is quadratic when k grows with |V|; TSP writes C(|V|,2) weights, quadratic again; SUBSET-SUM writes 2n + 2m numbers of n + m digits, quadratic in n + m; only 3-COLOURING is linear. Composed from φ to TSP, the size is O(m⁴) in the number of clauses. The degrees are modest; the constants are what make the composition unusable in practice. This is the honest content of "polynomial-time reduction": it is a statement about asymptotics and about the class boundary, not a recipe for solving anything. The figure lets you click any node and see the chain that proves it hard, with every intermediate size recomputed by running the reductions.

The reduction map: click a problem to highlight the chain that proves it NP-hard, with every instance size along the chain measured by running the reductions on φ blue = the chain of ancestors from CIRCUIT-SAT down to the problem you clicked (the proof of its hardness); amber = the problem itself; grey = the rest of the catalogue; each arrow is labelled with the gadget; the table lists the measured size of the instance produced at each step

What this page proves, what it describes, and what it only names

  • Carried out in full, with both directions of the proof and a verified worked instance. 3-SAT ≤ₚ CLIQUE (§11); CLIQUE ≤ₚ VERTEX-COVER and the VERTEX-COVER / INDEPENDENT-SET / CLIQUE identity (§12); HAM-CYCLE ≤ₚ TSP (§13); 3-SAT ≤ₚ SUBSET-SUM, SUBSET-SUM ≤ₚ PARTITION, SUBSET-SUM ≤ₚ KNAPSACK (§14); 3-SAT ≤ₚ 3-COLOURING (§15). Each of those five sections builds the reduced instance in its figure and cross-checks the answer against exhaustive search on both sides.
  • Described precisely, with the gadget's key property verified but the full reduction not rebuilt. VERTEX-COVER ≤ₚ HAM-CYCLE (§13). The construction's widget is 12 vertices and 14 edges per edge of G; the figure enumerates every path through that widget and confirms that exactly the three claimed traversal patterns exist and that the two forbidden ones do not. The assembly of widgets and selector vertices into G′ is described and its size computed, but G′ itself is too large to search exhaustively for the instances on this page, and the figure says so rather than pretending otherwise.
  • Named, with the gadget in one phrase and no proof. SET-COVER, 3-DIMENSIONAL-MATCHING, EXACT-COVER, BIN-PACKING, HAMILTONIAN-PATH, LONGEST-PATH, STEINER-TREE, INTEGER-PROGRAMMING, MAX-CUT, CLIQUE-COVER, FEEDBACK-VERTEX-SET. These appear on the map and in the boundary table of §16; the point of naming them is that when you meet one in the wild you should recognise it and stop looking for an exact polynomial algorithm.
The discipline, in four steps. To prove a new problem B NP-complete: (1) show B ∈ NP by naming the certificate and the polynomial verifier — skip this and you have proved NP-hardness only, which is a different and weaker claim (§07); (2) choose a known NP-complete A that resembles B structurally; (3) give a map f from instances of A to instances of B and argue it runs in polynomial time — which almost always means "the output has polynomial size and each piece is computed locally"; (4) prove x ∈ A ⟹ f(x) ∈ B and f(x) ∈ B ⟹ x ∈ A, both directions, because the forward direction alone is satisfied by the reduction that maps everything to a fixed yes-instance. Step 4's second half is where real proofs fail, and it is the half this page writes out every time.

11 3-SAT → Clique

CLIQUE asks, given an undirected graph G and an integer k, whether G contains k mutually adjacent vertices; it is NP-complete, and the proof reduces 3-SAT to it by making one vertex per literal occurrence, joining two vertices exactly when they lie in different clauses and their literals are not complementary, and asking for a clique of size k = m, the number of clauses. The reduction is the cleanest in the catalogue because the two "if and only if" directions are almost the same sentence read twice: a clique of size m must take exactly one vertex from each clause (vertices in the same clause are never adjacent), and the literals it takes are pairwise non-complementary, so they can all be made true at once — which is precisely a satisfying assignment. This section carries out the construction on the page's formula φ, gets a 9-vertex, 21-edge graph, and verifies by brute force over all C(9,3) = 84 triples that its maximum clique is exactly 3.

the problemCLIQUE = { ⟨G, k⟩ : G is an undirected graph containing a set of k vertices that are pairwise adjacent } a CLIQUE of G is a subset S ⊆ V with every pair u, v ∈ S, u ≠ v, joined by an edge — a complete subgraph the DECISION problem asks "is there one of size ≥ k?"; the OPTIMISATION problem asks for the largest; §03 shows the two are polynomial-time equivalent CLIQUE ∈ NP certificate: the list of k vertices. Verifier: check |S| = k and check all C(k,2) pairs are edges — O(k²) lookups in an adjacency matrix, polynomial in |⟨G,k⟩|. CLIQUE is NP-hard by the reduction below from 3-SAT (§09), which is NP-hard by Cook–Levin (§08). CLIQUE is therefore NP-COMPLETE — both halves are needed; the second alone would make it NP-hard but not necessarily in NP (§07). the reduction 3-SAT ≤ₚ CLIQUEGIVEN φ = C₁ ∧ C₂ ∧ … ∧ C_m in 3-CNF, with C_r = (l₁ʳ ∨ l₂ʳ ∨ l₃ʳ) BUILD V = { vᵣⁱ : r = 1…m, i = 1…3 } one vertex per literal OCCURRENCE — 3m vertices, NOT one per variable E = { (vᵣⁱ, v_sʲ) : r ≠ s AND lᵢʳ ≠ ¬l_jˢ } join two occurrences in DIFFERENT clauses whose literals are CONSISTENT k = m ask for one vertex per clause COST 3m vertices; at most 9·C(m,2) = O(m²) edges; each edge decided by one comparison of two literals — O(m²) time, clearly polynomial note two things the edge rule does NOT say. It does NOT forbid the SAME literal appearing in two clauses — x₁ in C₁ and x₁ in C₃ ARE joined (they are consistent). It does NOT join two occurrences inside one clause, whatever their literals — that is what forces a clique of size m to spread one vertex per clause.

Both directions of the proof

If φ is satisfiable then G has a clique of size m. Take a satisfying assignment a. Every clause has at least one literal made true by a; pick one such literal per clause and let S be the corresponding m vertices, one from each clause. Any two of them lie in different clauses, so the first edge condition holds. Any two of them are literals made true by the same assignment, so they cannot be complementary — a cannot make both xᵢ and ¬xᵢ true — so the second condition holds. Every pair is joined; S is a clique of size m.

If G has a clique of size m then φ is satisfiable. Let S be such a clique. No two vertices of S lie in the same clause, because same-clause vertices are never adjacent; since there are m clauses and |S| = m, S has exactly one vertex in each. Now define an assignment: for each vertex of S carrying literal l, set the underlying variable so that l is true. This is consistent — two vertices of S carrying complementary literals would not be adjacent, so no clique contains such a pair — and any variable not mentioned by S may be set arbitrarily. Under this assignment each clause has the literal at its chosen vertex true, so every clause is satisfied.

Notice what each half of the edge rule buys. The "different clauses" half makes the clique's size a counter: a clique of size m is forced to touch every clause. The "not complementary" half makes it a consistency check: the literals it touches can be simultaneously true. The reduction is exactly the statement that a satisfying assignment is a choice of one true literal per clause with no self-contradiction, and CLIQUE is the graph-shaped way of saying "choose one from each group, pairwise compatible".

Worked in full on φ

The formula is φ = (x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ ¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) ∧ (x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃), so m = 3, k = 3, and G has 3m = 9 vertices. Count the edges by hand, then check the count against the figure. There are C(3,2) = 3 pairs of distinct clauses and 3 × 3 = 9 literal pairs across each, so 27 candidate edges; subtract the complementary pairs.

clause paircandidate edgescomplementary pairs (excluded)edges kept
C₁ – C₂9x₁/¬x₁, ¬x₂/x₂, ¬x₃/x₃ — 36
C₁ – C₃9¬x₂/x₂, ¬x₃/x₃ — 27
C₂ – C₃9¬x₁/x₁ — 18
total27621

So G has 9 vertices and 21 edges, which is what the figure measures when it runs the construction. Brute force over all C(9,3) = 84 triples finds 12 that are cliques, and enumerating all 2⁹ = 512 subsets confirms that none of size 4 or more is a clique, so the maximum clique is exactly 3 = k and the reduction's promise holds: φ is satisfiable and G has a k-clique. The subset census is a useful sanity check in itself — 1 empty set, 9 singletons, 21 pairs (one per edge, as it must be), 12 triples, 0 larger.

Reading the assignments back out is the reverse map of the proof. Each of the 12 triples names three literals; setting their variables accordingly gives a partial assignment, and the free variables may be filled in arbitrarily. The 12 triples produce 9 distinct partial patterns — 11*, 110, 1*1, 101, 010, *10, 111, 001, *01, where * is a variable no chosen literal mentions — and completing the stars in every possible way yields exactly {001, 010, 101, 110, 111}, which is exactly the set of 5 satisfying assignments of φ found by exhaustive search over all 2³ = 8 assignments. The figure checks that identity at load and flags it. The correspondence is not a bijection in either direction — several cliques give the same assignment, and one clique with a star covers two — but the sets match, which is what the theorem claims.

What the reduction does on an unsatisfiable formula

Run the same construction on φ₈, the conjunction of all eight clauses over three variables, which is unsatisfiable. It has m = 8, so G has 24 vertices and k = 8. The candidate edges number C(8,2) × 9 = 252; the figure measures 204 edges, so 48 pairs were complementary. The maximum clique, computed by a Bron–Kerbosch search under a node counter, is 7 — one short of k = 8, so the reduction correctly reports "no". And 7 is not an accident: the largest number of clauses of φ₈ that any single assignment can satisfy is 7 (this is the instance §24 uses for MAX-3-SAT), and a clique of size j in this graph is precisely a consistent choice of one true literal from each of j clauses. The maximum clique of the reduced graph equals the maximum number of simultaneously satisfiable clauses — a stronger statement than the reduction needs, and one worth noticing because it is the seed of the approximation-preserving reductions in §20.

3-SAT → Clique: the formula, the graph the reduction builds, a maximum clique found by brute force, and the assignment read back out of it vertices are grouped by clause (one column each) and labelled with their literal; grey edges = compatible pairs in different clauses; amber = the clique currently shown; the readout cross-checks the brute-force maximum clique against the reduction's promise and the recovered assignment against exhaustive satisfiability search

Three things the reduction is often misread as saying

  • It does not say CLIQUE is harder than 3-SAT. A ≤ₚ B says B is at least as hard as A, up to polynomial factors. Since 3-SAT is NP-complete and CLIQUE is in NP, the reduction plus membership makes them equivalent: each reduces to the other, and a polynomial algorithm for either gives one for both. All NP-complete problems are interreducible; the map of §10 is a tree only because that is the order the proofs were found in, not because the relation is asymmetric.
  • It does not give an efficient algorithm for either problem. The reduction is polynomial; the search on the far side is not. Reducing 3-SAT on n variables and m clauses to CLIQUE and then running a brute-force clique search costs C(3m, m) candidate subsets, which is about 6.75^m by Stirling — worse than the 2ⁿ of direct search for every m above roughly 0.36·n, which is to say for essentially every formula anyone cares about. Reductions relocate a problem; they do not shrink it.
  • The vertex count is 3m, not 2n. One vertex per literal occurrence. A formula may repeat a literal across clauses and each repetition gets its own vertex — x₁ in C₁ and x₁ in C₃ are two vertices, joined by an edge. Building one vertex per literal instead breaks the reduction, because then a clique could not pick one vertex per clause.
  • Maximum clique and maximum independent set are the same problem in complementary graphs, which is the content of §12 — and the reason CLIQUE is usually the first stop on the way to the whole vertex-cover family.
Complexity of the reduction itself. Building G takes O(m²) time and O(m²) space: 3m vertices and at most 9·C(m,2) edges, each decided by one literal comparison. Reading a satisfying assignment back out of a clique takes O(m). Both are comfortably polynomial in |⟨φ⟩|, which is what the definition of ≤ₚ in §06 requires — and the requirement is on the reduction, not on anything that happens afterwards. A construction that produced an exponentially large graph would not be a polynomial-time reduction even if it were otherwise correct; this is exactly the trap that §14's pseudo-polynomial discussion turns on.

12 Clique → Vertex Cover → Independent Set

For any graph G = (V, E) and any subset S ⊆ V, these three statements are equivalent: S is an independent set of G; V ∖ S is a vertex cover of G; S is a clique of the complement graph Ḡ. One combinatorial fact, three problems — and so three NP-completeness proofs for the price of one. Maximum independent set, minimum vertex cover and maximum clique are the same optimisation wearing different clothes: the answers are related by |max independent set| = |V| − |min vertex cover| and by complementation of the graph, and any algorithm for one solves the other two after O(V + E) or O(V²) of rewriting. This section proves the cover half of the equivalence — the clique half is the complementation identity already proved in §06 — uses it to get CLIQUE ≤ₚ VERTEX-COVER from §11, and works the trio on a 7-vertex graph whose optimum is confirmed by enumerating all 2⁷ = 128 subsets.

the three problemsVERTEX-COVER = { ⟨G, k⟩ : G has a set S ⊆ V, |S| ≤ k, with every edge of G having at least one endpoint in S } "hit every edge" — a MINIMISATION problem INDEPENDENT-SET = { ⟨G, k⟩ : G has a set S ⊆ V, |S| ≥ k, with no two vertices of S joined by an edge } "pick no two neighbours" — a MAXIMISATION problem CLIQUE = { ⟨G, k⟩ : G has a set S ⊆ V, |S| ≥ k, with every two vertices of S joined by an edge } "pick all-neighbours" — a MAXIMISATION problem all three are in NP: the certificate is S itself, and the verifier checks |E| edges or C(k,2) pairs. the identity: the cover half proved here in one line, the clique half restated from §06S is INDEPENDENT in G ⟺ no edge has BOTH endpoints in S ⟺ every edge has at least one endpoint OUTSIDE S ⟺ V∖S is a VERTEX COVER of G S is a CLIQUE in Ḡ ⟺ S is INDEPENDENT in G — the complementation identity behind INDEPENDENT-SET ≤ₚ CLIQUE, proved in §06 consequences, exact and not merely asymptotic: α(G) + τ(G) = |V| α = size of a maximum independent set, τ = size of a minimum vertex cover (Gallai, 1959) α(G) = ω(Ḡ) ω = size of a maximum clique and therefore ⟨G, k⟩ ∈ CLIQUE ⟺ ⟨Ḡ, |V| − k⟩ ∈ VERTEX-COVER ⟺ ⟨Ḡ, k⟩ ∈ INDEPENDENT-SET the reduction CLIQUE ≤ₚ VERTEX-COVERGIVEN ⟨G, k⟩, an instance of CLIQUE with G = (V, E) BUILD Ḡ = (V, Ē) with Ē = { (u, v) : u ≠ v, (u, v) ∉ E } the COMPLEMENT: same vertices, edges exactly where G has none k′ = |V| − k OUTPUT ⟨Ḡ, k′⟩, an instance of VERTEX-COVER CORRECT G has a clique of size k ⟺ that clique is an independent set of Ḡ ⟺ its complement, of size |V| − k, is a vertex cover of Ḡ COST O(V²) — the complement of a graph on V vertices has C(V,2) − |E| edges, computed by one pass over the adjacency matrix. Polynomial. Not linear.

The proof written out

Forward. Suppose G has a clique S with |S| = k. Every pair inside S is an edge of G, hence a non-edge of Ḡ, so S is independent in Ḡ. Take any edge (u, v) of Ḡ. It cannot have both endpoints in S, so at least one endpoint lies in V ∖ S. Therefore V ∖ S covers every edge of Ḡ and has size |V| − k = k′.

Backward. Suppose Ḡ has a vertex cover C with |C| ≤ k′ = |V| − k. Let S = V ∖ C, so |S| ≥ k. If some pair u, v ∈ S were joined in Ḡ, that edge would have neither endpoint in C, contradicting the cover. So S is independent in Ḡ, hence a clique in G, of size at least k; discard vertices to get exactly k if you want the sizes to match. Both directions hold, the construction is polynomial, and since §11 made CLIQUE NP-hard and VERTEX-COVER is visibly in NP, VERTEX-COVER is NP-complete. The same two paragraphs with Ḡ replaced by G prove INDEPENDENT-SET NP-complete without touching the graph at all.

What "take the complement" costs

The complement is cheap in theory and expensive in practice, and the difference is worth stating precisely. A graph with V vertices and E edges has a complement with C(V,2) − E edges, so a sparse graph — E = O(V), the usual case — has a dense complement with Θ(V²) edges. The 7-vertex instance below has 8 edges and a complement with C(7,2) − 8 = 21 − 8 = 13. The clique graph of §11 has 9 vertices and 21 edges, and its complement has C(9,2) − 21 = 36 − 21 = 15; the figure measures both and its vertex-cover optimum on the complement is 6, which is 9 − 3, exactly as α + τ = |V| requires.

Two operational consequences. First, an adjacency-list representation, which is the right one for sparse graphs, is the wrong one on the far side of a complement: the reduction turns a linear-size input into a quadratic-size one, which is polynomial and therefore legal, but means "the same problem" now needs Θ(V²) memory. Second, this is why practitioners solve independent set and vertex cover directly on the sparse graph rather than complementing into a clique solver — the two problems are equivalent in complexity but wildly different in the constants, and a state-of-the-art clique solver on Ḡ will often lose to a mediocre vertex-cover solver on G. Equivalence up to polynomial factors is a statement about class membership, not about which code to write.

The trio on one graph

The demonstration graph has vertices a … g and the 8 edges ab, bc, cd, ce, de, df, dg, ef. Enumerating all 2⁷ = 128 subsets — the figure does this at load, under a counter — gives:

questionanswerthe setchecked against
minimum vertex cover of G3, and it is unique{b, d, e}all 128 subsets tested for "covers every one of the 8 edges"
maximum independent set of G4{a, c, f, g}all 128 subsets tested for "contains no edge"
maximum clique of Ḡ4{a, c, f, g} — the same setall 128 subsets of the 13-edge complement
α + τ7 = |V|the two sets are complementaryarithmetic, re-checked in the readout

Check the cover by hand once, because the checking is the whole content of the problem. Is {b, d, e} a cover? ab → b ✓; bc → b ✓; cd → d ✓; ce → e ✓; de → both ✓; df → d ✓; dg → d ✓; ef → e ✓. Eight edges, all hit. Is {a, c, f, g} independent? The pairs are ac, af, ag, cf, cg, fg, and none is in the edge list ✓. And the two sets partition {a…g}, which is the identity made visible. No 2-element cover exists — d alone leaves ab, bc, ce and ef; adding any single vertex cannot hit all four — so 3 is optimal, and the enumeration confirms it is the only cover of size 3.

The uniqueness matters for §21. Because the optimum is exactly 3, the greedy maximal-matching 2-approximation, which returns both endpoints of every matched edge, has a worst case available on this very graph: taking the edges in the stored order gives the matching (a,b), (c,d), (e,f), all three edges pairwise vertex-disjoint and maximal (every remaining edge touches a matched vertex), so the algorithm returns all 6 endpoints — a ratio of exactly 6/3 = 2.000, the bound met with equality. That is the sharpest kind of example: not "close to 2" but "2 exactly", which proves the analysis of the approximation cannot be improved.

One set, three readings: switch between vertex cover, independent set and clique-in-the-complement and watch the same 7-vertex graph answer all three blue = the set S currently selected; grey = its complement; red edges = edges left uncovered (vertex-cover view) or edges inside S (independent-set view); the readout enumerates all 128 subsets for each of the three questions and flags the identity α + τ = |V|

Why this trio is the workhorse of the catalogue

  • Vertex cover is the standard source for further reductions because "hit every edge with few vertices" is a shape that recurs: SET-COVER generalises it (an element per edge, a set per vertex — the greedy analysis is §23), DOMINATING-SET is its close cousin, and the HAM-CYCLE construction of §13 reduces from it rather than from 3-SAT because the "k chosen vertices" translate naturally into k selector vertices on a cycle.
  • Vertex cover is the standard example for two coping strategies at once. It has a clean 2-approximation from a maximal matching (§21) — and, unusually, that factor 2 is essentially the best known: under the unique games conjecture no polynomial (2 − ε)-approximation exists, and, assuming only P ≠ NP, no polynomial approximation with ratio below √2 ≈ 1.414 exists (by way of the 2-to-2 games theorem, which superseded the earlier threshold of about 1.36). It also has a textbook fixed-parameter algorithm running in O(1.2738ᵏ + k·n) time, which is polynomial in n for every fixed k (§27). The same problem is a poster child for "approximate it" and for "the parameter is small", and which route you take depends on which of k and the required accuracy is the binding constraint.
  • Independent set is much harder to approximate than vertex cover, even though the two are complementary. This is the standard warning that approximation ratios do not transfer across a reduction that preserves exact answers: a cover of size 2·τ gives an independent set of size |V| − 2τ, which may be far below α = |V| − τ and may even be zero. Unless P = NP, maximum independent set cannot be approximated within n^(1−ε) for any fixed ε > 0. Exact-equivalence and approximation-equivalence are different relations, and §20 introduces the stricter reductions that do preserve ratios.
  • On special graph classes the problems split apart. Minimum vertex cover is polynomial on bipartite graphs, where König's theorem makes it equal to the maximum matching and a max-flow computation solves it; maximum independent set is polynomial on bipartite graphs for the same reason, and on trees by a simple tree dynamic program. Maximum clique is polynomial on chordal and on perfect graphs. Hardness is a statement about the general problem; the first question to ask about a real instance is what class it lies in.
Optimisation versus decision, stated carefully here because it is easy to slip. The decision problems VERTEX-COVER, INDEPENDENT-SET and CLIQUE — each with a bound k in the input — are NP-complete. The optimisation problems "find a minimum vertex cover", "find a maximum independent set" and "find a maximum clique" are NP-hard, and are not NP-complete, because they are not decision problems and so are not members of NP at all: NP is a class of languages. Saying "minimum vertex cover is NP-complete" is loose shorthand that this page avoids. §03 shows the two versions are polynomial-time equivalent by binary search on k plus self-reducibility, so nothing is lost by proving hardness for the decision version — but the two labels are not interchangeable, and §07 gives the definitions they come from.

13 Hamiltonian cycle, and Hamiltonian cycle → TSP

A Hamiltonian cycle of an undirected graph is a cycle that visits every vertex exactly once; deciding whether one exists is NP-complete, and the travelling-salesman decision problem — "is there a tour of all cities of total weight at most B?" — inherits that hardness through a reduction so simple it fits in one line: put weight 1 on the edges of G, weight 2 on every other pair, and set B = |V|. The contrast with the Euler tour, which visits every edge exactly once and is decided in O(V + E) by counting odd-degree vertices, is the sharpest "same-looking pair, different cost" on this page: one local condition per vertex versus a global condition on the whole ordering. This section states the Hamiltonian-cycle reduction honestly — it comes from VERTEX-COVER, with a 12-vertex widget per edge whose three admissible traversals the figure verifies exhaustively — then does the TSP reduction in full, and finally sharpens the weights into the version that §22 uses to rule out any constant-factor approximation of general TSP.

the two problems, and the one they are confused withHAM-CYCLE = { ⟨G⟩ : the undirected graph G contains a simple cycle through EVERY VERTEX exactly once } NP-COMPLETE HAM-PATH = { ⟨G⟩ : G contains a simple path through every vertex exactly once } NP-complete too; a graph can have a Hamiltonian path and no cycle EULER-TOUR = { ⟨G⟩ : G contains a closed walk using EVERY EDGE exactly once } in P — decided in O(V + E) EULER's criterion (1736) a connected graph (ignoring isolated vertices) has an Euler CIRCUIT ⟺ every vertex has EVEN degree it has an Euler TRAIL (open, endpoints differ) ⟺ exactly TWO vertices have odd degree — a LOCAL condition, one integer per vertex, so it is a single scan; Hierholzer's algorithm then BUILDS the tour in O(E). there is no such criterion for HAM-CYCLE, and — unless P = NP — there is none to find: a polynomial-time checkable characterisation would put HAM-CYCLE in P. Dirac's and Ore's theorems give SUFFICIENT conditions for |V| ≥ 3 (min degree ≥ |V|/2; deg(u) + deg(v) ≥ |V| for every non-adjacent pair), never necessary ones. TSP, in its three commonly-confused formsTSP (decision) ⟨complete graph, weights w, bound B⟩ : is there a tour visiting every city once with total weight ≤ B? NP-COMPLETE TSP (optimisation) find the cheapest such tour NP-HARD — and NOT NP-complete, because it is not a decision problem (§07) METRIC TSP the special case where w is symmetric and obeys the triangle inequality w(u,w) ≤ w(u,v) + w(v,w) still NP-hard; has a 3/2-approximation (§22) EUCLIDEAN TSP the special case where cities are points in the plane and w is the straight-line distance still NP-hard; has a PTAS (§22) these are three different problems with three different approximability stories, and §22 keeps them apart. "TSP is NP-complete" without qualification means the decision version.

Why Euler is easy and Hamilton is not

Both ask for a closed walk with a covering property, and they were even posed in the same century, so the difference in cost deserves a reason rather than a shrug. Euler's condition is local and additive: a closed walk enters and leaves each vertex the same number of times, so every vertex's degree is used up in pairs, so every degree must be even — and conversely, even degrees plus connectivity are enough, because Hierholzer's algorithm can always splice a new closed sub-walk into the current one at any vertex with unused edges. The property decomposes over vertices; checking it is V independent tests.

The Hamiltonian condition does not decompose. Whether a vertex can be visited exactly once depends on which of its neighbours the rest of the tour has already consumed, and there is no quantity per vertex whose values can be checked independently. Every attempt at a local criterion fails on some graph: the Petersen graph is 3-regular, 3-connected, vertex-transitive and has girth 5 — as homogeneous as a graph can be — and has no Hamiltonian cycle at all, while having a Hamiltonian path. It is exactly the kind of counterexample that a plausible degree-based criterion dies on. The figure runs both tests side by side and prints both counts: the Euler test is a degree scan, and the Hamiltonian search enumerates tours.

The two instances, and what exhaustive search measures

The first instance gHam has 8 vertices and the 11 edges 01, 12, 23, 34, 45, 56, 67, 70, 03, 16, 25. Enumerating every cyclic ordering of the 8 vertices — fixing vertex 0 and taking one of each direction-pair, so 7!/2 = 2 520 tours — finds exactly 2 Hamiltonian cycles: 0-1-2-3-4-5-6-7 (the outer 8-cycle) and 0-3-4-5-2-1-6-7. The figure measures 2 520 tours and 4 356 edge lookups to establish that. Its degree sequence is ⟨3,3,3,3,2,3,3,2⟩, so six vertices have odd degree and the Euler test answers "no Euler circuit" after a degree scan of |E| + |V| = 19 increments — 41 in all once the connectivity walk, which traverses both directions of every edge, is counted.

The second instance is the Petersen graph: 10 vertices, 15 edges, 3-regular. All 9!/2 = 181 440 tours are enumerated — 296 787 edge lookups — and none is a Hamiltonian cycle. A Hamiltonian path does exist, and a depth-first search finds 0-1-2-3-4-9-6-8-5-7 by taking the lowest-numbered unused neighbour at every step, with no backtracking at all: 10 search nodes, one per vertex. That juxtaposition is the honest picture of exponential search. Finding a solution can be instantaneous; proving there is none is what costs, and it costs the whole space. Every vertex having odd degree (3) also means the Petersen graph has no Euler circuit, established by a 25-step degree scan (55 steps with the connectivity walk).

For a graph that does have an Euler circuit, the figure offers a third instance: gHam with the edge 56 removed and the edges 02 and 13 added, giving 12 edges and the degree sequence ⟨4,4,4,4,2,2,2,2⟩ — all even, and connected. The 20-step degree scan (44 with the connectivity walk) reports "yes", and Hierholzer's algorithm then produces the circuit 0-1-2-3-4-5-2-0-7-6-1-3-0 in 12 further steps, one per edge — Θ(E). On that same graph the Hamiltonian search still has to walk all 2 520 tours, at 5 678 edge checks. Same 8 vertices, same order of magnitude of edges, two questions, and the measured costs differ by a factor of about 130 at n = 8 and grow apart factorially.

HAM-CYCLE is NP-complete: the widget, stated honestly

Membership in NP is immediate — the certificate is the sequence of |V| vertices, and the verifier checks that each vertex appears once and that consecutive pairs, including the wrap-around, are edges: O(V) lookups. Hardness is proved by reducing VERTEX-COVER ≤ₚ HAM-CYCLE, taking the vertex-cover problem that §12 has just made NP-complete as the source. The construction turns an instance ⟨G, k⟩ into a graph G′ that has a Hamiltonian cycle if and only if G has a vertex cover of size k.

the widget: one copy per edge of Gfor each edge (u, v) ∈ E, G′ contains a widget W(u,v) with 12 vertices and 14 edges: two columns of six [u,v,1] … [u,v,6] and [v,u,1] … [v,u,6] joined as two paths: 10 edges four crossing edges [u,v,1]–[v,u,3] [u,v,3]–[v,u,1] [u,v,4]–[v,u,6] [u,v,6]–[v,u,4] : 4 edges the ONLY vertices of the widget with edges going OUTSIDE it are the four corners [u,v,1], [u,v,6], [v,u,1], [v,u,6]. Under that restriction, a Hamiltonian cycle of G′ can traverse the widget in exactly THREE ways — VERIFIED in the figure by enumerating every path: (b) enter [u,v,1], leave [u,v,6], covering all 12 vertices "u is in the cover, v is not" exactly 1 such path exists (d) enter [v,u,1], leave [v,u,6], covering all 12 vertices "v is in the cover, u is not" exactly 1 such path exists (c) two disjoint passes, [u,v,1]→[u,v,6] and [v,u,1]→[v,u,6], six vertices each "both u and v are in the cover" exactly 1 such pair exists and the forbidden patterns do NOT exist: 0 paths from [u,v,1] to [v,u,6] covering all 12, 0 from [v,u,1] to [u,v,6], and 0 ways to split into disjoint [u,v,1]→[v,u,6] and [v,u,1]→[u,v,6] covering all 12. That is what makes the widget a widget. assembling G′, and its sizefor each vertex u ∈ V, order its neighbours u⁽¹⁾ … u⁽ᵈᵉᵍ ᵘ⁾ and chain the widgets on u's edges: add the edges ([u, u⁽ⁱ⁾, 6], [u, u⁽ⁱ⁺¹⁾, 1]) for i = 1 … deg(u) − 1 add k SELECTOR vertices s₁ … s_k, each joined to the FIRST vertex [u, u⁽¹⁾, 1] and the LAST vertex [u, u⁽ᵈᵉᵍ ᵘ⁾, 6] of every such chain |V′| = 12|E| + k ≤ 12|E| + |V| |E′| = 14|E| + (2|E| − |V|) + 2k|V| = 16|E| + (2k − 1)|V| ≤ 16|E| + (2|V| − 1)|V| — polynomial, and constructible in polynomial time WHY IT WORKS a Hamiltonian cycle of G′ must leave each selector sⱼ into the chain of some vertex u, walk the whole chain, and come back to a selector. k selectors ⟹ exactly k chains are "entered", i.e. exactly k vertices of G are chosen ⟹ every widget (every EDGE of G) is covered by one of those chains ⟹ every edge of G has a chosen endpoint ⟹ the k chosen vertices are a vertex cover. Reverse the argument for the converse.

The figure verifies the widget's traversal property by exhaustive search over paths through its 12 vertices, and reports the counts — 1, 1, 1 for the three admissible patterns and 0, 0, 0 for the forbidden ones. What it does not do is search G′ itself: applied to the 7-vertex instance of §12 with k = 3, the construction yields |V′| = 12·8 + 3 = 99 vertices and |E′| = 16·8 + 5·7 = 163 edges, and no exhaustive tour search over 98!/2 orderings will ever run. That is the honest limit of what a browser figure can check, and the page says so rather than printing a number it did not measure. The widget's three-traversal lemma is the load-bearing part of the proof, and that part is measured.

HAM-CYCLE ≤ₚ TSP, the easy version

the reductionGIVEN ⟨G⟩ = (V, E), an instance of HAM-CYCLE BUILD the COMPLETE graph K on the same vertex set V, with w(u, v) = 1 if (u, v) ∈ E w(u, v) = 2 if (u, v) ∉ E and the budget B = |V| OUTPUT ⟨K, w, B⟩, an instance of TSP (decision) COST C(|V|, 2) weights, each set by one adjacency lookup — O(V²) time and space. Polynomial. CORRECT every tour visits |V| cities and so uses exactly |V| edges. G has a Hamiltonian cycle ⟹ that cycle is a tour, all |V| of its edges have weight 1, total = |V| = B ⟹ a tour of weight ≤ B exists. a tour of weight ≤ |V| exists ⟹ since each of its |V| edges costs 1 or 2, ALL of them cost 1 ⟹ all of them are edges of G ⟹ the tour is a Hamiltonian cycle of G. so ⟨G⟩ ∈ HAM-CYCLE ⟺ ⟨K, w, B⟩ ∈ TSP. Both directions; both needed.

The figure runs this reduction on both instances and searches the resulting TSP instance exhaustively. On gHam: the optimum over all 2 520 tours is 8, which equals the budget |V| = 8, and the optimal tour is 0-1-2-3-4-5-6-7 — one of the two Hamiltonian cycles, as it must be. On the Petersen graph: the optimum over all 181 440 tours is 11, strictly above the budget of 10, and the optimal tour 0-1-2-3-4-7-9-6-8-5 uses nine graph edges and exactly one non-edge. The gap of 1 is the smallest possible, and that is exactly why this weighting says nothing useful about approximation. On a Hamiltonian instance OPT = |V|, so a ρ-approximation may return anything up to ρ·|V|; on a non-Hamiltonian one OPT is only |V| + 1. The test "is the returned weight ≤ |V|?" therefore stops certifying anything as soon as ρ·|V| ≥ |V| + 1, that is for every fixed ρ > 1 once |V| ≥ 1/(ρ − 1). At ρ = 1.2 that threshold is |V| ≥ 5, and both instances here are past it: a 1.2-approximation on gHam may return 1.2 × 8 = 9.6 > 8, so its answer no longer distinguishes the two cases. The construction rules out only approximations with ratio below 1 + 1/|V|, which degrades to nothing as the instance grows — so the outside weight has to be widened.

The sharpened weights, and why §22 needs them

Widen the gap and the argument becomes an inapproximability proof. Fix any constant ρ ≥ 1 and build the same complete graph with

the gap versionw(u, v) = 1 if (u, v) ∈ E w(u, v) = ρ·|V| + 1 if (u, v) ∉ E then G HAS a Hamiltonian cycle ⟹ OPT = |V| G has NO Hamiltonian cycle ⟹ every tour uses ≥ 1 non-edge ⟹ OPT ≥ (ρ|V| + 1) + (|V| − 1) = ρ|V| + |V| > ρ·|V| so a polynomial ρ-approximation A, which by definition returns a tour of weight ≤ ρ·OPT, would answer HAM-CYCLE in polynomial time: A returns ≤ ρ|V| ⟺ G is Hamiltonian. Therefore: if P ≠ NP, general TSP has NO polynomial ρ-approximation for ANY constant ρ ≥ 1. note where the hypothesis goes: the constructed instance VIOLATES the triangle inequality on purpose, so the argument says nothing about METRIC TSP, which does have a 3/2-approximation (§22).

The figure builds this instance too. With ρ = 2 the non-edge weight is 2·10 + 1 = 21, and the exhaustive optimum on the Petersen instance is 30 — nine weight-1 edges plus one weight-21 non-edge — against the threshold ρ·|V| = 20. On gHam the optimum is 8 = |V|, comfortably under ρ·|V| = 16. A 2-approximation would have to return at most 16 on the Hamiltonian instance and could not possibly return less than 30 on the non-Hamiltonian one, so its output alone decides HAM-CYCLE. §22 quotes this argument; the numbers above are what it rests on.

Two details that are worth getting exactly right, because they are where this argument is usually mis-stated. First, the non-edge weight must be a polynomial-size number for the reduction to be polynomial: ρ|V| + 1 is written in O(log(ρ|V|)) bits, so it is, for constant ρ. Second, the conclusion is about constant-factor approximation of general (non-metric) TSP and is conditional on P ≠ NP. It is not a claim that general TSP has no useful heuristics — §28 and §30 are full of algorithms that solve large real instances — and it is not a claim about metric or Euclidean TSP, which are genuinely easier to approximate. Three problems, three stories.

Hamiltonian cycle by exhaustive tour search, the Euler test run beside it, and the TSP instance the reduction builds — every count measured left: the graph, with the tour currently found highlighted; right: the widget of the vertex-cover reduction with its three admissible traversals; the readout reports tours enumerated, edge lookups, Euler degree-scan steps, and the exhaustive TSP optimum against the reduction's budget under both the 1/2 weighting and the gap weighting
What to carry forward. Euler is local and linear; Hamilton is global and NP-complete, and the Petersen graph shows why no degree-based criterion can work. HAM-CYCLE's hardness comes from VERTEX-COVER through a 12-vertex-per-edge widget whose whole job is to make "this edge is covered from the u side / the v side / from both" the only three ways a cycle can pass through it. TSP's decision version inherits hardness in one line — weight 1 inside G, weight 2 outside, budget |V| — and widening the outside weight to ρ|V| + 1 upgrades the reduction into a proof that general TSP has no constant-factor approximation unless P = NP. Optimisation TSP is NP-hard, not NP-complete. Metric TSP and Euclidean TSP are different problems with much better approximation stories, and §22 tells all three apart.

14 Subset sum, partition, knapsack — and pseudo-polynomial time

SUBSET-SUM asks, given a set S of positive integers and a target T, whether some subset of S sums to exactly T; it is NP-complete, and the reduction from 3-SAT writes each number as a string of decimal digits with one column per variable and one column per clause, arranged so that no column can ever carry — which turns "does a subset sum to T?" into "is there a consistent choice of one polarity per variable that lights up every clause?". It is the most beautiful gadget in the catalogue and also the one that most repays care, because SUBSET-SUM has a dynamic program running in O(nT) time, and understanding exactly why that does not put it in P is the single most useful thing on this page. T is written in ⌈log₂(T + 1)⌉ bits, so O(nT) is exponential in the length of the input — pseudo-polynomial, not polynomial. Everything §02 said about encodings is cashed in here.

the three problems of this sectionSUBSET-SUM = { ⟨S, T⟩ : S a finite set of positive integers, and some S′ ⊆ S has ∑_{s ∈ S′} s = T } NP-COMPLETE PARTITION = { ⟨S⟩ : S can be split into two parts of EQUAL sum } NP-complete — the special case T = (∑S)/2, and a reduction back KNAPSACK = { ⟨w, v, W, V⟩ : some subset of items has total weight ≤ W and total value ≥ V } NP-complete (the 0-1, decision version) all three are in NP: the certificate is the subset; the verifier adds up at most n numbers of at most L bits each — O(nL) bit operations, polynomial in the input length. the OPTIMISATION knapsack ("maximise value subject to weight ≤ W") is NP-HARD and not NP-complete, for the reason §07 and §12 both give: it is not a decision problem.

3-SAT ≤ₚ SUBSET-SUM: the digit-column gadget

the constructionGIVEN φ in 3-CNF with n variables x₁ … x_n and m clauses C₁ … C_m, no clause containing both a variable and its negation BUILD numbers written in BASE 10 with n + m digit columns: one column per VARIABLE, then one column per CLAUSE for each variable xᵢ, TWO numbers: vᵢ : 1 in column xᵢ; 1 in column Cⱼ for each clause containing the literal xᵢ "set xᵢ TRUE" vᵢ′ : 1 in column xᵢ; 1 in column Cⱼ for each clause containing the literal ¬xᵢ "set xᵢ FALSE" for each clause Cⱼ, TWO slack numbers: sⱼ : 1 in column Cⱼ, 0 everywhere else sⱼ′ : 2 in column Cⱼ, 0 everywhere else the TARGET T : 1 in every variable column, 4 in every clause column 2n + 2m numbers of n + m digits each — O((n + m)²) digits in all, so the construction is polynomial. WHY BASE 10 AND WHY 4 a variable column receives 1 from vᵢ and 1 from vᵢ′, so its total over ALL numbers is at most 2. a clause column receives at most 3 from literal numbers (a clause has 3 literals) plus 1 + 2 = 3 from its two slacks, so its total is at most 6. 6 < 10, so NO COLUMN CAN EVER CARRY into the next, and the addition is n + m INDEPENDENT one-digit sums. That is the whole trick. the target digit 1 in a variable column forces EXACTLY ONE of vᵢ, vᵢ′ — a consistent assignment, never both polarities. the target digit 4 in a clause column: the literal numbers contribute L ∈ {0,1,2,3}, the slacks contribute 0, 1, 2 or 3. 4 is reachable ⟺ L ≥ 1 ⟺ the clause has a TRUE literal. (L = 0 would need 4 from slacks, and 1 + 2 = 3 is the most they give.)

Both directions follow immediately from the no-carry property. If φ has a satisfying assignment, take vᵢ when xᵢ is true and vᵢ′ when it is false; each variable column then sums to 1 ✓, and each clause column receives L ∈ {1, 2, 3} from the literal numbers, so add the slacks {sⱼ, sⱼ′}, {sⱼ′} or {sⱼ} to top it up to exactly 4 ✓. Conversely, if a subset sums to T, then because no column carries, every column matches independently; the variable columns force exactly one of each vᵢ, vᵢ′, giving a well-defined assignment, and each clause column's 4 cannot be reached from slacks alone, so at least one literal number contributes — i.e. each clause has a true literal. The assignment satisfies φ.

The table for φ, and its verification
