With φ = (x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ ¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) ∧ (x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) there are n = 3 variable columns and m = 3 clause columns, so 2n + 2m = 12 numbers of 6 digits and a target of 111444.

numbermeaningx₁ x₂ x₃ | C₁ C₂ C₃value
v₁x₁ true — x₁ occurs in C₁, C₃1 0 0 | 1 0 1100101
v₁′x₁ false — ¬x₁ occurs in C₂1 0 0 | 0 1 0100010
v₂x₂ true — x₂ occurs in C₂, C₃0 1 0 | 0 1 1010011
v₂′x₂ false — ¬x₂ occurs in C₁0 1 0 | 1 0 0010100
v₃x₃ true — x₃ occurs in C₂, C₃0 0 1 | 0 1 1001011
v₃′x₃ false — ¬x₃ occurs in C₁0 0 1 | 1 0 0001100
s₁ , s₁′slack for C₁0 0 0 | 1 0 0 and 0 0 0 | 2 0 0000100, 000200
s₂ , s₂′slack for C₂0 0 0 | 0 1 0 and 0 0 0 | 0 2 0000010, 000020
s₃ , s₃′slack for C₃0 0 0 | 0 0 1 and 0 0 0 | 0 0 2000001, 000002
target Tone per variable, four per clause1 1 1 | 4 4 4111444

Enumerating all 2¹² = 4 096 subsets of these twelve numbers finds exactly 5 that sum to 111444, and the assignments they encode — read off as "did the subset take vᵢ or vᵢ′?" — are exactly {001, 010, 101, 110, 111}: the five satisfying assignments of φ that exhaustive search over all 2³ = 8 assignments finds. Not merely the same count, the same set. The correspondence is even a bijection here, because once the assignment is fixed each clause column's shortfall 4 − L is 3, 2 or 1 and is achieved by exactly one subset of {1, 2} — {1,2}, {2}, {1} respectively. The figure recomputes all of this at load and flags the agreement.

Take the solution for the assignment 111 and check it by hand: v₁ + v₂ + v₃ + s₁ + s₁′ + s₂′ + s₃ = 100101 + 010011 + 001011 + 000100 + 000200 + 000020 + 000001. Variable columns: 1, 1, 1 ✓. Clause column C₁: v₁ gives 1 (the literal x₁ is in C₁ and is true), v₂ and v₃ give 0, slacks give 1 + 2 = 3; total 4 ✓. Column C₂: v₂ and v₃ each give 1, slack s₂′ gives 2; total 4 ✓. Column C₃: all three literal numbers give 1, slack s₃ gives 1; total 4 ✓. Sum = 111444, no carries anywhere.

Down the chain: SUBSET-SUM ≤ₚ PARTITION ≤ₚ KNAPSACK

SUBSET-SUM ≤ₚ PARTITIONGIVEN ⟨S, T⟩ with σ = ∑S and 0 ≤ T ≤ σ BUILD S⁺ = S ∪ { a, b } with a = 2σ − T and b = σ + T so ∑S⁺ = σ + (2σ − T) + (σ + T) = 4σ, and each half must sum to 2σ CORRECT a + b = 3σ > 2σ (for σ > 0), so no half contains BOTH; and a half with NEITHER sums to at most σ < 2σ, so every half contains EXACTLY ONE. the half containing a needs 2σ − a = T more from S; the half containing b needs 2σ − b = σ − T more from S, i.e. its complement in S sums to T. either way: S⁺ splits evenly ⟺ some subset of S sums to T. CHECK S = {3, 7, 11, 14, 19, 23}, T = 37, σ = 77 ⟹ a = 117, b = 114, ∑S⁺ = 308, half = 154. measured: 6 subsets of S⁺ sum to 154, i.e. 3 even partitions, each counted once from each side — the 3 subset-sum solutions paired with a ({14,23,117}, {3,11,23,117}, {7,11,19,117}), and as the other half of each, the rest of S paired with b. SUBSET-SUM ≤ₚ KNAPSACK (0-1, decision)GIVEN ⟨S, T⟩ BUILD one item per s ∈ S with weight wᵢ = sᵢ and value vᵢ = sᵢ ; capacity W = T ; value bound V = T CORRECT a subset with weight ≤ T and value ≥ T has weight = value = exactly T, since wᵢ = vᵢ for every item. So it sums to T. Conversely such a subset satisfies both bounds. a one-line reduction — which is why knapsack is NP-complete, and why the knapsack DP of the Dynamic Programming page carries the same pseudo-polynomial caveat.

The O(nT) dynamic program, and why it is not polynomial

SUBSET-SUM has an exact algorithm that everyone meets before they meet NP-completeness. Let R[i][t] be true when some subset of the first i numbers sums to t. Then

the recurrence and its costR[0][0] = TRUE, R[0][t] = FALSE for t > 0 R[i][t] = R[i−1][t] ("skip sᵢ") OR R[i−1][t − sᵢ] if t ≥ sᵢ ("take sᵢ") answer = R[n][T] TIME Θ(n·(T+1)) cell evaluations, each O(1) SPACE Θ(T) with one rolling row, or Θ(n·T) bits if the table is kept for traceback measured on S = {3,7,11,14,19,23}, T = 37: 6 × 38 = 228 cells, of which 74 are TRUE; R[6][37] = TRUE, matching the 3 solutions found by enumerating all 2⁶ = 64 subsets. why Θ(nT) is NOT polynomial in the INPUT SIZE — §02's point, cashedthe input ⟨S, T⟩ is a STRING. Its length is |⟨S, T⟩| = ∑ᵢ ⌈log₂(sᵢ + 1)⌉ + ⌈log₂(T + 1)⌉ + separators ≈ L bits for the instance above: 3,7,11,14,19,23 need 2+3+4+4+5+5 = 23 bits and T = 37 needs 6, so L = 29 bits. The table has 228 cells. now DOUBLE the number of bits in T, keeping n fixed: T ↦ about T², the table's area is multiplied by T. T = 37 (6 bits) → 228 cells T = 1 000 (10 bits) → 6 006 cells T = 10⁶ (20 bits) → 6 000 006 cells T = 10¹⁸ (60 bits) → 6 × 10¹⁸ cells — a 60-bit number fits in one machine word and the table does not fit in the universe. Θ(nT) = Θ(n·2^(bits of T)) — LINEAR in the numeric VALUE, EXPONENTIAL in the number of DIGITS. That is what PSEUDO-POLYNOMIAL means, and it is the whole of it. a polynomial-time algorithm must be polynomial in L. An O(nT) algorithm is polynomial in L only if T is bounded by a polynomial in L, i.e. only if the numbers are SMALL.

The figure makes this visible by sliding the target's magnitude and plotting two curves from the same measured cell counts: area against T, which is a straight line, and area against ⌈log₂(T + 1)⌉, the number of bits actually written down, which is a doubling staircase. One picture, two axes, and the whole of pseudo-polynomiality.

Three consequences follow, and each is a place where the distinction earns its keep:

  • SUBSET-SUM in unary is in P. Write the numbers as strings of tally marks and the input length becomes Θ(∑sᵢ + T), so Θ(nT) is polynomial in that length. The problem did not change; the encoding did. This is not a curiosity — it is the formal content of the phrase "the hardness lives in the magnitudes, not in the combinatorics", and it is exactly why §02 insists that a problem is a language over an alphabet and not an abstract question.
  • SUBSET-SUM is NP-complete but not strongly NP-complete. A problem is strongly NP-complete when it stays NP-complete even if all numbers in the input are bounded by a polynomial in the input length — equivalently, when no pseudo-polynomial algorithm can exist unless P = NP. SUBSET-SUM, PARTITION and 0-1 KNAPSACK are not strongly NP-complete: their O(nT) and O(nW) tables settle them whenever the numbers are small. 3-PARTITION and BIN-PACKING are strongly NP-complete, and general TSP is too, which is the deeper reason §13's gap construction works and why no analogue of the knapsack table exists for TSP.
  • Knapsack has an FPTAS precisely because it is only weakly NP-hard. Rounding the values divides them by a scale factor, which shrinks V — the axis the table is indexed by — and turns the pseudo-polynomial table into a genuinely polynomial one at the cost of a controlled error. §25 builds it. A strongly NP-hard optimisation problem with a polynomially bounded objective cannot have an FPTAS unless P = NP, and that is the same observation read backwards.

The Dynamic Programming page develops the same table as a design exercise — the state, the transition, the traceback, the rolling-row space optimisation — and its subset-DP section gives the other exponential route, O(2ⁿ·n) over subsets, which is better than O(nT) when the numbers are huge and the set is small. Neither is polynomial; they are exponential in different parameters, and choosing between them is the first and cheapest form of "coping" on the list in §19. There is also the meet-in-the-middle algorithm, which splits S in half, sorts the 2^(n/2) subset sums of each half and matches them, running in O(2^(n/2)·n) time and O(2^(n/2)) space — still exponential, but with the exponent halved, and enough to settle n ≈ 60 instances that both other methods choke on.

The subset-sum table filling cell by cell, with the target's magnitude on a slider so the table's area can be plotted against T and against T's bit length at the same time green cell = reachable, grey = not; the highlighted trail is the traceback of one solution; the two small plots below measure the table's area against the numeric target (a straight line) and against the number of bits the target is written in (a doubling staircase) — the same measurements, two axes
The sentence to remember, and the one to never write. Write: "SUBSET-SUM has an O(nT) dynamic program, which is pseudo-polynomial — polynomial in the numeric value of T but exponential in the number of bits T is written in, and therefore not a polynomial-time algorithm." Never write: "SUBSET-SUM is solvable in polynomial time by dynamic programming, so P = NP" — a claim that has been submitted in earnest more than once, and whose error is entirely contained in §02. The same caveat attaches to knapsack's O(nW), to the O(V·W) "pseudo-polynomial" scheduling tables, and to every algorithm whose running time mentions a number from the input rather than the length of the input.

15 Graph colouring: 3-colouring is hard, 2-colouring is easy

A proper k-colouring of a graph assigns one of k colours to each vertex so that no edge joins two vertices of the same colour; deciding whether a proper 2-colouring exists is exactly deciding bipartiteness and takes O(V + E), while deciding whether a proper 3-colouring exists is NP-complete. Nothing about the statement changes between k = 2 and k = 3. What changes is that a 2-colouring is forced: fix one vertex's colour and every other vertex in its component is determined, so a single traversal either succeeds or exhibits an odd cycle. With three colours nothing is forced — each vertex left un-coloured has two options after a neighbour is fixed — and the choices interact globally. The reduction from 3-SAT makes that interaction do arithmetic: a palette triangle names TRUE, FALSE and BASE, a two-vertex gadget per variable makes a literal and its negation take opposite truth colours, and a pair of OR-gadgets per clause forces at least one of its three literals to be TRUE.

the problem, and the boundary inside itk-COLOURING = { ⟨G⟩ : the vertices of G can be coloured with k colours so that adjacent vertices differ } the CHROMATIC NUMBER χ(G) is the least such k. ω(G) ≤ χ(G) ≤ Δ(G) + 1 (ω = clique number, Δ = maximum degree; the upper bound is greedy, §below) Brooks' theorem: χ ≤ Δ unless G is a complete graph or an odd cycle k = 1 trivial: a proper 1-colouring exists ⟺ G has no edges O(1) once |E| is known k = 2 in P: a proper 2-colouring exists ⟺ G is BIPARTITE ⟺ G has no ODD CYCLE O(V + E) by one BFS or DFS k = 3 NP-COMPLETE, and it stays NP-complete for PLANAR graphs and for graphs of maximum degree 4 k ≥ 3 NP-complete for every fixed k ≥ 3 k = 4 on PLANAR graphs: always YES — the four colour theorem — so PLANAR-4-COLOURING is trivially in P while PLANAR-3-COLOURING is NP-complete. 3-COLOURING ∈ NP: the certificate is the colouring itself; the verifier checks |E| edges. O(E). 2-colouring is forced, and that is the whole reason it is easyBFS from any unvisited vertex s, colouring s with 0 and every newly discovered vertex with the opposite of its discoverer. INVARIANT when a vertex v is first coloured, its colour is determined by the parity of its BFS distance from s: colour(v) = dist(s, v) mod 2. so the colouring of each connected component is unique up to swapping the two colours — there are exactly 2^(#components) proper 2-colourings, or none. FAILURE an edge (u, v) with colour(u) = colour(v) is found ⟺ dist(s,u) and dist(s,v) have the same parity ⟺ an ODD CYCLE exists through u and v. COST every edge examined at most twice (once from each endpoint) plus one verification pass: Θ(V + E). No search, no backtracking, no choice ever made. with THREE colours the invariant dies: fixing one vertex leaves two colours for each neighbour, the choices are not forced, and the constraint propagates globally instead of locally.

3-SAT ≤ₚ 3-COLOURING: palette, variables, OR-gadgets

the constructionGIVEN φ in 3-CNF, n variables, m clauses. Call the three colours T, F, B. BUILD PALETTE three mutually adjacent vertices T — F — B — T. A triangle, so in any proper 3-colouring they take all three colours; NAME the colours by them: whatever colour the vertex T receives IS "true", whatever F receives IS "false". 3 vertices, 3 edges VARIABLE GADGET for each xᵢ, two vertices xᵢ and ¬xᵢ, with edges xᵢ — ¬xᵢ , xᵢ — B , ¬xᵢ — B. the three edges make {xᵢ, ¬xᵢ, B} a triangle, so xᵢ and ¬xᵢ get the two colours OTHER than B's — i.e. one is T-coloured and the other F-coloured. a proper colouring therefore IS a truth assignment, and vice versa. 2 vertices, 3 edges per variable OR-GADGET OR(u, v) on two "signal" vertices u, v introduces three new vertices p, q, r with edges p — q , q — r , r — p (a triangle: p, q, r take all three colours) u — p , v — q (the inputs) r — B (so the OUTPUT r is T-coloured or F-coloured, never B) 3 vertices, 6 edges LEMMA (verified in the figure by enumerating all 3³ colourings of (p, q, r) under each of the four input patterns — exactly 2 are proper in each): u F, v F ⟹ r is FORCED F-coloured (2 proper completions, both with r = F) u T, v F or u F, v T ⟹ r may be either (2 proper completions, one with r = T and one with r = F) u T, v T ⟹ r is FORCED T-coloured (2 proper completions, both with r = T) the reduction uses two things from it. 3-colourable ⟹ satisfiable needs the first line, via its contrapositive: r T-coloured ⟹ at least one input is T-coloured. Satisfiable ⟹ 3-colourable needs the other lines: whenever at least one input is T-coloured, r CAN be T-coloured. CLAUSE GADGET for Cⱼ = (a ∨ b ∨ c): r₁ = OR(a, b); r₂ = OR(r₁, c); then add the edge r₂ — F. r₂ is adjacent to B (from the OR-gadget) and to F, so r₂ is FORCED to be T-coloured — which by the lemma forces a ∨ b ∨ c. 6 vertices, 13 edges per clause SIZE |V′| = 3 + 2n + 6m |E′| = 3 + 3n + 13m built in O(n + m) time. Polynomial. FOR φ (n = 3, m = 3): 27 vertices, 51 edges — measured by the figure, and 3-colourable, with the assignment read back out of the colouring. FOR φ₈ (n = 3, m = 8, UNSATISFIABLE): 57 vertices, 116 edges — and the figure's backtracking search proves it NOT 3-colourable after 61 264 search nodes.

Both directions again. Satisfiable ⟹ 3-colourable: colour the palette with the three colours, colour each literal vertex by the assignment (true literals get T's colour, false literals get F's colour) — legal because xᵢ and ¬xᵢ differ and neither is B-coloured — and then, clause by clause, use the lemma to give each OR-gadget's output the T colour, which is possible because each clause has a true literal; the internal p, q, r of each gadget can always be completed. 3-colourable ⟹ satisfiable: read the assignment off the literal vertices, which is consistent because xᵢ and ¬xᵢ are adjacent and both avoid B's colour. Each clause's r₂ is adjacent to both B and F, so it holds T's colour; by the contrapositive of the lemma its inputs are not both F-coloured, and following that back through the two gadgets gives at least one true literal in the clause.

The lemma is the only step with any content, and it is small enough to verify by hand as well as by machine. Suppose u and v are both F-coloured. Then p ≠ F and q ≠ F, and since {p, q, r} is a triangle it uses all three colours, so {p, q} = {T, B} and r = F: the output is forced false. Suppose instead u is T-coloured and v is F-coloured. Setting q = B (allowed: q ≠ F) and p = F (allowed: p ≠ T) leaves r = T: the output can be true, and taking p = B, q = T instead leaves r = F, so in the mixed case the output is free. The case u false, v true is the mirror image. And if both inputs are T-coloured then p ≠ T and q ≠ T, so the triangle leaves only T for r — the output is forced true, which is also harmless. The figure enumerates all 3³ = 27 colourings of a single OR-gadget's three internal vertices under each of the four input patterns and prints the counts: exactly 2 are proper in every case.

The demonstration graph

The figure's default instance gCol has 7 vertices and the 10 edges 01, 12, 20, 23, 34, 45, 53, 15, 06, 64. Its measured properties:

questionanswerhow the figure establishes itmeasured cost
bipartite? (2-colourable?)noBFS colouring finds the edge 12 joining two same-parity vertices — the triangle 0-1-2violation found after 5 adjacency examinations; a full scan of the graph is 2|E| + |E| = 30
proper 2-colourings0 of 2⁷ = 128exhaustive enumeration, as a cross-check on the linear test128 assignments, 10 edge checks each at worst
proper 3-colourings24 of 3⁷ = 2 187exhaustive enumeration2 187 assignments
chromatic number30 proper 2-colourings and 24 proper 3-colourings128 + 2 187 assignments
proper 4-colourings744 of 4⁷ = 16 384exhaustive enumeration (the chromatic polynomial evaluated at 4)16 384 assignments

The two costs in the first row are the point of the section. The linear test answers "not bipartite" after five adjacency examinations, and would answer "bipartite" after a complete Θ(V + E) scan; the 3-colouring question on the same seven vertices required 2 187 assignments to answer by enumeration. At n = 7 the ratio is a few hundred; at n = 30 it is 3³⁰/90 ≈ 2 × 10¹². The figure plots both counts as n grows and the curves separate immediately.

The count 24 deserves a moment. There are 3! = 6 ways to permute the three colours, and every proper colouring's permutations are also proper, so the 24 colourings fall into 24/6 = 4 classes under colour renaming — the graph has four essentially different 3-colourings. Any count of proper colourings is divisible by k! divided by the symmetry of unused colours; here all three colours are used in every one of the 24, so the divisor is exactly 6. This is also why practical colouring solvers fix the colours of a clique at the start: it kills the symmetry and shrinks the search space by a factor of k! before the search begins.

Greedy colouring, and why it is not an approximation

The obvious heuristic: take the vertices in some order and give each one the smallest colour not already used by a coloured neighbour. It runs in O(V + E) and it never uses more than Δ + 1 colours, because when a vertex is reached at most Δ of its neighbours are coloured and one of the first Δ + 1 colours must be free. That is a genuine bound and it is worth having. What it is not is an approximation guarantee.

greedy's failure, on a family where the gap is unboundedthe CROWN graph on 2n vertices: parts {u₁…u_n} and {w₁…w_n}; join uᵢ — wⱼ for every i ≠ j. (K_{n,n} with a perfect matching removed) it is BIPARTITE, so χ = 2. greedy in the INTERLEAVED order u₁, w₁, u₂, w₂, …, u_n, w_n uses n colours — MEASURED in the figure for n = 3, 4, 5: 3, 4, 5 colours against χ = 2 every time. u₁ gets colour 0; w₁ is not adjacent to u₁ (the matching edge was removed) so it also gets 0; u₂ is adjacent to w₁ (colour 0) so it gets 1; w₂ is adjacent to u₁ (colour 0) so it gets 1; … the pattern continues, one new colour per pair. so the RATIO greedy / χ is n/2 — UNBOUNDED as n grows. Greedy is not a ρ-approximation for any constant ρ. (the crown graph is also the standard example that greedy is order-dependent: the order u₁…u_n, w₁…w_n on the SAME graph uses 2 colours.)

The failure is not fixable by a cleverer ordering rule, and the reason is a theorem rather than a lack of imagination: unless P = NP, no polynomial-time algorithm approximates the chromatic number within n^(1−ε) for any fixed ε > 0. Colouring is one of the problems where the honest advice is that approximation does not help either, and the routes that remain are the exact-exponential ones of §26, the parameterised ones of §27 when the graph has small treewidth, and the solvers of §30. Good orderings — largest-degree-first, DSATUR, smallest-last — are still worth using, because they are cheap and they do well on the graphs that arise in practice; they simply carry no guarantee. This is the pattern Greedy Algorithms §27 describes in general: a greedy rule with no matroid or exchange argument behind it is a heuristic, and calling it an approximation requires a proof it does not have.

Where colouring comes from

  • Register allocation. A compiler builds an interference graph — a vertex per live value, an edge between two values that are live at the same time — and a proper k-colouring with k = the number of machine registers is exactly an assignment of values to registers with no two simultaneously-live values sharing one. The problem is NP-complete in general, so real allocators use Chaitin-style graph colouring with heuristic simplification and spilling: when no vertex of degree < k remains, some value is written to memory and removed from the graph. The spill is what "the problem is NP-complete" looks like in a production compiler — not failure, but a fallback that costs performance rather than correctness.
  • Scheduling and frequency assignment. Exams that share students, jobs that share a machine, radio transmitters whose ranges overlap — each is a conflict graph whose chromatic number is the number of time slots or frequencies needed. Timetabling systems solve these with the same mixture of heuristics, local search and integer programming that §30 describes.
  • Sudoku is a 9-colouring. The 81 cells are vertices, joined when they share a row, a column or a box; the givens are pre-coloured vertices. Generalised n²×n² Sudoku is NP-complete, which is a fact about the family, not about the 9×9 puzzles in a newspaper — those have 81 vertices and are solved instantly by constraint propagation.
  • Bipartiteness is checked far more often than colourability. Two-colouring is the test behind "is this graph a valid bipartite matching instance?", behind detecting odd cycles in consistency checks, and behind the parity arguments in Graph Representations & Traversal, §14, which gives the BFS version in full. The gap between §14 there and this section is the gap between k = 2 and k = 3, and it is the whole subject in miniature.
Colouring the same graph two ways: the linear bipartiteness test on the left, exhaustive k-colouring search on the right, with both operation counts measured left: the BFS 2-colouring, with the odd-cycle edge highlighted when the test fails, and its adjacency examinations counted; right: the proper k-colouring found by enumeration, with the number of assignments tried and proper colourings found; the plot compares the two measured counts as the graph grows
The pattern to take away. The jump from 2 to 3 is not about the number of colours; it is about whether the constraint propagates deterministically. With two colours, fixing one vertex determines its whole component, so the algorithm is a traversal. With three, each step leaves a free choice, choices interact across the whole graph, and the reduction of this section shows that those interactions are expressive enough to encode arbitrary Boolean satisfiability. The same pattern explains 2-SAT versus 3-SAT (§16), matching versus 3-dimensional matching, and unit propagation on Horn clauses (Greedy Algorithms, §24) versus general SAT. When you meet a new problem, the useful question is not "how big is the search space" — it is always exponential — but "does fixing one piece force the next?".

16 Where the boundary runs

The boundary between P and the NP-complete problems does not run between problems that look different; it runs between problems that look almost identical, and in every case the crossing is caused by one structural property collapsing. Two literals per clause is in P and three is NP-complete. Two colours is in P and three is NP-complete. Every edge once is in P and every vertex once is NP-complete. Shortest path is in P and longest simple path is NP-hard. Matching in two dimensions is in P and in three dimensions it is NP-complete. A spanning tree is in P and a Steiner tree is NP-hard. Real-valued linear programming is in P and integer-valued linear programming is NP-complete. Knapsack with unary numbers is in P and with binary numbers it is NP-complete. This section is the table of those pairs with the reason in each, because "which side of this line is my problem on?" is the question the rest of the page exists to answer, and the pairs are the fastest way to learn to see it.

the algorithm on the easy side of the first pair, in full, because the whole section turns on why it exists2-SAT, in O(n + m): every clause (a ∨ b) is the PAIR of implications ¬a → b and ¬b → a — falsify one literal and the other is FORCED build the IMPLICATION GRAPH on the 2n literal vertices { x₁, ¬x₁, …, x_n, ¬x_n }: 2m arcs compute its strongly connected components O(n + m) — the method is on the Graph Representations & Traversal page UNSATISFIABLE ⟺ some variable has xᵢ and ¬xᵢ in the SAME component (each would then imply the other, so each implies its own negation) otherwise, set xᵢ TRUE when comp(xᵢ) comes EARLIER than comp(¬xᵢ) in reverse topological order (equivalently, LATER in topological order — the order Tarjan's algorithm emits components in is the reverse one) — always a satisfying assignment a 3-clause (a ∨ b ∨ c) is NOT a set of implications between single literals. The best it gives is ¬a ∧ ¬b → c, a TWO-premise rule, and no digraph on literals expresses it. Nothing is forced until two of the three literals are already decided — so there is a CHOICE, and choices compose multiplicatively. the three mechanisms that recur down the third column of the tableFORCING fixing one piece of the solution DETERMINES the next → the algorithm is a propagation, not a search 2-SAT · 2-colouring · Horn-SAT · unit propagation LOCAL CERTIFICATE "no small local improvement exists" IMPLIES globally optimal → a polynomial local search is CORRECT Berge's lemma (matching) · the cut property (MST) · convexity (LP) · the relaxation conditions (shortest paths) DECOMPOSITION sub-problems are independent and their answers ADD or COMPOSE → a polynomial-state dynamic program exists shortest paths · Euler's degree condition · interval scheduling on the hard side of every pair, at least one of the three is absent — and the reduction that proves the hardness is, every time, a construction that exploits exactly the missing one to encode a Boolean variable and an OR.
easy side (in P)hard sidethe structural property that collapses
2-SAT — O(n + m) by the implication graph and its strongly connected components3-SAT — NP-complete (§09)A 2-clause (a ∨ b) is two implications, ¬a → b and ¬b → a: falsifying one literal forces the other. The whole formula becomes a digraph on 2n literal vertices, and it is satisfiable iff no variable has xᵢ and ¬xᵢ in the same SCC. A 3-clause gives only ¬a ∧ ¬b → c, a two-premise implication that no digraph expresses — nothing is forced until two literals are already decided, so there is a choice, and choices compose into a search.
2-colouring — O(V + E) by BFS parity (§15)3-colouring — NP-completeSame collapse, geometrically. Fixing one vertex's colour determines every vertex in its component, so the algorithm is a traversal with no branching. With three colours each neighbour has two remaining options and the constraints interact globally. 4-colouring planar graphs is trivially yes (the four colour theorem) while 3-colouring planar graphs is still NP-complete.
Euler circuit — O(V + E): all degrees even, plus connectivity; Hierholzer builds it in O(E)Hamiltonian cycle — NP-complete (§13)"Use every edge once" is a local, additive condition — a walk enters and leaves a vertex in pairs, so parity of degree is necessary and sufficient. "Visit every vertex once" has no per-vertex quantity: whether a vertex can be visited once depends on which neighbours the rest of the tour already consumed. Additivity over vertices is what dies.
Shortest path — Dijkstra, Bellman-Ford (Shortest Paths, MST & Flow, §04, §07)Longest simple path — NP-hard; its decision version NP-completeOptimal substructure. A subpath of a shortest path is a shortest path between its endpoints, so relaxation composes. A subpath of a longest simple path is not a longest simple path — extending it may revisit a vertex — so the sub-problems are not independent and no relaxation argument exists. §03 of that page makes exactly this hand-off, and Dynamic Programming, §02 gives the cut-and-paste proof that fails here. Note the same collapse is what makes shortest paths with negative cycles hard: "shortest simple path" is the longest-path problem in disguise.
Bipartite matching — Hopcroft–Karp O(E√V); general graphs by Edmonds' blossom algorithm in polynomial time (§25 there)3-dimensional matching — NP-completeMatching in two parts has augmenting paths: a matching is maximum iff no augmenting path exists (Berge's lemma), and one search finds one or proves there is none. With three parts there is no augmenting-path characterisation — a local improvement may require re-arranging an unbounded number of triples — so the certificate of optimality disappears and with it the algorithm.
Minimum spanning tree — Kruskal/Prim, O(E log V) (§16 there)Steiner tree — NP-hard; decision version NP-completeMST must connect all vertices, so the cut property holds: the lightest edge across any cut is in some optimum, and greed is safe (this is the matroid argument of Greedy Algorithms, §17). Steiner tree must connect a subset of terminals and may use any of the others as junctions, so which non-terminals to include is a choice, the cut property no longer applies, and the greedy exchange argument fails. Metric Steiner tree does have a 2-approximation, from the MST on the terminals.
Linear programming — polynomial: ellipsoid (1979), interior point (1984); simplex is exponential in the worst case but excellent in practiceInteger linear programming — NP-complete (0-1 ILP is one of Karp's 21)The feasible region of an LP is a convex polytope, and a linear objective attains its optimum at a vertex; convexity means local optimality implies global optimality, so a hill-climb terminates correctly. Requiring integer coordinates deletes the interior of the polytope and leaves a lattice with no convexity, so no local criterion certifies a global optimum. Note the asymmetry: LP's polynomial-time status came 30 years after the simplex method, and the practical algorithm is still the exponential-worst-case one.
Knapsack with unary numbers — O(nW), which is polynomial when W is written in W tally marksKnapsack with binary numbers — NP-complete (§14)Nothing about the combinatorics changes at all — only the encoding. In binary, W takes ⌈log₂(W+1)⌉ bits and O(nW) is exponential in the input length. This pair is the cleanest possible demonstration that "polynomial time" is a statement about the input string (§02), and the reason the phrase "pseudo-polynomial" exists.
Horn-SAT — O(n + m) by unit propagation, and the minimal model it finds is uniqueGeneral SAT — NP-completeA Horn clause has at most one positive literal, so it reads as an implication a ∧ b ∧ … → c with a single conclusion. Unit propagation is then forced: a clause whose premises are all derived must fire, and firing never has to be undone, because the algorithm only ever sets variables to true and the minimal model is unique. Drop the "at most one positive literal" restriction and a clause with two positive literals offers a choice of which to satisfy. Greedy Algorithms, §24 proves the algorithm and its minimality; there is no need to repeat it here.
2-SAT, again — deciding satisfiabilityMAX-2-SAT — maximising satisfied clauses: NP-hardThe sharpest pair on the list, because both sides are 2-SAT. Deciding "can all clauses be satisfied?" is linear; deciding "can at least k clauses be satisfied?" is NP-complete. The implication graph answers an all-or-nothing question; it says nothing about how to trade one violated clause against another. Optimising a problem whose feasibility question is easy is not automatically easy — a warning that applies to every "easy side" entry above.
Primality — deterministic polynomial time (AKS, 2002; refined variants run in Õ(log⁶ n) bit operations); Miller–Rabin in practiceInteger factorisation — no polynomial algorithm known, and not known to be NP-completeDeciding whether a number has a non-trivial factor and finding one came apart in 2002. Primality is in P; factoring is in NP ∩ co-NP (§17) and its status is genuinely open — it is one of the very few natural problems believed to be neither in P nor NP-complete. It is on this table as a warning that "hard" and "NP-complete" are different claims.

What the pairs have in common

Read down the third column and the same three mechanisms recur.

  • Forcing. On the easy side, fixing one piece of the solution determines the next piece, so the algorithm is a propagation with no branching: 2-SAT's implications, 2-colouring's parity, Horn-SAT's unit propagation. On the hard side there is always a free choice left, and free choices compose multiplicatively.
  • A local certificate of global optimality. Berge's lemma for matching, the cut property for MST, convexity for LP, the relaxation optimality conditions for shortest paths. Each says "if no small local improvement exists, you are globally optimal", which is what makes a polynomial local search correct. Every hard-side entry is a problem where a local optimum need not be global.
  • Additivity or independence of subproblems. Shortest paths decompose; longest simple paths do not. Euler's condition is a sum over vertices; Hamiltonicity is not. This is the same "optimal substructure" property that Dynamic Programming, §02 requires, and its absence is what rules out the obvious dynamic program over polynomially many states.

None of the three is a decision procedure — there is no algorithm that takes a problem statement and reports which side of the line it falls on, and there cannot be one in general. But they are what an experienced person is checking when they look at a new problem for thirty seconds and say "this smells NP-hard". The productive follow-up is to try to build the gadget: if you can encode a Boolean variable and an OR, you are almost certainly done, and the attempt usually either succeeds within an hour or shows you the forcing structure that makes the problem easy.

The costs, measured

The figure takes three of the pairs and runs both algorithms on instances of growing size, plotting the measured operation counts on one logarithmic axis. On the easy side it runs the real polynomial algorithm to completion. On the hard side it runs exhaustive search — every assignment, every colouring, every tour — and the caption is explicit that this is the size of the search space, not the best known algorithm; §26 and §30 show how much pruning buys. Measured at load:

paireasy side, measuredhard side, measuredat the largest size shown

2-colouring vs 3-colouringBFS adjacency examinations + verification pass: 18 → 78 for n = 4 → 12 — linear in V + Eassignments enumerated: 81 → 531 441, i.e. exactly 3ⁿ, with 1.58 million edge checksn = 12: 78 versus 531 441 — a factor of about 6 800
Euler vs Hamiltoniandegree scan plus connectivity walk: 22 → 55 for n = 4 → 10 — linear in V + Etours enumerated: 3 → 181 440, i.e. exactly (n−1)!/2, with 297 000 edge lookupsn = 10: 55 versus 181 440 — a factor of about 3 300

The readout flags each hard-side count against its closed form — 2ⁿ, 3ⁿ, (n−1)!/2 — so the measurement and the formula are visibly the same number, and reports the primitive-operation counts separately, which are not closed forms. The three easy-side curves are straight lines on a linear axis and indistinguishable from the x-axis on the log plot; that visual is the point.

Same-looking pair, different cost: both algorithms of each pair run on instances of growing size, with the measured operation counts on one logarithmic axis solid green = the polynomial algorithm on the easy side of the pair; dashed red = exhaustive search on the hard side; both are real runs under a counter, not formulas — the readout checks each exhaustive count against its closed form and reports the primitive-operation counts as well
How to use this table on a real problem. Find the closest entry. If your problem is on the easy side of a pair, look for the forcing structure or the local certificate, because that is what the polynomial algorithm will be built from — and check that your problem really has it and not merely something that resembles it. If it is on the hard side, do not spend a week on an exact polynomial algorithm; spend an hour on a reduction to confirm the hardness, and then read §19. And if your problem sits between two entries — feasibility easy, optimisation unknown; numbers small, combinatorics large — the parameter that decides it is usually visible in the table's third column, and the right move is often to fix that parameter and reach for §27.

17 co-NP, NP ∩ co-NP, and problems of unknown status

co-NP is the class of languages whose complements are in NP — equivalently, the class of problems whose no-instances have short, efficiently checkable certificates. NP is not symmetric under complement, and the asymmetry is built into the definition: a certificate for "yes, this formula is satisfiable" is one assignment, three lines long; there is no known short certificate for "no, this formula is satisfiable under none of its 2ⁿ assignments". Whether one exists is the question "NP = co-NP?", and it is a different open question from "P = NP?" — related, since P = NP would imply NP = co-NP, but not known to be equivalent. This section makes the asymmetry concrete, names the co-NP-complete problems, and then deals with the genuinely open middle: NP ∩ co-NP, and integer factorisation, which is the single most frequently mis-stated entry in this whole subject.

the definitionsfor a language L ⊆ Σ*, its COMPLEMENT is L̄ = Σ* ∖ L. co-NP = { L : L̄ ∈ NP } "the NO-instances have short certificates" co-P = P P IS closed under complement: run the polynomial decider and flip its answer. That is why the question only arises for NP. SAT ∈ NP certificate for YES: a satisfying assignment. Verified in O(m) literal evaluations. UNSAT ∈ co-NP by definition, being SAT's complement (modulo malformed inputs). No short certificate for YES is known. TAUTOLOGY ∈ co-NP "φ is true under EVERY assignment". Its complement — "some assignment falsifies φ" — is in NP, certified by that assignment. both UNSAT and TAUTOLOGY are co-NP-COMPLETE: every co-NP language reduces to them, by complementing Cook–Levin (§08). the four possible worlds, and what is known about themKNOWN P ⊆ NP ∩ co-NP because P is closed under complement, so P ⊆ NP gives P ⊆ co-NP too KNOWN P = NP ⟹ NP = co-NP if NP = P then NP is closed under complement KNOWN NP ≠ co-NP ⟹ P ≠ NP the contrapositive of the line above — so proving NP ≠ co-NP would settle P vs NP KNOWN if ANY NP-complete problem is in co-NP, then NP = co-NP; if any co-NP-complete problem is in NP, likewise KNOWN if ANY problem in NP ∩ co-NP is NP-complete, then NP = co-NP — the fact that decides the factoring question below OPEN P = NP ? OPEN NP = co-NP ? OPEN P = NP ∩ co-NP ? BELIEVED P ≠ NP, NP ≠ co-NP, and NP ∩ co-NP strictly between P and NP — but none of these is proved, and "no polynomial algorithm is KNOWN" is the only honest phrasing for any of them (§07).

The asymmetry, made concrete

Take the page's formula φ, which is satisfiable, and its unsatisfiable companion φ₈. To convince a sceptic that φ is satisfiable, hand over the assignment x₁ = x₂ = x₃ = TRUE; checking it means evaluating at most 3m = 9 literals and confirming that each of the 3 clauses contains a true one. The check does not depend on how large the search space is; it is linear in the formula. To convince the same sceptic that φ₈ is unsatisfiable, there is nothing to hand over but the search itself: all 2³ = 8 assignments, each rejected. The figure scales this up with a control on n, generating a satisfiable instance (planted around a hidden assignment) and an unsatisfiable one (containing all eight clauses on three of the variables, which is unsatisfiable regardless of the rest) at each size, and measuring both costs.

At n = 20, with 4n = 80 clauses, the verifier does 126 literal evaluations on the yes-instance and the exhaustive search does 2²⁰ = 1 048 576 assignments — 8.9 million literal evaluations — on the no-instance. The ratio grows without bound, and that is the shape of the open question: is the second column necessarily exponential, or is there a certificate for unsatisfiability we have not found? A proof system for unsatisfiability does exist — resolution — but the refutations can be exponentially long: the pigeonhole principle, expressed in CNF, requires resolution refutations of size 2^Ω(n). That is a lower bound for one proof system, not for all of them, and proving super-polynomial lower bounds for strong enough proof systems is exactly the programme that would separate NP from co-NP.

NP ∩ co-NP: the problems with good characterisations

A problem in NP ∩ co-NP has short certificates both ways: whatever the answer, it can be justified in polynomial time. Edmonds called this a "good characterisation", and the striking historical pattern is that problems with good characterisations have, one after another, turned out to be in P — often decades later.

problemyes-certificateno-certificatestatus
Bipartite matching of size kthe matchinga vertex cover of size < k (König's theorem)in P — Hopcroft–Karp, and long before that by augmenting paths
Max-flow value ≥ kthe flowan s-t cut of capacity < k (max-flow min-cut)in P — Edmonds-Karp and successors
Linear programming (decision form)a feasible point with objective ≥ ka dual solution with objective < k (LP duality / Farkas' lemma)in NP ∩ co-NP from duality; proved in P by the ellipsoid method in 1979 — 30 years after the simplex method
PRIMES ("is N prime?")a Pratt certificate — a primitive root plus a recursive certificate of each factor of N − 1 (1975)a non-trivial factor of N, checked by one multiplicationin NP ∩ co-NP since 1975; proved in P by the AKS algorithm in 2002
FACTORING (decision form: "does N have a factor < k?")the factor, plus the full factorisation of N with primality certificatesthe full factorisation, showing every factor is ≥ kin NP ∩ co-NP; status open — no polynomial algorithm known, and not known to be NP-complete

Factoring: the claim to get right

Integer factorisation is not known to be NP-complete, it is not known to be in P, and it is widely believed to be neither. The three statements that matter:

  • It is in NP ∩ co-NP. The decision form "does N have a non-trivial factor less than k?" has a short certificate either way: the complete prime factorisation of N, together with a Pratt certificate of primality for each prime factor, proves both what the factors are and that there are no others, and the whole object is polynomial in log N. This is the sense in which factoring is "certifiable both ways" — and it is only possible because primality itself has short certificates.
  • If it were NP-complete, NP would equal co-NP. That is the general fact stated above: an NP-complete problem in NP ∩ co-NP forces the two classes to coincide, because every NP language then reduces into co-NP. Since NP = co-NP is widely disbelieved, so is the NP-completeness of factoring. This is an inference from a conjecture, not a theorem, and should be stated as such.
  • Its presumed hardness is a cryptographic assumption, not a complexity theorem. RSA's security rests on factoring being hard on average for randomly chosen semiprimes — a much stronger requirement than NP-hardness, which is a worst-case notion. The best known classical algorithm, the general number field sieve, runs in time roughly exp(c·(log N)^(1/3)·(log log N)^(2/3)): sub-exponential in log N, super-polynomial, and nothing like the 2ⁿ of brute force. And Shor's algorithm factors in polynomial time on a quantum computer, which is a statement about BQP and says nothing about P versus NP — no quantum polynomial algorithm for an NP-complete problem is known, and none is expected.

The same three-part story applies, with different details, to discrete logarithm and to graph isomorphism. Graph isomorphism is in NP, is not known to be in P, and is not known to be NP-complete; it is not known to be in co-NP either, but graph NON-isomorphism has a short interactive proof and lies in AM — so graph isomorphism lies in coAM — from which it follows that if graph isomorphism were NP-complete the polynomial hierarchy would collapse to its second level — which is the strongest evidence available that it is not. Babai's 2015 algorithm runs in quasi-polynomial time, exp((log n)^O(1)) — faster than any exponential, slower than any polynomial. These are the natural inhabitants of the region that Ladner's theorem guarantees is non-empty: if P ≠ NP, there exist NP-intermediate languages, in NP but neither in P nor NP-complete. The theorem's construction is artificial; factoring and graph isomorphism are the natural candidates.

Certificate asymmetry: verifying a yes-instance against establishing a no-instance, both counted, with the instance size on a control left bar = literal evaluations to check the supplied satisfying assignment of the yes-instance; right bar = assignments examined and literal evaluations to establish that the no-instance has no satisfying assignment; both are measured runs, and the readout confirms that the yes-instance is satisfiable and the no-instance is not by exhaustive search

What a proof of NP ≠ co-NP would give

It would settle P ≠ NP immediately, by the contrapositive above — and it is, in one sense, the more tractable target, because it is a statement about proof length rather than about algorithms. To show NP ≠ co-NP it suffices to show that TAUTOLOGY has no polynomially-bounded proof system: no way of writing down, for every tautology, a proof that is polynomial in the formula's size and checkable in polynomial time. This reframing, due to Cook and Reckhow, turned the question into proof complexity, and the programme has produced real results — exponential lower bounds for resolution (Haken, 1985), for bounded-depth Frege systems, for cutting planes — each one a proof system knocked out. No super-polynomial lower bound is known for Frege systems or for extended Frege, and those remain the frontier.

Two practical morals for someone who is not going to prove any of this. First, the asymmetry is real and it shapes tools: a SAT solver that reports "satisfiable" hands you an assignment you can check in seconds, while one that reports "unsatisfiable" is asserting something you must trust its search to have done correctly — which is why modern solvers emit resolution proof logs that an independent checker replays, and why those logs are sometimes gigabytes long for instances the solver settled in minutes. Second, when you build a verification system, notice which direction you need: "find me a bug" is an NP-shaped question with a short witness, and "prove there is no bug" is a co-NP-shaped question with no short witness in general. That difference is why bug-finding tools scale and full verification does not.

The three sentences most often got wrong here, corrected. (1) "Factoring is NP-complete" — false as a claim of knowledge; it is not known to be, and if it were, NP = co-NP. (2) "PRIMES was proved to be in NP ∩ co-NP in 2002" — wrong date and wrong result; PRIMES was known to be in NP ∩ co-NP from 1975 (Pratt certificates for the yes side, a factor for the no side), and 2002 is when AKS put it in P. (3) "NP = co-NP is the same question as P = NP" — false; one implication is known (P = NP ⟹ NP = co-NP) and the converse is open, so a world with P ≠ NP and NP = co-NP is not ruled out by anything currently proved.

18 Beyond NP: PSPACE, EXPTIME, and the undecidable

NP is not the top of the hierarchy, and it is not even the place where the first proved separation happens: the time hierarchy theorem establishes that P ⊊ EXPTIME unconditionally, which means that at least one of the inclusions P ⊆ NP ⊆ PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME is strict — we simply do not know which. Above NP sit PSPACE, whose complete problems are quantified Boolean formulas and two-player games of polynomially bounded length; EXPTIME, whose complete problems include generalised chess and Go on an n × n board; and above everything computable, the undecidable problems, of which the halting problem is the root and Rice's theorem is the generalisation that makes program analysis permanently approximate. This section draws the containment diagram, marks exactly which inclusions are proved strict and which are merely believed, and is careful about the difference throughout, because this is the part of the subject where confident-sounding false statements are most common.

the classes, by the resource they boundL deterministic LOGARITHMIC work space (the input is read-only and not counted) e.g. undirected s-t connectivity (Reingold, 2004) NL nondeterministic logarithmic space e.g. DIRECTED s-t connectivity, which is NL-complete P deterministic polynomial TIME §04 NP nondeterministic polynomial time = polynomial-time VERIFIABLE §05 co-NP complements of NP languages §17 PH the POLYNOMIAL HIERARCHY: Σ₀ᵖ = Π₀ᵖ = P, Σ₁ᵖ = NP, Π₁ᵖ = co-NP, Σₖ₊₁ᵖ = NP^(Σₖᵖ), PH = ⋃ₖ Σₖᵖ PSPACE deterministic polynomial SPACE, with no bound on time EXPTIME deterministic time 2^(n^O(1)) NEXPTIME nondeterministic time 2^(n^O(1)) EXPSPACE deterministic space 2^(n^O(1)) R the decidable languages — those with a Turing machine that halts on every input RE the recursively enumerable languages — a machine that halts and accepts on YES, and may run forever on NO. HALT is RE-complete, and NOT in R. what is KNOWN, and what is only BELIEVED — the distinction this section exists forPROVED, as inclusions: L ⊆ NL ⊆ P ⊆ NP ⊆ PH ⊆ PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME ⊆ NEXPTIME ⊆ EXPSPACE ⊆ R ⊂ RE PROVED, as EQUALITIES: PSPACE = NPSPACE (Savitch, 1970: nondeterminism costs only a SQUARE in space) NL = co-NL (Immerman–Szelepcsényi, 1987: nondeterministic SPACE classes ARE closed under complement, unlike NP) NL ⊆ P (the configuration graph of a log-space machine has polynomially many nodes; reachability on it is in P) PROVED, as STRICT separations (all from the hierarchy theorems — diagonalisation): L ⊊ PSPACE space hierarchy theorem NL ⊊ PSPACE Savitch puts NL inside SPACE(log²n), and the space hierarchy theorem gives SPACE(log²n) ⊊ PSPACE P ⊊ EXPTIME TIME hierarchy theorem — DTIME(f(n)) ⊊ DTIME(g(n)) whenever f(n)·log f(n) = o(g(n)), g time-constructible NP ⊊ NEXPTIME nondeterministic time hierarchy theorem PSPACE ⊊ EXPSPACE space hierarchy theorem OPEN, every one of them: L = P ? NL = P ? P = NP ? NP = co-NP ? NP = PSPACE ? P = PSPACE ? PH = PSPACE ? PSPACE = EXPTIME ? CONSEQUENCE of P ⊊ EXPTIME: since P ⊆ NP ⊆ PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME, AT LEAST ONE of those three inclusions is STRICT. Nobody knows which. This is the sharpest honest statement about how little is proved: we know a separation exists somewhere in that chain and cannot locate it.

The time hierarchy theorem: the separations we do have

Almost nothing is proved about the classes in this subject, and it is worth knowing where the exceptions come from. They come from diagonalisation, the same argument Cantor used on the reals and Turing used on the halting problem. Sketch, for time: build a machine D that, on input ⟨M⟩, simulates M on ⟨M⟩ for f(n) steps and then outputs the opposite of whatever M did. D runs in time about f(n)·log f(n) — the logarithmic overhead is the cost of a universal machine simulating an arbitrary machine with an arbitrary alphabet and tape count. And D's language cannot be decided in time f(n) by any machine, since any such machine M disagrees with D on the input ⟨M⟩. The machine M being simulated contributes its own constant factor, so the separation needs a little slack beyond f·log f: hence DTIME(f(n)) ⊊ DTIME(g(n)) whenever f(n)·log f(n) = o(g(n)) and g is time-constructible — for instance g = f·log²f — and taking f(n) = n^k against 2ⁿ gives P ⊊ EXPTIME.

Why does the same argument not separate P from NP? Because it relativises: it works unchanged if every machine is given access to an oracle, and Baker, Gill and Solovay showed in 1975 that there are oracles A with Pᴬ = NPᴬ and oracles B with Pᴮ ≠ NPᴮ. Any proof technique that survives the addition of an arbitrary oracle therefore cannot settle P versus NP in either direction. Two further barriers are known — natural proofs (Razborov–Rudich, 1994) and algebrization (Aaronson–Wigderson, 2008) — and between them they rule out most of what is currently known how to do. That is the real reason the problem is open: not that nobody has tried, but that the available techniques are provably insufficient.

PSPACE: the same space reused exponentially often

PSPACE is where the quantifiers alternate. NP asks "does there exist a certificate?" — one existential quantifier. The canonical PSPACE-complete problem allows arbitrarily many alternating quantifiers:

TQBF, the PSPACE-complete problemTQBF = { ⟨Ψ⟩ : Ψ = Q₁x₁ Q₂x₂ … Qₙxₙ φ(x₁ … xₙ) is TRUE }, each Qᵢ ∈ {∃, ∀}, φ a Boolean formula SAT is the special case with EVERY quantifier ∃; TAUTOLOGY is the case with every quantifier ∀ — so TQBF generalises both NP-complete and co-NP-complete problems. the recursive evaluator: EVAL(i): if i > n return φ(x₁ … xₙ) if Qᵢ = ∃ return EVAL(i+1 with xᵢ = 0) OR EVAL(i+1 with xᵢ = 1) if Qᵢ = ∀ return EVAL(i+1 with xᵢ = 0) AND EVAL(i+1 with xᵢ = 1) TIME up to 2^(n+1) − 1 recursive calls — EXPONENTIAL SPACE recursion depth n, one bit of assignment per level — Θ(n), LINEAR the space is REUSED: the ∃-branch's workspace is released before the ∀-branch runs. That reuse is exactly what "polynomial SPACE, unbounded time" buys, and it is why PSPACE contains problems believed far above NP while still being a class of feasible-MEMORY computations. measured in the figure at n = 3 on φ: ∃x₁∃x₂∃x₃ φ = TRUE (5 calls) · ∃x₁∀x₂∃x₃ φ = TRUE (7) · ∀x₁∃x₂∀x₃ φ = FALSE (7) · ∀x₁∀x₂∀x₃ φ = FALSE (4) — depth 3 in every case.

The quantifier alternation is what makes TQBF the natural model of a two-player game: "does White have a move such that for every Black reply White has a move such that …". Generalised versions of two-player games whose play length is bounded by a polynomial in the board size are typically PSPACE-complete — generalised Hex, generalised geography. One-player puzzles such as Sokoban and Rush Hour are PSPACE-complete for a different reason: their shortest solutions can need exponentially many moves, but a position is polynomial-size, and whether a goal position is reachable is decidable in polynomial space (Savitch's theorem applied to the graph of positions). Two-player games whose play can last exponentially long are typically EXPTIME-complete, not PSPACE-complete: generalised chess, generalised checkers and generalised Go (with the Japanese ko rule) are EXPTIME-complete. That distinction is routinely got wrong and is worth holding onto: for two-player games the class follows from how long the game can last, not from how hard the game feels. Because P ⊊ EXPTIME is proved, the EXPTIME-complete games are provably not solvable in polynomial time — one of the very few natural problems for which "no polynomial algorithm exists" can be asserted outright rather than conditionally.

Other PSPACE-complete problems worth recognising: equivalence of two ordinary regular expressions, the NFA universality problem ("does this automaton accept every string?"), and — the one most likely to arrive in a real job — planning in the STRIPS sense, where a state is a set of propositions and the question is whether a sequence of operators reaches a goal. Plan existence is PSPACE-complete precisely because the plan may need to be exponentially long even though each state is polynomial to write down. This is also the formal shape of model checking against a large state space, and it is why symbolic methods that manipulate sets of states rather than enumerating them are the only ones that scale.

The polynomial hierarchy in one paragraph

Between NP and PSPACE sits a tower. Σ₁ᵖ = NP is "∃ certificate, checkable in P". Σ₂ᵖ is "∃ y ∀ z, checkable in P" — for instance MINIMUM-EQUIVALENT-DNF, "is there a formula of size ≤ k that agrees with this one on every input?". Π₂ᵖ reverses the quantifiers. Each level is NP with an oracle for the level below, and PH = ⋃ₖ Σₖᵖ ⊆ PSPACE. The hierarchy is believed infinite, but if any two adjacent levels coincide it collapses to that level — and in particular P = NP collapses the whole of PH to P. "The polynomial hierarchy collapses" is therefore the standard way of saying "this would be very surprising", and it is the form the evidence against graph isomorphism's NP-completeness takes (§17). Whether PH = PSPACE is open, and there is a neat argument for believing it is not: PSPACE has complete problems (TQBF is one), so PH = PSPACE would give PH a complete problem — and a complete problem for PH would have to lie in some finite level Σₖᵖ, forcing the hierarchy to collapse to that level. Anyone who believes PH is infinite must therefore believe PH ⊊ PSPACE.

Below P: L and NL

It is easy to forget that there is a hierarchy below polynomial time, and it matters for streaming and for very large inputs. L is deterministic logarithmic work space: enough for a constant number of pointers into the input, and nothing else. NL is its nondeterministic version, and directed s-t connectivity is NL-complete under log-space reductions — you can guess the path one vertex at a time, holding only the current vertex. Undirected s-t connectivity was the famous open case, known to be in NL and conjectured to be in L, until Reingold proved it in L in 2004 by a derandomised random walk. L ⊆ NL ⊆ P, all inclusions open; L ⊊ PSPACE is proved. And note the asymmetry that space enjoys and time does not: NL = co-NL is a theorem, while NP = co-NP is open. Nondeterministic space is closed under complement; nondeterministic time is not known to be.

The floor: undecidability

the halting problem, and the diagonal argument in three sentencesHALT = { ⟨M, w⟩ : the Turing machine M halts on input w }. THEOREM (Turing, 1936): HALT is UNDECIDABLE — no machine decides it on every input. 1. Suppose H decides HALT. Build D(⟨M⟩): if H says M halts on ⟨M⟩, loop forever; otherwise halt. 2. Run D on its own description ⟨D⟩. If D halts on ⟨D⟩ then H said "halts", so D loops — contradiction. If D loops then H said "loops", so D halts — contradiction. 3. Therefore no such H exists. The self-reference is the whole argument; it is Cantor's diagonal with programs in place of reals. RICE'S THEOREM (1951) generalises it: EVERY non-trivial property of the LANGUAGE a program recognises is undecidable. "non-trivial" = some program has the property and some does not; "of the language" = it depends only on WHAT the program computes, not HOW. so each of these is undecidable: does this program ever output 7? is it equivalent to that one? does it terminate on all inputs? is this line reachable? is it free of null dereferences? what is NOT covered: SYNTACTIC properties — "does the source contain a goto?", "are there more than 500 lines?" — those are decidable, because they are not properties of the language computed.

The practical consequence is the reason every static analyser behaves the way it does. Since "does this program have a null dereference?" is undecidable, no analyser can be simultaneously sound (never misses a real bug), complete (never reports a false one) and total (always terminates with an answer). Every real tool gives up one. Type checkers and sound analysers keep soundness and totality and accept false positives — they reject programs that would in fact have been fine. Bug-finders like most linters and fuzzers keep completeness-ish behaviour and totality and accept false negatives — they miss real bugs. Interactive theorem provers keep soundness and completeness and give up totality — they may need human help or may not terminate. When a tool's documentation says "may report spurious warnings", that sentence is Rice's theorem showing through, not an implementation shortcut.

Note the relationship to NP-hardness, which is the pairing this page has been building toward. NP-hard does not mean decidable. HALT is NP-hard — given a 3-CNF formula, emit the program "try all assignments; halt if one satisfies", which is computable in polynomial time and halts iff the formula is satisfiable, so 3-SAT ≤ₚ HALT — and HALT is undecidable, hence not in NP, hence not NP-complete. An NP-hard problem is at least as hard as everything in NP; it may be very much harder, and it may not be solvable at all. §07 defines the two terms; this is the example that shows why they must be kept apart.

The containment diagram, with the proved-strict separations drawn differently from the merely-believed ones, and a control that toggles between what is proved and what is believed solid boundary = a containment that is PROVED strict; dashed boundary = a containment that is not known to be strict — the two classes may be equal for all anyone can prove; the "believed" view redraws the picture as most researchers expect it to be, and the readout runs the recursive TQBF evaluator to show exponential time against linear space
What to say and what not to say. Say: "P ⊊ EXPTIME is proved, so at least one of P ⊆ NP, NP ⊆ PSPACE, PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME is strict." Say: "generalised chess is EXPTIME-complete, and therefore provably not in P." Say: "TQBF is PSPACE-complete; SAT is the all-∃ special case." Do not say: "PSPACE is bigger than NP" — that is believed, not known. Do not say: "NP-hard problems are undecidable" or "undecidable problems are NP-hard-but-worse" — NP-hardness is a lower bound and carries no upper bound at all, and HALT is the example. Do not say "Savitch's theorem shows NP = PSPACE"; Savitch shows NPSPACE = PSPACE, which is a statement about space and has no known consequence for NP.

19 Four responses to a hardness proof

A proof that a problem is NP-hard is a statement about the worst case of the general problem, solved exactly, in polynomial time — and every practical response to such a proof works by giving up exactly one of those four words. The proof does not say your instances are hard; it says that some instance, of some size, is hard, for every polynomial algorithm, unless P = NP. That leaves four doors. Give up exactness and you get approximation algorithms with a proved ratio (§20–§25) or heuristics with none (§28–§29). Give up generality and you get special cases, structural restrictions and bounded parameters (§27, and the tractable islands of §16). Give up the polynomial bound and you get exact exponential algorithms whose exponent is small enough, or whose pruning is good enough, that real instances finish (§26, §30). Give up determinism, or the worst-case criterion itself, and you get randomised algorithms and average-case analysis. This section states the four responses as trades, so that the rest of the page reads as a catalogue of the price each one charges.

What the hardness proof actually forbids — and what it leaves alone

Read the statement of §07 literally. "3-SAT is NP-complete" says: if some algorithm decides every 3-CNF formula correctly in time bounded by a polynomial in the encoded length of the formula, then P = NP. Four quantifiers do the work, and each one is a place to stand.

the claim, with its four quantifiers exposedNO algorithm A such that ∀ instances x : A(x) is EXACTLY right and time(A, x) ≤ poly(|x|) — unless P = NP ↑ ALGORITHM ↑ EVERY instance ↑ EXACT ↑ POLYNOMIAL, WORST case randomise it, restrict the family, settle for a settle for exponential with a or accept a or bound a parameter ratio, or for small exponent, or for a bound failure probability of the instances no guarantee that only the worst case breaks the four trades, as a table of what you keep every instance exact answer polynomial worst case proof of the guarantee approximation ✓ ✗ ✓ ✓ (a ratio) heuristic ✓ ✗ usually ✓ ✗ special case / parameter ✗ ✓ ✓ (in n) ✓ exact exponential ✓ ✓ ✗ ✓ (a base) randomisation ✓ ✗ (whp) or ✓ ✓ expected ✓ (in probability)

Two things the proof does not forbid, and both matter more in practice than anything it does forbid. First, it does not forbid an algorithm that is fast on your instances: SAT solvers routinely dispatch industrial formulas with millions of variables (§30) while choking on random formulas with a few hundred, and nothing in the theorem is violated — the theorem only says that some family defeats every polynomial bound. Second, it does not forbid a good exponential: a bound of O(1.3·2ⁿ) and a bound of O(1.31ⁿ) are both exponential and the second solves instances the first cannot look at, which is the whole content of §26.

Response 1 — give up optimality

Return a solution that is provably within a factor of the best one. An algorithm with approximation ratio ρ is a theorem, not a hope: for every instance, the cost it returns is within ρ of the optimum, and you never need to know the optimum to trust it. §20 fixes the definitions; §21–§24 prove four such theorems (vertex cover at 2, metric TSP at 3/2, set cover at H(d), MAX-3-SAT at 7/8); §25 shows a scheme whose ratio you dial. The catch is that the ratio is itself subject to hardness: for many problems there is a threshold below which approximating is as hard as solving, and §20 names the theorem (PCP) that produces those thresholds. The other catch is that a proved ratio is often loose — the figure of §20 measures how loose, on this page's own instances.

If you do not need a proof, drop the ratio and keep the answer: a heuristic. Nearest neighbour for TSP (§22), 2-opt and simulated annealing (§28), genetic algorithms (§29) all return tours, none comes with a bound worth quoting, and on the instances people actually solve they routinely beat the algorithms that do come with bounds. Machine learning runs almost entirely on this response: finding the smallest decision tree consistent with a training set is NP-complete, so every practical learner grows the tree greedily one split at a time and abandons optimality without apology (Decision Trees). That is not a paradox: a worst-case ratio bounds the worst instance, not the typical one, and the measured column in the §20 figure separates the two.

Response 2 — give up generality

Solve a sub-family exactly. Three shapes, in increasing order of cleverness. (i) A syntactic restriction: 2-SAT is in P while 3-SAT is NP-complete; Horn-SAT is in P; 2-colouring is bipartiteness-testing and 3-colouring is NP-complete; matching is polynomial on any graph while its "vertex" twin, independent set, is not. §16 collects these boundary pairs, and the useful reflex is to look at your instances and ask which side of a known boundary they sit on. (ii) A structural restriction: many NP-hard graph problems are polynomial — often linear-time — on trees, on series-parallel graphs, on interval graphs, or on graphs of bounded treewidth, because the dynamic programming of Dynamic Programming has somewhere to run. Planarity alone is not on that list: planar 3-colouring (§15, §16), planar vertex cover and planar Hamiltonian cycle all stay NP-complete. What planarity buys is weaker but real — approximation schemes by Baker's layering technique and exact algorithms in 2^O(√n) time, because a planar graph has treewidth O(√n). (iii) A parameter that is small in practice: vertex cover is solvable in O(1.2738ᵏ + k·n) time where k is the cover size, which is polynomial — linear, even — in n for any fixed k. §27 develops this into fixed-parameter tractability, which is the most under-used of the four responses.

Response 3 — give up the polynomial worst case

Solve it exactly, and be honest about the exponent. Brute force over all 2ⁿ subsets is one such algorithm and usually the worst one; branch and bound prunes it with a bound computed at each node; dynamic programming over subsets (Held–Karp) solves TSP in O(n²·2ⁿ) instead of O(n!), which is the difference between 20 cities and 12; and a modern SAT or ILP solver is an exact exponential algorithm with two decades of engineering inside the constant. §26 and §30 are the two halves of this response. The measurement that matters is not the exponent in the bound but the size of the search tree the solver actually builds, and every figure in those sections counts nodes rather than quoting a bound.

Response 4 — give up determinism, or the worst case as the criterion

Randomise. §24's MAX-3-SAT algorithm is the clean example: flip a fair coin for every variable and you satisfy 7m/8 clauses in expectation, which is within 8/7 of the optimum, from an algorithm that does not look at the formula. Randomisation buys three different things — a guarantee in expectation (as there), a guarantee that holds with high probability, or merely a way to escape the adversarial instance that a deterministic rule would always meet — and in the first case it can often be removed afterwards, which is what the method of conditional expectations does in §24. Separately, one can keep exactness and change the criterion: average-case analysis under an instance distribution, or smoothed analysis, both of which say that the hard instances are rare rather than absent. The phase-transition picture of §30 is exactly this: random 3-SAT is easy at most clause densities and hard in a narrow band around m/n ≈ 4.27.

The fifth response, which is not on anyone's list

Solve a different problem. Much of the time the NP-hard formulation is an artefact of how the question was written down, not of what was asked. If the real requirement is "a good schedule by Friday", the NP-hard "an optimal schedule" was never the requirement. If the objective is a guess — a hand-tuned cost function, a proxy metric — then an exactly optimal solution to the guess is not obviously better than an approximate solution to it, and the modelling error may dwarf the optimality gap. If the constraint that makes the problem hard was added defensively, relax it. Concretely: fractional knapsack is solved by a greedy rule in O(n log n) while 0-1 knapsack is NP-hard (Greedy Algorithms); bipartite matching is polynomial while 3-dimensional matching is NP-complete; minimum spanning tree is polynomial while the Steiner tree that it is often mistaken for is NP-hard. Checking whether the hardness is in the problem or in the formulation costs an afternoon and occasionally saves the project.

responsewhat you give upwhat you keepon this pagethe question to ask first
approximation with a proved ratiooptimality, by a factor you can nameevery instance, polynomial time, a theorem§20–§25is a factor of 2 acceptable to the caller?
heuristicoptimality and the guaranteespeed, simplicity, usually excellent answers§28, §29can I measure quality on real instances?
special case / structural restrictiongeneralityexactness, polynomial time, a theorem§16, §27are my instances trees, planar, bipartite, sparse?
bounded parameter (FPT)generality, in a controlled wayexactness; polynomial in n for fixed k§27is there a number in my instances that stays small?
exact exponential searchthe polynomial worst caseexactness, every instance, a provable base§26, §30how big do my instances actually get?
randomisationdeterminism (and sometimes exactness)a guarantee in expectation or whp§24, §28is an expected guarantee enough?
change the problemthe original formulationeverything else§32did the hard constraint come from the requirement or from me?
The responses compose, and in practice they always do. A production solver for a routing problem is typically an exact exponential search (response 3) over a restricted model (response 2), warm-started from a constructive approximation (response 1), improved by local search (response 1's undisciplined cousin), with randomised restarts (response 4), answering a question that was deliberately simplified (response 5). Nothing on this page is a rival to anything else on it; the sections are components. §32 assembles them into a decision procedure.

20 Approximation ratios, and the APX / PTAS / FPTAS taxonomy

An algorithm has approximation ratio ρ(n) if, on every instance of size n, the cost C it returns and the optimal cost C* satisfy max(C/C*, C*/C) ≤ ρ(n) — one formula for both minimisation and maximisation, normalised so that ρ ≥ 1 always and ρ = 1 means exact. That convention is the one this page uses everywhere: every ratio quoted below is at least 1, and a bigger number is a worse guarantee. On top of it sit three classes, each a strictly stronger promise than the last: APX, the problems with a polynomial ρ-approximation for some constant ρ; PTAS, the problems where ρ can be pushed to 1 + ε for any ε > 0 you name, in time polynomial in n for each fixed ε; and FPTAS, where that time is polynomial in n and in 1/ε together. This section fixes the definitions, states the containments and why each is believed strict, names the theorem (PCP) that produces inapproximability thresholds, and explains the one structural property — strong NP-hardness — that rules an FPTAS out.

The definition, both directions at once

approximation ratioan algorithm has APPROXIMATION RATIO ρ(n) if for every instance of size n, with C = the cost it returns and C* = the optimal cost (both > 0): max( C / C* , C* / C ) ≤ ρ(n) MINIMISATION (vertex cover, TSP, set cover): C* ≤ C, so the live term is C / C* ≤ ρ — "at most ρ times too big" MAXIMISATION (MAX-3-SAT, knapsack): C ≤ C*, so the live term is C* / C ≤ ρ — "at least 1/ρ of the best" the ratio is NEVER below 1; ρ = 1 is an exact algorithm; larger ρ = weaker promise the other conventions you will meet — and this page does NOT usefor maximisation some texts write C ≥ α·C* with α ≤ 1 (a "7/8-approximation"); that α is 1/ρ. ► MAX-3-SAT's "7/8 algorithm" is the α = 7/8 convention; in this page's ρ ≥ 1 convention it is an 8/7-approximation. ► where a source's number could be read either way, this page writes BOTH, as in "7/8 of the optimum, i.e. ratio 8/7 ≈ 1.143". ABSOLUTE error |C − C*| — a much stronger and much rarer promise. Colouring a PLANAR graph is one of the few examples: χ(G) ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4} there (four colour theorem); χ = 1 and χ = 2 are decidable in linear time (no edges; bipartite), and 3-colourability of a planar graph is NP-complete — so "1 if edgeless, 2 if bipartite, else 4" is within an ABSOLUTE 1 of χ(G), off only on the 3-chromatic graphs. For most problems no absolute bound is possible: scaling every cost by 10 scales the absolute error by 10 and leaves the ratio alone. RELATIVE error |C − C*| / C* = ρ − 1 for minimisation. This is what "within 5%" means.

Two small traps in that definition, both of which this page's figures check rather than assume. First, C* must be positive for the ratio to be defined; problems whose optimum can be zero (minimum number of edges to delete, say) need an additive convention instead, and a "ratio" quoted for them is a different object. Second, the ratio is a worst-case quantity over all instances of a size, so measuring it on one instance can only ever produce a lower bound on it. The figure below measures achieved ratios on this page's instances; those measurements confirm the bounds, they never prove them, and the gap between measured and proved is itself the thing to look at.

Algorithm versus scheme

A ρ-approximation algorithm is one program with one ratio baked in: §21's vertex-cover algorithm is a 2-approximation and there is no dial on it. An approximation scheme takes the instance and a parameter ε > 0, and for each fixed ε it is a (1 + ε)-approximation algorithm — a whole family of algorithms indexed by how good you want the answer. The only question left is what the price of a small ε is.

the three classesAPX there is a polynomial-time ρ-approximation for some CONSTANT ρ. metric TSP (ρ = 3/2, §22), vertex cover (ρ = 2, §21), MAX-3-SAT (ρ = 8/7, §24) PTAS for every fixed ε > 0 there is a (1 + ε)-approximation running in time polynomial in n. the DEGREE of that polynomial may depend on ε — O(n^(2/ε)) or O(n^(1/ε)·2^(1/ε²)) is still a PTAS. Euclidean TSP (§22), makespan on identical machines, knapsack FPTAS a PTAS whose running time is polynomial in n AND in 1/ε — O(n³/ε), O(n/ε²), … halving ε multiplies the time by a CONSTANT, not by n^something. knapsack / subset sum (§25) containments FPTAS ⊆ PTAS ⊆ APX ⊆ NPO each ⊆ is immediate from the definitions; each is STRICT unless P = NP: APX ⊄ PTAS : MAX-3-SAT is in APX (§24) and has no PTAS unless P = NP (Håstad's 7/8 threshold, below). PTAS ⊄ FPTAS: minimum makespan on identical machines has a PTAS and is STRONGLY NP-hard, so no FPTAS unless P = NP. NPO ⊄ APX : GENERAL TSP has no constant-factor approximation at all unless P = NP (§22), and MAX-CLIQUE has none within n^(1−ε) for any ε > 0 unless P = NP. every one of these separations is CONDITIONAL on P ≠ NP. If P = NP the four classes collapse into one.

Where the thresholds come from: the PCP theorem, stated carefully

Until 1992 there was no general technique for proving a problem hard to approximate; a reduction that preserves yes/no answers says nothing about near-optimal solutions. The PCP theorem supplied one. In its complexity form it says NP = PCP(log n, 1): every language in NP has a proof system in which the verifier tosses O(log n) random coins, reads a constant number of bits of the proof, accepts every correct proof of a true statement, and rejects every proof of a false statement with probability at least 1/2. Its equivalent form is the one used here — a gap statement.

PCP, in the form that makes approximation hardthere is a constant δ > 0 and a polynomial-time map φ ↦ ψ from 3-CNF formulas to 3-CNF formulas such that φ SATISFIABLE ⟹ ψ is satisfiable (all m clauses) φ UNSATISFIABLE ⟹ no assignment satisfies more than (1 − δ)·m clauses of ψ consequence: a polynomial algorithm approximating MAX-3-SAT within 1/(1 − δ) could tell the two cases apart, hence decide SAT, hence P = NP. So MAX-3-SAT has NO PTAS unless P = NP. — approximating past the gap is as hard as solving exactly. This is the GAP-INTRODUCING reduction, and every threshold below is obtained by transporting this gap to another problem with a gap-preserving reduction. thresholds, each with its hypothesis statedMAX-3-SAT any ratio below 8/7 (i.e. satisfying more than (7/8 + ε)·m clauses) NP-hard §24 — for formulas whose clauses have exactly three DISTINCT variables. Matched EXACTLY by §24's algorithm. SET COVER any ratio of the form (1 − ε)·ln n, for each fixed ε > 0 NP-hard §23 — i.e. nothing beats ln n even by a constant factor. — matched to within lower-order terms by the greedy H(d) bound. VERTEX COVER any ratio below 1.3606… (= 10√5 − 21) NP-hard §21 any ratio below √2 ≈ 1.4142 (i.e. √2 − ε for each fixed ε > 0) NP-hard (via the 2-to-2 games theorem) any ratio below 2 (i.e. 2 − ε for each fixed ε > 0) hard UNDER THE UNIQUE GAMES CONJECTURE — an unproven hypothesis, NOT a theorem METRIC TSP any ratio below 123/122 ≈ 1.0082 NP-hard §22 GENERAL TSP ANY constant ratio NP-hard §22 MAX-CLIQUE any ratio below n^(1−ε), for each fixed ε > 0 NP-hard §11

Read those lines with §07's discipline. "NP-hard" here means: a polynomial algorithm achieving that ratio would give a polynomial algorithm for SAT, so it would prove P = NP. It does not mean such an algorithm is known not to exist — P ≠ NP is unproven, and every line above is conditional. The unique-games line is conditional twice over, on a hypothesis that is not even a consequence of P ≠ NP; it is listed because the vertex-cover gap between the known 2 and the known-hard √2 (earlier 1.3606) is the standard example of a gap that a conjecture, not a theorem, would close.

Strong NP-hardness: the reason some problems can never have an FPTAS

§14 drew the distinction the argument needs.
