A problem is strongly NP-hard if it stays NP-hard when every number in the instance is bounded by a polynomial in the instance length — that is, when the numbers are written in unary. Subset sum and knapsack are not strongly NP-hard: their O(nW) and O(nV) dynamic programs are polynomial once the numbers are small, which is exactly the hook §25's FPTAS hangs on. TSP, 3-colouring, 3-SAT, vertex cover and 3-Partition are strongly NP-hard — their hardness survives tiny numbers, because they have no big numbers to shrink.

theorem — strong NP-hardness excludes an FPTASlet Π be an optimisation problem whose objective is INTEGER-valued, and suppose OPT(I) ≤ p(|I|, max(I)) for some polynomial p, where max(I) is the largest number appearing in I. If Π is STRONGLY NP-hard, it has no FPTAS unless P = NP. proof sketch: suppose an FPTAS runs in time q(|I|, 1/ε) for a polynomial q. Choose ε = 1 / (p(|I|, max(I)) + 1) < 1/OPT(I). then the relative error is below 1/OPT, so |C − OPT| < 1, and both are integers: C = OPT — the scheme is EXACT. its running time is q(|I|, p(|I|, max(I)) + 1) — polynomial in |I| and max(I), i.e. a PSEUDO-POLYNOMIAL exact algorithm. A strongly NP-hard problem has none unless P = NP (that is what "strongly" means). ∎ reading it backwards: an FPTAS is, in disguise, a pseudo-polynomial algorithm with the numbers scaled down. so LOOK FOR AN FPTAS exactly where §14 found a pseudo-polynomial DP, and nowhere else.

What a proved ratio is worth in practice — the measurement

The figure runs every approximation algorithm on this page against the exhaustive optimum of its own instance and plots the achieved ratio beside the proved bound. Three things come out of it, and the third is the one worth internalising. (i) The bounds hold, every time — that is the agree flag. (ii) Most of them are loose by a wide margin on ordinary instances: Christofides' 3/2 guarantee produces a tour 0.14% above optimal on the eight-city instance of §22, and greedy set cover's H(6) = 2.45 guarantee produces a cover 1.33 times the optimum. (iii) The bounds are not loose on the instances built to break them: the maximal-matching vertex cover hits its bound of 2 exactly, on both the seven-vertex instance and the fifty-vertex adversarial family, and greedy set cover reaches 3.5 on the family of §23. A proved ratio describes the adversary, and the adversary is rarely who you are dealing with — which is precisely why §28's unguaranteed local search can beat the guaranteed algorithms and still be the right choice.

Every approximation algorithm on this page: the ratio it achieves, measured, against the ratio it promises blue bar = the MEASURED ratio C/C* (minimisation) or C*/C (maximisation), computed against an exhaustive optimum; the amber tick = the proved worst-case bound; the bar turns red if a measurement ever exceeds its bound (none does); nearest neighbour has no constant bound at all and is drawn without a tick

Vocabulary the rest of the page uses without comment

  • NPO — the optimisation analogue of NP: instances and solutions are polynomially bounded and recognisable, the objective is computable in polynomial time, and one asks for a maximum or a minimum. Every problem in this section's taxonomy is in NPO.
  • APX-hard — every problem in APX reduces to it by an approximation-preserving reduction; by the PCP theorem it therefore has no PTAS unless P = NP. The implication runs one way only: "no PTAS unless P = NP" does not by itself make a problem APX-hard. Vertex cover, metric TSP, MAX-3-SAT and set cover are all APX-hard.
  • Gap-preserving reduction — a reduction that carries a "either ≥ a or ≤ b" gap in one problem to a gap in another, so that a good-enough approximation for the second would resolve the first. Ordinary Karp reductions (§06) do not do this; approximation hardness needs the stronger kind.
  • Tight — a ratio is tight when some instance family achieves it. §21's 2 is tight (a single edge already does it), §22's 3/2 is tight for Christofides, §23's H(d) is tight to within lower-order terms. A tight bound cannot be improved by a better analysis; only a better algorithm can help.
  • Asymptotic ratio — some guarantees hold only for large optima, as in bin packing's first-fit-decreasing rule, which uses at most (11/9)·OPT + 6/9 bins (and that additive constant is tight). This page states the additive term whenever there is one; a bare ratio here means the bound holds on every instance.

21 Vertex cover: 2 from a maximal matching

Take any maximal matching of the graph and output both endpoints of every matched edge: that is a vertex cover, and it is at most twice the minimum, because every cover must contain at least one endpoint of each matched edge and the matched edges are disjoint. The algorithm is three lines, it never computes or estimates the optimum, and its proof is two sentences — and yet, more than forty years after it was published, no polynomial algorithm with a constant ratio below 2 is known. That combination makes it the standard first example of an approximation algorithm, and also the standard example of how far a trivial algorithm can be from improvable. This section gives the algorithm, the proof in full, the instance on which the bound is exactly attained, the reason the other obvious greedy — repeatedly take the highest-degree vertex — is not a 2-approximation despite looking smarter, and the current state of the hardness results, with the conditionality of each stated precisely.

The algorithm

APPROX-VERTEX-COVER(G = (V, E))C ← ∅ E′ ← E while E′ ≠ ∅: pick any edge (u, v) ∈ E′ ← ANY edge. No rule, no heuristic, no tie-breaking. Any. C ← C ∪ {u, v} delete from E′ every edge incident on u or on v return C running time Θ(V + E) with adjacency lists and a "used" flag per vertex — one pass over the edge list. what it is really doing: the edges it PICKS form a MAXIMAL MATCHING M (no two share an endpoint, and no edge outside M can be added), and it returns the 2|M| endpoints of M.

Two observations before the proof, because they are where the algorithm's power actually lives. First, the maximality of the matching is what makes the output a cover: if some edge (x, y) had neither endpoint in C, then neither x nor y was ever matched, so (x, y) could have been added to M — contradicting maximality, and equally contradicting the loop condition, since (x, y) would still be in E′. Second, the matching is a lower bound you can hold in your hand: the algorithm produces, alongside its answer, a certificate |M| with OPT ≥ |M|. That is unusual and valuable. Most approximation algorithms prove their ratio by an argument about an optimum nobody computes; this one hands you the witness, so on any particular instance you can report "my cover has 6 vertices and the optimum is at least 3" without further work. §26 uses exactly this kind of computable lower bound to prune a branch-and-bound search.

The proof, in full

theoremAPPROX-VERTEX-COVER runs in polynomial time and returns a vertex cover C with |C| ≤ 2·|C*|, where C* is a minimum vertex cover. It is therefore a 2-approximation algorithm (ρ = 2 in the ρ ≥ 1 convention of §20). proof(1) C IS A COVER. The loop exits only when E′ = ∅, and an edge leaves E′ only when one of its endpoints enters C. So every edge of E has an endpoint in C. (2) |C| = 2|M|. Each iteration adds exactly two vertices, both previously absent — the chosen edge (u, v) is still in E′, so neither u nor v is in C yet (if either were, the edge would have been deleted). M is the set of chosen edges; the iterations are |M| in number; hence |C| = 2|M|, exactly, never less. (3) |C*| ≥ |M|. ◄── the only step with content. The edges of M are pairwise VERTEX-DISJOINT: when (u, v) is chosen, every other edge touching u or v is deleted, so no later chosen edge shares an endpoint with it. A vertex cover must contain at least one endpoint of (u, v), for every (u, v) ∈ M. Those |M| obligations are on |M| DISJOINT pairs of vertices, so they are satisfied by |M| DISTINCT vertices. Hence any cover, and in particular C*, has at least |M| vertices. (4) chain (2) and (3): |C| = 2|M| ≤ 2|C*|. ∎ note what the proof never does: it never computes C*, never bounds |C*| from ABOVE, and never uses any property of WHICH edge the loop picks. Every maximal matching gives a valid 2-approximation; the choices only change WHICH one.

Worked on the seven-vertex instance, and the bound attained exactly

The instance is the graph of §12: vertices a … g and the eight edges ab, bc, cd, ce, de, df, dg, ef. Degrees are a 1, b 2, c 3, d 4, e 3, f 2, g 1, summing to 16 = 2·8 as they must. Taking the edges in that stored order, the algorithm chooses ab (both endpoints free), skips bc and ce and de and df and dg (each already touches a matched vertex), and chooses cd and ef. The matching is M = {ab, cd, ef}, |M| = 3, and the returned cover is {a, b, c, d, e, f} — six vertices, every edge covered (dg through d, bc through b, and so on), with g left out. Exhaustive search over all 2⁷ = 128 subsets says the minimum cover has 3 vertices and is unique: {b, d, e}.

the measured ratio on this instance|C| = 6, |C*| = 3, ratio = 6 / 3 = 2.000 exactly — the bound of the theorem, attained. the certificate the algorithm hands over: |M| = 3, so OPT ≥ 3; and C has 6; the two meet, so here the LOWER bound is tight as well and the algorithm has, in passing, PROVED that OPT = 3. the ratio is tight already at two verticesG = a single edge (u, v). Maximal matching = {(u, v)}; C = {u, v}, |C| = 2; C* = {u}, |C*| = 1; ratio 2/1 = 2. G = the complete bipartite graph K_{n,n}. Any maximal matching is a perfect matching, |M| = n, so |C| = 2n; either side is a cover, so |C*| = n; ratio 2n/n = 2, for every n. ► so the "2" is not an artefact of small graphs or of a bad tie-break. It is the worst case, it is attained by an infinite family, and no sharper ANALYSIS of this algorithm can beat it. Only a different algorithm could.

The complete-bipartite example is worth a second look, because it shows the algorithm failing for a structural reason rather than an unlucky one. In K_{n,n} a maximal matching must saturate every vertex, so the algorithm is forced to output all 2n vertices — and the optimum is one whole side. There is nothing the edge-selection rule could have done. The information the algorithm never uses is that this graph is bipartite; on a bipartite graph the minimum vertex cover equals the maximum matching (König's theorem) and is computable exactly in polynomial time by the flow reduction of Shortest Paths, MST & Flow. That is response 2 of §19 in miniature: vertex cover is NP-hard in general and polynomial on bipartite graphs, and detecting bipartiteness costs one breadth-first search.

The "smarter" greedy is worse: maximum degree is Θ(log n), not 2

The rule everyone proposes on first hearing the problem is: repeatedly take a vertex of maximum remaining degree, delete its edges, repeat. It is a better heuristic — on the seven-vertex instance it picks d (degree 4), then b (degree 2 among what is left), then e, returning exactly {b, d, e}, the unique optimum, where the matching algorithm returned six vertices. It is also not a 2-approximation, and the gap is unbounded.

The reason is that this greedy is the greedy set-cover algorithm of §23 in disguise: cover the set E of edges by the sets S_v = {edges incident on v}. So it inherits set cover's guarantee, H(Δ) where Δ is the maximum degree, and it inherits set cover's lower bound too — there are graphs on which it really does take Θ(log n) times the optimum. The family the figure builds is the standard one: a left side L of k vertices, and for each i = 2, 3, …, k a right group of ⌊k/i⌋ vertices, each joined to i distinct left vertices with the groups partitioning L within each i. Every right vertex of group i has degree exactly i, so the greedy sweeps the right side from i = k downwards and takes ∑ᵢ₌₂ᵏ ⌊k/i⌋ ≈ k(ln k − 1) vertices, while L — which covers every edge, since every edge has a left endpoint — has k.

measured on the family, with the optimum PINNED without exhaustive search k n |E| max-degree greedy maximal matching OPT greedy ratio matching ratio 8 20 48 12 16 8 1.500 2.000 12 35 115 23 24 12 1.917 2.000 16 50 204 34 32 16 2.125 ◄── 2.000 20 66 319 46 40 20 2.300 2.000 30 111 732 81 60 30 2.700 2.000 40 158 1 302 118 80 40 2.950 2.000 60 261 2 954 201 120 60 3.350 2.000 how OPT is known exactly for n = 261 without touching 2²⁶¹ subsets — the section's own two lemmas, closing on it: L is a cover ⟹ OPT ≤ k OPT ≥ |M|, and |M| = k here ⟹ OPT ≥ k so OPT = k, proved, for every row of the table. ► the 2-approximation's own lower-bound certificate is what certifies the ratio of its rival.

The crossing point is worth naming: at n = 50 the max-degree greedy returns 34 vertices against an optimum of 16 — a ratio of 2.125, above 2 — while the maximal-matching algorithm returns 32, exactly 2·OPT. Below that size the greedy wins on this family and above it the greedy loses, by a margin that keeps growing like ln n. That is the shape of the whole approximation/heuristic trade of §19: on this family the algorithm with the proof sits at exactly 2 at every size — never worse, and never better either — while the algorithm without one is better at the small sizes and unboundedly worse at the large ones. (On other graphs the matching algorithm can of course do better than 2: on a triangle it returns two vertices against an optimum of two, ratio 1. Its guarantee is a ceiling, not a prediction.) Neither dominates, and only the first can be quoted to a caller who needs a bound.

The 2-approximation, edge by edge: the matching builds, the cover is read off, and an exhaustive search over all 2ⁿ subsets supplies the optimum amber edge = the edge being examined; green edge = taken into the maximal matching; green vertex = in the returned cover; blue outline = in the (exhaustive) minimum cover; grey dashed = an edge skipped because an endpoint is already matched; the readout measures |M|, |C|, the exhaustive optimum, the achieved ratio and whether ratio ≤ 2

Why the complement trick does not give an independent-set approximation

§12 proved that C is a vertex cover if and only if V \ C is an independent set, so the two problems are the same problem read in a mirror and an exact algorithm for one is an exact algorithm for the other. Approximation is not preserved by the mirror, and this is the cleanest illustration on the page of why approximation classes are finer than complexity classes. Take a graph on n = 2k vertices with minimum cover k − 1, so the maximum independent set has k + 1 vertices. A 2-approximate cover may have 2k − 2 vertices — a fine cover, ratio under 2 — and its complement is an independent set of 2 vertices against an optimum of k + 1: ratio (k+1)/2, unbounded. The arithmetic is the point: a multiplicative error on |C| becomes an additive error of the same size, and an additive error of k is nothing next to n but everything next to an optimum of size k + 1. Consistently, vertex cover is in APX with ratio 2, while maximum independent set (equivalently maximum clique, §11) has no n^(1−ε)-approximation for any fixed ε > 0 unless P = NP.

What is known about doing better than 2 — with every hypothesis stated

  • No constant below 2 is known. The best published ratios are all of the form 2 − o(1): 2 − (ln ln n)/(2 ln n), improved to 2 − Θ(1/√(log n)). Every one of these tends to 2 as n grows, so none is a ρ-approximation for any constant ρ < 2. "No better than 2 is known" is the accurate sentence; "2 is optimal" is not a theorem.
  • Vertex cover is APX-hard, so it has no PTAS unless P = NP. Concretely, achieving any ratio below 1.3606… = 10√5 − 21 is NP-hard, and that was strengthened by way of the 2-to-2 games theorem: achieving √2 − ε ≈ 1.4142 is NP-hard for every fixed ε > 0, so no ratio below √2 is reachable. Both are unconditional NP-hardness results — they assume nothing beyond P ≠ NP.
  • Under the unique games conjecture — an unproven hypothesis, not a theorem, and not known to follow from P ≠ NP — achieving 2 − ε is NP-hard for every fixed ε > 0, so no ratio below 2 would be reachable, which would make this three-line algorithm optimal. The honest statement of the field's position is: the truth is somewhere in [√2, 2], most people expect 2, and nobody has proved it.
  • Weighted vertex cover — minimise ∑ w(v) over covers — is not solved by this algorithm at all: taking both endpoints of a matched edge can take an arbitrarily heavy vertex when a light one would do. A ratio of 2 survives by a different route: write the integer program, relax the integrality of x_v ∈ {0, 1} to 0 ≤ x_v ≤ 1, solve the linear program in polynomial time — it is a convex problem, so a local optimum is a global one (Optimization) — and round every x_v ≥ 1/2 up. Every edge constraint x_u + x_v ≥ 1 forces at least one of its endpoints to 1/2, so the rounded set is a cover; and rounding at most doubles each variable, so the weight at most doubles the LP optimum, which is itself a lower bound on the integer optimum. §30 returns to this LP-relaxation-and-round pattern, which is the single most productive source of approximation algorithms.
  • On special graph classes the problem is not hard at all. Bipartite: exact by matching (König). Trees: exact by a linear-time dynamic program over the tree, as in Dynamic Programming. Bounded treewidth: exact. Planar: a PTAS exists. Small cover: the FPT algorithm of §27 finds a cover of size k in O(1.2738ᵏ + k·n) — exactly, not approximately.

22 The travelling salesman: 2, then 3/2, and why general TSP has none

There are three travelling-salesman problems, and they have three different answers: the general problem, with arbitrary non-negative costs, admits no polynomial ρ-approximation for any constant ρ unless P = NP; the metric problem, whose costs obey the triangle inequality, has a 2-approximation from a spanning tree and a 3/2-approximation from a spanning tree plus a matching; and the Euclidean problem, whose cities are points in the plane, has a PTAS. Conflating them is the single most common error made about approximation, and it is easy to make, because all three are called "TSP" and all three have a decision version of the same shape, "is there a tour of cost at most B?" — NP-complete for general and for metric costs, and NP-hard for Euclidean distances, where it is not even known to be in NP, because comparing a sum of square roots against B exactly is not known to be possible in polynomial time. The difference is not in how hard the problems are to solve — all three are NP-hard — but in how much structure an approximation algorithm has to exploit. This section derives both metric algorithms with their proofs, measures both on an eight-city instance against an exhaustive optimum over all 2 520 tours, and then runs the impossibility argument for the general problem on the gap instance that §13 constructs — measuring that too.

The three problems, and the hypothesis each bound needs

the input is always the same shape; the hypothesis on c is what differsGENERAL TSP a complete graph on n vertices with costs c(u, v) ≥ 0. No further assumption. approximation: NONE with any constant ratio, unless P = NP. (proved below) METRIC TSP additionally c(u, w) ≤ c(u, v) + c(v, w) for all u, v, w ← the TRIANGLE INEQUALITY and c symmetric. "A detour is never a shortcut." approximation: 2 (double tree), 3/2 (Christofides), and since 2021 a randomised 3/2 − ε, derandomised in 2023 (ε about 10⁻³⁶ in the original analysis). hardness: no ratio better than 123/122 ≈ 1.0082, unless P = NP. So NO PTAS: metric TSP is APX-hard. EUCLIDEAN TSP the cities are POINTS in the plane and c is straight-line distance. (Metric, and much more.) approximation: a PTAS — for every ε > 0, a (1 + ε)-approximation in time n·(log n)^O(1/ε). hardness: still NP-hard to solve EXACTLY. A PTAS is not a polynomial exact algorithm. ► these are nested: Euclidean ⊂ metric ⊂ general. A bound proved for the general problem applies to all three; a bound proved for the metric problem says NOTHING about the general one. Every ratio below names its class.

The eight-city instance used throughout is Euclidean — cities at A(0,0) B(4,0) C(8,1) D(9,5) E(6,8) F(2,7) G(0,4) H(4,4) — and the figure checks the triangle inequality over all 8³ = 512 ordered triples before it quotes a ratio, because both metric bounds are void without it. Exhaustive search over all 7!/2 = 2 520 distinct tours (fix the start, halve by direction) gives the optimum 32.1721, the tour A-B-C-D-E-H-F-G.

The 2-approximation: build a tree, walk it, shortcut

APPROX-TSP-TOUR(G, c) — "double tree"1. pick any root r; compute a minimum spanning tree T of the complete graph under c (Prim or Kruskal — see Shortest Paths, MST & Flow) 2. let H = the list of vertices in PREORDER of a depth-first walk of T from r 3. return the Hamiltonian cycle that visits the vertices in the order H running time O(n² ) dominated by the MST on a complete graph (O(V²) Prim with an array, O(E log V) with a heap). proof that the ratio is 2 — three inequalities, and only the third uses the triangle inequality(1) c(T) ≤ c(H*) where H* is an optimal tour. DELETE any one edge from H*: what remains is a spanning PATH, which is a spanning tree. Costs are ≥ 0, so that tree costs at most c(H*). T is a MINIMUM spanning tree, so c(T) ≤ that tree ≤ c(H*) = OPT. ◄ no triangle inequality needed; only c ≥ 0. (2) the FULL WALK W — the closed walk that traverses every tree edge exactly twice, once down and once back up — has c(W) = 2·c(T). It visits every vertex and returns to r, but repeats vertices, so it is not a tour. (3) SHORTCUTTING W to H does not increase the cost: c(H) ≤ c(W). H is obtained from W by deleting each repeat visit, which replaces a stretch u ⇝ … ⇝ v of W by the single edge (u, v). By the triangle inequality applied along that stretch, c(u, v) ≤ (cost of the stretch). ◄ THIS is the only step that needs the triangle inequality, and it is where general TSP loses everything. chain them: c(H) ≤ c(W) = 2·c(T) ≤ 2·c(H*) = 2·OPT. ∎

Measured on the eight-city instance: the minimum spanning tree has weight 27.5804 — edges A-B, A-G, G-F, F-H, B-C, C-D, F-E — and is indeed below the optimal tour's 32.1721, as step (1) requires. The full walk costs 2 × 27.5804 = 55.1608. Shortcutting the preorder walk A-B-C-D-C-B-A-G-F-E-F-H-F-G-A gives the tour A-B-C-D-G-F-E-H at 39.1592, comfortably under the walk's 55.1608 and under 2·OPT = 64.3442. The achieved ratio is 1.2172 against a promised 2 — the guarantee is loose here by a factor of more than 1.6, which is the general experience with this algorithm.

Is the 2 tight? Essentially yes: there are metric families on which the double-tree tour approaches 2·OPT. The reason is visible in the measured tour above — the shortcut D→G costs 9.0554, crossing the whole instance, because the preorder walk finished the B-C-D branch and had to jump to the other side of the tree. The algorithm's weakness is that doubling every tree edge is wasteful: only the odd-degree vertices actually need help.

Christofides' 3/2: double only what must be doubled

The double-tree algorithm doubles every edge in order to make every vertex degree even, which is what an Eulerian circuit needs. But a tree's degrees are already even at most vertices; the ones that need fixing are exactly the odd-degree ones, and there is an even number of those (the handshake lemma: degrees sum to 2|E|, so the odd ones are even in number). Pair them up with a minimum-weight perfect matching instead of doubling everything.

CHRISTOFIDES(G, c) — metric TSP, ratio 3/21. T ← a minimum spanning tree of G 2. O ← the vertices of ODD degree in T |O| is EVEN, by the handshake lemma 3. M ← a MINIMUM-WEIGHT PERFECT MATCHING on O, using the costs c polynomial: Edmonds' blossom algorithm, O(n³) 4. every vertex of the multigraph T ∪ M has even degree tree degree + 1 for each vertex of O 5. find an Eulerian circuit of T ∪ M Hierholzer, O(|E|) 6. shortcut it to a Hamiltonian cycle and return it proof that the ratio is 3/2(1) c(T) ≤ OPT exactly as before — delete an edge from an optimal tour. (2) c(M) ≤ OPT / 2 ◄── the heart of the proof. let H* be an optimal tour and let H*|O be the CYCLE on O obtained by visiting the vertices of O in the order H* visits them, shortcutting past everything else. By the triangle inequality, c(H*|O) ≤ c(H*) = OPT. H*|O is a cycle on an EVEN number of vertices, so its edges split into TWO disjoint perfect matchings of O (take alternate edges). The cheaper of the two costs at most c(H*|O)/2 ≤ OPT/2. M is a MINIMUM perfect matching on O, so c(M) ≤ OPT/2. ◄ the triangle inequality is used once more here, to shortcut H* down to O. (3) c(T ∪ M) = c(T) + c(M) ≤ OPT + OPT/2 = (3/2)·OPT. (4) the Eulerian circuit of T ∪ M has cost exactly c(T ∪ M), and shortcutting does not increase it (triangle inequality, a third time). So c(tour) ≤ (3/2)·OPT. ∎

Measured on the eight-city instance. The MST's degree sequence is A 2, B 2, C 2, D 1, E 1, F 3, G 2, H 1 — summing to 14 = 2 × 7 edges, as it must — so the odd-degree set is O = {D, E, F, H}, four vertices, giving 3!! = 3 perfect matchings to compare. The cheapest is {(D,E), (F,H)} at 4.2426 + 3.6056 = 7.8482, and the proof's bound on it is OPT/2 = 16.0860 — satisfied with room to spare. The multigraph T ∪ M weighs 27.5804 + 7.8482 = 35.4286, well under (3/2)·OPT = 48.2581. Its Eulerian circuit is A-B-C-D-E-F-H-F-G-A; shortcutting the repeated F gives the tour A-B-C-D-E-F-H-G at 32.2175 — a ratio of 1.0014, which is 0.14% above an optimum found by examining all 2 520 tours. That is characteristic: Christofides' tours are usually within a couple of per cent of optimal even though its promise is 50%.

On the 3/2, and on not overstating the 2021 improvement. The 3/2 algorithm dates from 1976 (found independently twice that year) and its ratio stood as the best known for metric TSP for forty-five years. In 2021 it was improved — to a randomised 3/2 − ε for a constant ε of order 10⁻³⁶ — and in 2023 the same authors derandomised it, so a deterministic 3/2 − ε algorithm now exists too. That is a landmark because it broke a barrier, not because it produces better tours: no instance you will ever run distinguishes 3/2 from 3/2 − 10⁻³⁶. The practical statement remains "3/2 is the best ratio anyone can deliver", and the theoretical statement is "3/2 is no longer known to be the limit". Note also that 3/2 is tight for this algorithm — there are metric families on which Christofides' tour approaches (3/2)·OPT — so any further improvement must change the algorithm, not sharpen its analysis. The gap between the achievable 3/2 and the proved-hard 123/122 is one of the best-known open gaps in the field.

Nearest neighbour: no constant bound, and why

The obvious heuristic — start somewhere, repeatedly jump to the nearest unvisited city, close the loop — is worth measuring because it is what everyone writes first and because it illustrates what "no constant bound" looks like in practice. From A the rule goes A → B (4), B → H (4, just beating C at 4.1231), H → F (3.6056), F → G (3.6056), G → E (7.2111), E → D (4.2426), D → C (4.1231), and then has no choice but to close C → A at 8.0623 — the longest edge in the tour, forced at the last step by the seven greedy decisions before it. Total 38.8502, ratio 1.2076, which on this instance is slightly better than the double-tree algorithm's 1.2172 despite having no guarantee at all. On metric instances nearest neighbour's worst-case ratio is at most ½·(⌈log₂ n⌉ + 1) and there are families forcing Ω(log n), so it is not a ρ-approximation for any constant ρ — it merely usually behaves like one. Feed its tour to 2-opt (§28) — a local search on the space of tours, with all the local-minimum hazards that phrase implies (Optimization) — and, on this instance, first-improvement 2-opt needs 2 improving moves to bring it to 32.1721 (best-improvement, from the same start, stops at a worse local optimum, 33.0944): the exact optimum, found by a heuristic with no guarantee whatsoever, from a start produced by another heuristic with no guarantee whatsoever. §19's trade, once more, with numbers.

General TSP: no constant ratio at all

Everything above rests on the triangle inequality, used three times in Christofides' proof and once in the double tree's. Remove it and not only do those proofs fail — every constant-factor approximation becomes impossible, unless P = NP. The instance that shows it has already been built: §13 takes the reduction HAM-CYCLE ≤ₚ TSP, whose plain form weights edges of G at 1 and non-edges at 2 against a budget of |V|, and widens the non-edge weight to ρ|V| + 1 so that the gap between the two cases exceeds any constant ρ you name. §13 stops once the instance is built and its optimum measured; this is where the approximation argument is actually run. The costs, the budget and the numbers below are §13's, unchanged — writing n for |V|, as the rest of this section does.

theorem — if P ≠ NP, then for no constant ρ ≥ 1 is there a polynomial ρ-approximation for general TSPsuppose A is a polynomial ρ-approximation for TSP, ρ a constant (WLOG an integer). We decide HAM-CYCLE with it. given G = (V, E) with |V| = n, build the complete graph G′ on V with costs c(u, v) = 1 if (u, v) ∈ E c(u, v) = ρ·n + 1 if (u, v) ∉ E ← §13's weight. The ONLY choice in the construction, and it depends on ρ size: n(n−1)/2 costs, each writable in O(log(ρn)) bits — polynomial, for constant ρ. c is symmetric but NOT metric. the threshold A is tested against is ρ·n — the budget |V| of §13's plain reduction, multiplied by ρ. G has a Hamiltonian cycle ⟹ that cycle uses n edges of E, so OPT(G′) = n exactly, and A returns ≤ ρ·OPT = ρ·n. G has none ⟹ every tour uses at least one non-edge and n − 1 further edges of weight ≥ 1, so OPT ≥ (ρ·n + 1) + (n − 1) = ρ·n + n > ρ·n, and A, which returns a real tour, returns ≥ OPT > ρ·n. so: A's answer ≤ ρn ⟺ G is Hamiltonian. One call to A decides HAM-CYCLE in polynomial time, so P = NP. ∎ why the "+ 1" is worth carrying: without it the non-Hamiltonian case gives only OPT ≥ ρn + (n − 1), which exceeds ρn only when n ≥ 2. With it the strict inequality holds for EVERY n, with no side condition to state — which is why §13 chose it and why this section uses the same constant rather than a second one. the gap is what matters: the construction makes the two cases differ by MORE than the factor ρ, so an algorithm accurate to within ρ cannot straddle them. Any constant ρ can be defeated because ρ is allowed into the costs. ► and the instance is NOT metric — by design. c(u,w) = ρn + 1 while c(u,v) + c(v,w) = 2 when both are edges. The theorem does not contradict the 3/2 of Christofides; it applies to a family Christofides is not defined on.

The figure builds this instance for a chosen ρ and measures both cases exhaustively, re-deriving §13's numbers. With ρ = 2 the non-edge weight is 2·10 + 1 = 21 on the ten-vertex instance and 2·8 + 1 = 17 on the eight-vertex one. On the eight-vertex graph, which has a Hamiltonian cycle, the optimum over all 2 520 tours is exactly 8 — eight unit edges, equal to n — against a threshold ρ·n of 16. On the Petersen graph, which has ten vertices and no Hamiltonian cycle (verified over all 9!/2 = 181 440 tours), the optimum is 30: an optimal tour is 0-1-2-3-4-7-9-6-8-5 (closing back to 0), which uses nine unit edges — the Hamiltonian path 7-9-6-8-5-0-1-2-3-4 — plus one forced non-edge, 4–7, at 21. Note that 30 = ρ·n + n exactly, so the proof's lower bound is not merely satisfied here but attained. The threshold is ρ·n = 20 and 30 > 20, so an algorithm promising to stay within a factor 2 of the optimum would have to return something at most 16 in the first case and could not possibly return anything at most 20 in the second — reading off the answer to a Hamiltonicity question from a single number. Sweeping ρ on the Petersen instance gives 20 against a threshold of 10 at ρ = 1, 25 against 15 at ρ = 1.5, 40 against 30 at ρ = 3 and 50 against 40 at ρ = 4 — always ρ·n + n against ρ·n, always strictly above. Every constant is defeated, and the figure lets you pick it.

Eight cities: the exhaustive optimum, the double tree, Christofides, nearest neighbour — each tour drawn, its cost measured, its ratio checked against its bound grey = the minimum spanning tree; violet = the matching on the odd-degree vertices; blue = the tour returned; the readout prints the measured cost, the ratio against the optimum found over all 2 520 tours, and an agree / DISAGREE flag on "ratio ≤ the proved bound"; the last option leaves the metric world entirely and measures §13's gap instance, where non-edges cost ρ·n + 1 and the triangle inequality fails by design

Loose ends, each stated with its variant

  • The decision version is NP-complete; the optimisation version is NP-hard but not NP-complete — it is not a decision problem, so it is not in NP at all (§07). This distinction is not pedantry here: every hardness statement in this section is about the optimisation problem, and every one of them is phrased as "unless P = NP", not "because TSP is NP-complete".
  • Asymmetric TSP — c(u, v) ≠ c(v, u), as with one-way streets — is a fourth problem. Neither algorithm above survives, since a spanning tree has no orientation. A constant-factor approximation for the asymmetric metric case was first obtained in 2018, with a very large constant that has since been reduced substantially; the practical algorithms are the solvers of §30. Approximating asymmetric metric TSP better than 75/74 is NP-hard.
  • Euclidean TSP's PTAS partitions the plane recursively and argues that a near-optimal tour can be forced to cross the cut lines only at O(1/ε) designated points per line, which makes a dynamic program over the partition tractable. It is a genuine PTAS, and it is also genuinely impractical for the ε anyone would ask for; the constant hidden in n·(log n)^O(1/ε) is large. It is a theorem about what structure buys, not a recommendation.
  • What people actually run for large Euclidean instances is a constructive heuristic followed by local search — 2-opt, 3-opt, or the Lin-Kernighan family (§28) — which routinely lands within 1–2% of optimal on instances with millions of cities, with no bound of any kind. For exact answers up to tens of thousands of cities, the tool is an integer-programming formulation with cutting planes (§30), not any algorithm on this page.
  • The MST is not a tour and is not a lower bound for everything. c(T) ≤ OPT is the bound this section uses; the reverse is false and the tree is not a solution. The one-tree (Held-Karp) bound sharpens c(T) considerably and is what branch-and-bound codes prune with (§26).

23 Set cover: greedy and the ln n bound

Repeatedly take the set that covers the most still-uncovered elements, and you finish with at most H(d) times the optimal number of sets, where d is the size of the largest set and H(d) = 1 + 1/2 + ⋯ + 1/d is the d-th harmonic number — and no polynomial algorithm can do better than (1 − ε)·ln n unless P = NP, so this three-line greedy is essentially optimal. That pairing is what makes set cover the standard example of a logarithmic approximation: the ratio grows with the input, which looks like a weak result until you learn that nothing better is possible. Two things need care and this section gives both. The bound is H(d), not ln n: H(d) ≤ H(n) ≤ ln n + 1, so "ln n + 1" is the convenient looser restatement and "ln n" its asymptotic shorthand — strictly, ln n slightly underestimates H(n), since ln(n + 1) < H(n). Neither is the theorem. And the charging argument that proves it — every element pays, at the moment it is covered, the reciprocal of how many new elements its set brought in — is the proof technique to learn here, because it recurs throughout approximation.

The problem, and where it has already appeared

SET COVERgiven a finite universe X with |X| = n, and a family F = {S₁, …, S_m} of subsets of X with ⋃F = X find a subfamily C ⊆ F of MINIMUM SIZE with ⋃C = X (the unweighted version; §21's LP note covers weights) decision version "is there a cover of size ≤ k?" NP-complete (3-SAT ≤ₚ VERTEX-COVER ≤ₚ SET-COVER, §12 — vertex cover IS set cover with X = the edges and S_v = the edges incident on v) optimisation NP-hard. Sets may overlap; an element may be covered many times and is paid for once. d = max |Sᵢ| the largest set. For the vertex-cover special case, d = Δ, the maximum degree.

Greedy Algorithms introduces this problem as the canonical case where the greedy choice property fails — there is no exchange argument, the greedy answer is genuinely not optimal on the instance below — and defers the guarantee to here. This is that guarantee. The problem is also the abstract form of a great many real questions: choose the fewest sensors covering every zone, the fewest test cases exercising every branch, the fewest features covering every training example, the fewest data-centre regions covering every latency requirement.

The algorithm, and a worked instance

GREEDY-SET-COVER(X, F)U ← X the still-uncovered elements C ← ∅ while U ≠ ∅: select S ∈ F maximising |S ∩ U| ← the greedy choice: the most NEW elements, not the biggest set U ← U − S C ← C ∪ {S} return C running time O(n·m·min(n, m)) naively; O(∑|Sᵢ|) with bucket queues by current gain — near-linear in the input size. termination every iteration covers at least one new element (the family covers X), so at most n iterations.

The instance the figure uses has n = 12 elements and six sets: S₁ = {1,2,3,4,5,6}, S₂ = {5,6,8,9}, S₃ = {1,4,7,10}, S₄ = {2,5,7,8,11}, S₅ = {3,6,9,12}, S₆ = {10,11}. Greedy's trace, with the gain measured at each step:

stepgains |Sᵢ ∩ U| for S₁ … S₆picksnew elementsprice each paysU after
16, 4, 4, 5, 4, 2S₁1 2 3 4 5 61/6{7,8,9,10,11,12}
20, 2, 2, 3, 2, 2S₄7 8 111/3{9,10,12}
30, 1, 1, 0, 2, 1S₅9 121/2{10}
40, 0, 1, 0, 0, 1S₃ (tie with S₆, lower index)101∅

Greedy returns four sets. Exhaustive search over all 2⁶ = 64 subfamilies says the minimum cover has 3 sets and is unique: {S₃, S₄, S₅}, whose union is {1,4,7,10} ∪ {2,5,7,8,11} ∪ {3,6,9,12} — all twelve elements, each covered exactly once except 7. Greedy never picks that cover because its first move is dictated by S₁'s size six, and S₁ is in no optimal cover at all. The measured ratio is 4/3 = 1.333, against a promised H(d) = H(6) = 2.45, so the bound is loose here by nearly a factor of two. (For comparison, ln n = ln 12 = 2.4849 — very close to H(6) on this instance by coincidence, and the section's point is that they are different quantities.)

The proof, by charging

The whole proof is the idea of a price. When greedy picks a set that brings in g new elements, it spends one set and buys g elements, so charge each of those g elements a price of 1/g. Then the total price paid by all elements is exactly the number of sets greedy used — the cost has been spread over the elements rather than the sets. Now bound, for any single set S in the family, the total price its own elements carry; because the optimal cover's sets together contain every element, summing that bound over the optimal cover bounds the total price, hence greedy's size.

the priceslet S₁, S₂, …, S_k be the sets greedy picks, in order, and let Uᵢ = X − (S₁ ∪ ⋯ ∪ Sᵢ) (U₀ = X). for each element x, let i(x) be the step at which x is first covered. define c_x = 1 / |S_{i(x)} ∩ U_{i(x)−1}| — one over the GAIN of the set that first covered x immediately: ∑_{x ∈ X} c_x = ∑_{i=1}^{k} |Sᵢ ∩ U_{i−1}| · (1 / |Sᵢ ∩ U_{i−1}|) = ∑_{i=1}^{k} 1 = k = |greedy cover| ► the k sets have been repackaged as n prices with the same total. On the worked instance: 6 elements at 1/6, 3 at 1/3, 2 at 1/2, 1 at 1 → 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 = 4 = the number of sets greedy used. ✓ the key lemma: for EVERY set S ∈ F, ∑_{x ∈ S} c_x ≤ H(|S|)fix S ∈ F, and write uᵢ = |S ∩ Uᵢ| = how many elements of S are still uncovered after greedy's i-th pick; u₀ = |S|, and u_k = 0. Let ℓ be the smallest index with u_ℓ = 0. At step i exactly (u_{i−1} − u_i) elements of S are covered for the first time, each at price 1 / |Sᵢ ∩ U_{i−1}|. So ∑_{x ∈ S} c_x = ∑_{i=1}^{ℓ} (u_{i−1} − u_i) · 1 / |Sᵢ ∩ U_{i−1}| GREEDY'S CHOICE enters here, and only here: at step i, S was available and Sᵢ was preferred, so |Sᵢ ∩ U_{i−1}| ≥ |S ∩ U_{i−1}| = u_{i−1} ← the ONE inequality the algorithm supplies ∑_{x ∈ S} c_x ≤ ∑_{i=1}^{ℓ} (u_{i−1} − u_i) / u_{i−1} ≤ ∑_{i=1}^{ℓ} ∑_{j = u_i + 1}^{u_{i−1}} 1/j since 1/u_{i−1} ≤ 1/j for every j ≤ u_{i−1} = ∑_{i=1}^{ℓ} ( H(u_{i−1}) − H(u_i) ) the sum telescopes = H(u₀) − H(u_ℓ) = H(|S|) − H(0) = H(|S|) ∎ the theoremlet C* be an optimal cover. Every element lies in at least one set of C*, so |greedy| = ∑_{x ∈ X} c_x ≤ ∑_{S ∈ C*} ∑_{x ∈ S} c_x (double-counting overlaps only INCREASES the right side) ≤ ∑_{S ∈ C*} H(|S|) by the lemma ≤ |C*| · H(d), d = max |Sᵢ| ∎ GREEDY-SET-COVER is an H(d)-approximation, and since d ≤ n, also an H(n)-approximation. on the worked instance: the three optimal sets carry prices 1.667 + 1.333 + 1.333 = 4.333 ≥ 4 ✓, and each is under its own H(|S|): 1.667 ≤ H(4) = 2.083, 1.333 ≤ H(5) = 2.283, 1.333 ≤ H(4) = 2.083 ✓ — the lemma, verified termwise.

H(d) is not ln n — what the relation actually is

harmonic numbers against the logarithmH(k) = 1 + 1/2 + ⋯ + 1/k satisfies ln k < H(k) ≤ ln k + 1 for every k ≥ 1 (equality at k = 1) more precisely H(k) = ln k + γ + 1/(2k) − 1/(12k²) + ⋯ , γ = 0.5772156649… (Euler-Mascheroni) so the chain is: |greedy| ≤ H(d)·OPT ≤ H(n)·OPT ≤ (ln n + 1)·OPT ► "greedy is a ln n-approximation" is a LOOSENING of the theorem in two places at once: d is replaced by n, and H(n) by ln n + 1 (or, more casually, by ln n, which is smaller than H(n) and so not even an upper bound on H(n) — the casual statement is false as an inequality and true as an asymptotic). ► measured on the worked instance: H(6) = 2.4500, H(12) = 3.1032, ln 12 = 2.4849, ln 12 + 1 = 3.4849. Note H(6) < ln 12 < H(12) < ln 12 + 1 — the two quantities interleave, which is exactly why they must not be used interchangeably. ► the sharpest known statement: greedy's worst-case ratio is ln n − ln ln n + Θ(1).

The family on which greedy really is Θ(log n) times the optimum

The H(d) bound would be a curiosity if greedy never approached it. It does. The construction partitions the same universe in two different ways and makes the greedy's local view prefer the wrong partition at every single step, by exactly one element.

the instance, for a parameter kpartition X, the "greedy bait" way: S₁, S₂, …, S_k pairwise disjoint, |Sᵢ| = 2ⁱ. so n = |X| = 2 + 4 + 8 + ⋯ + 2ᵏ = 2^(k+1) − 2. partition X, the "optimal" way: T₁, T₂ each taking exactly HALF of every Sᵢ, so |T₁| = |T₂| = 1 + 2 + 4 + ⋯ + 2^(k−1) = 2ᵏ − 1. OPT = 2: {T₁, T₂} covers X. (It is optimal: no single set is all of X.) GREEDY = k: step 1 — the gains are |S_k| = 2ᵏ against |T₁| = |T₂| = 2ᵏ − 1. S_k wins BY ONE. step 2 — S_k is gone; each Tⱼ has 2ᵏ − 1 − 2^(k−1) = 2^(k−1) − 1 left, S_{k−1} has 2^(k−1). S_{k−1} wins BY ONE. … and so on, down to S₁ with 2 against 1. greedy takes S_k, S_{k−1}, …, S₁ — all k of them — and never touches T₁ or T₂. ratio = k / 2 = (log₂(n + 2) − 1) / 2 = Θ(log n). The H(d) bound with d = 2ᵏ gives H(2ᵏ)·2 ≈ (k·ln 2 + γ)·2, about 1.4k — so greedy's k is within a constant of the bound, and the bound is tight up to that constant. measured, by running the real algorithm and an exhaustive optimum over all 2^(k+2) subfamilies k n = |X| sets greedy OPT ratio d = max|S| H(d) H(d)·OPT 3 14 5 3 2 1.500 8 2.718 5.44 4 30 6 4 2 2.000 16 3.381 6.76 5 62 7 5 2 2.500 32 4.058 8.12 6 126 8 6 2 3.000 64 4.744 9.49 7 254 9 7 2 3.500 128 5.433 10.87 the ratio grows like ½·log₂ n while OPT stays at 2 — there is no constant ρ that covers this family, so this algorithm does not place set cover in APX. (Nothing does, unless P = NP — see the threshold below.)
Greedy set cover, one pick per step: the gain measured for every set, the price charged to each element, and the exhaustive optimum beside it the universe is a grid of elements; a column of bars shows every set's current gain |Sᵢ ∩ U|, measured; the picked set is amber and its newly covered elements turn green and are labelled with the price 1/gain they pay; the sets of the exhaustive optimum are outlined in blue; the readout sums the prices (which must equal the number of sets greedy used), checks the lemma ∑ c ≤ H(|S|) for every set, and reports the measured ratio against H(d)

The matching lower bound, and what it makes greedy

  • No (1 − ε)·ln n-approximation unless P = NP, for every fixed ε > 0 — so no polynomial algorithm beats ln n even by a constant factor. The result was first proved in 1998 under the weaker hypothesis NP ⊄ DTIME(n^(log log n)) and strengthened in 2014 to plain P ≠ NP. Since greedy achieves ln n − ln ln n + Θ(1), the algorithm and the lower bound agree to within lower-order terms: greedy set cover is optimal, as far as anyone can ever prove without settling P versus NP. That is an unusual and satisfying state for a three-line algorithm.
  • Set cover is not in APX — no constant ratio is achievable, unless P = NP — which is why the double-counting in §20's taxonomy puts it outside the constant-factor world entirely, alongside maximum clique.
  • Bounded set sizes rescue it. If every set has at most d elements, the bound is H(d), a constant, so the problem is in APX for each fixed d. The vertex-cover special case is the extreme: every "set" is a vertex's edge list, d = Δ, and on a bounded-degree graph greedy is a constant-factor approximation. The other special structure that helps is the element's frequency f — the maximum number of sets containing any one element — which gives an f-approximation by LP rounding; for vertex cover f = 2, and that is §21's ratio of 2, arrived at from the other side.
  • Weighted set cover — minimise ∑ w(S) — keeps the same bound with the greedy rule changed to "maximise the number of new elements per unit weight", i.e. pick S minimising w(S) / |S ∩ U|. The charging proof goes through verbatim with c_x = w(S)/|S ∩ U|.
  • Maximum coverage — pick k sets covering as many elements as possible — is the maximisation twin, and the same greedy gives 1 − 1/e ≈ 0.632 of the optimum, i.e. ratio e/(e−1) ≈ 1.582, which is also the best possible unless P = NP. That result, and its submodularity proof, is the reason greedy feature selection has a guarantee at all.

24 MAX-3-SAT: 7/8 by coin flip, then derandomised

Set every variable by an independent fair coin flip. Each clause of three distinct literals fails only if all three of its literals come out false, which happens with probability (1/2)³ = 1/8, so each clause is satisfied with probability exactly 7/8 and the expected number satisfied is exactly 7m/8 — an 8/7-approximation from an algorithm that never reads the formula. Two further facts turn that party trick into one of the most striking results on this page. The expectation can be achieved deterministically, by walking the variables and at each step taking the value with the larger conditional expectation — the method of conditional expectations, which converts the probabilistic argument into a simple polynomial algorithm. And the ratio cannot be improved: satisfying more than (7/8 + ε)·m clauses in polynomial time, for any fixed ε > 0, is NP-hard. So the best polynomial algorithm for MAX-3-SAT, up to ε, is flip coins and do not look at the input — unless P = NP.

The problem, and the hypothesis the argument needs

MAX-3-SATgiven a 3-CNF formula φ with m clauses over n variables, where EVERY CLAUSE HAS EXACTLY THREE LITERALS ON THREE DISTINCT VARIABLES (so no clause contains both x and ¬x, and none repeats a variable) find a truth assignment satisfying as many clauses as possible. MAXIMISATION, so §20's live ratio is OPT / C. ► the distinctness hypothesis is not decoration. A clause (x ∨ ¬x ∨ y) is satisfied with probability 1, which is harmless. A clause (x ∨ x ∨ y) is really the 2-clause (x ∨ y) and is satisfied with probability only 3/4 — BELOW 7/8 — so a formula made of such clauses has E[satisfied] = 3m/4, and the 8/7 guarantee fails (only 4/3 survives). So the BOUND, and not only the exact value 7m/8 and the tightness of the whole section, needs the hypothesis, and the figure checks it on every instance it loads before it quotes a number. ► the decision version "are ALL m clauses satisfiable?" is 3-SAT, NP-complete (§09). MAX-3-SAT is its optimisation form and is NP-hard; it is not a decision problem, so it is not NP-complete (§07). ► the hard cases are the UNSATISFIABLE formulas. If φ is satisfiable the optimum is m — but FINDING the satisfying assignment is still NP-hard, and, remarkably, the 7/8 threshold below holds even when you are PROMISED the formula is satisfiable.

The randomised algorithm and its expectation

RANDOM-3-CNF(φ)for each variable xᵢ independently: set xᵢ ← TRUE with probability 1/2, FALSE with probability 1/2 return the assignment time O(n). It does not read the clauses. the analysis — one indicator per clause, and linearity of expectationlet Yⱼ = 1 if clause Cⱼ is satisfied, 0 otherwise. Let Y = ∑_{j=1}^{m} Yⱼ = the number satisfied. Cⱼ has three literals on three DISTINCT variables, so the three literal values are INDEPENDENT fair coins. Cⱼ is unsatisfied ⟺ all three literals are false ⟹ Pr[Cⱼ unsatisfied] = (1/2)·(1/2)·(1/2) = 1/8 Pr[Yⱼ = 1] = 7/8, E[Yⱼ] = 7/8 E[Y] = E[ ∑ Yⱼ ] = ∑ E[Yⱼ] = 7m/8 ← linearity of expectation: NO independence between CLAUSES is needed, and there is none — clauses share variables freely. ratio: OPT ≤ m always, so OPT / E[Y] ≤ m / (7m/8) = 8/7 ≈ 1.1429. in the other convention, the algorithm satisfies at least 7/8 of the optimum, in expectation. the probabilistic method: an assignment achieving the expectation must EXISTY is an average of the values it takes, so it cannot be strictly below its own mean everywhere: some assignment satisfies at least ⌈7m/8⌉ clauses. ► a pure EXISTENCE proof — it names no assignment. The derandomisation below turns it into an algorithm. ► and since 7m/8 may not be an integer, the honest floor is ⌈7m/8⌉, not 7m/8.

The instance where the bound is exactly attained

Take all eight clauses of three literals over three variables: (¬x₁∨¬x₂∨¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁∨¬x₂∨x₃) ∧ (¬x₁∨x₂∨¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁∨x₂∨x₃) ∧ (x₁∨¬x₂∨¬x₃) ∧ (x₁∨¬x₂∨x₃) ∧ (x₁∨x₂∨¬x₃) ∧ (x₁∨x₂∨x₃). Every assignment falsifies exactly one clause — the one whose three literals are precisely the negations of the assignment's three values — and satisfies the other seven. So this formula is unsatisfiable, the optimum is 7, the distribution of Y is a single spike at 7 rather than a spread, and E[Y] = 7 = 7·8/8 = 7m/8 exactly. The 7/8 is attained, not merely approached: on this instance every run of the randomised algorithm returns an optimal assignment, and there is no assignment that does better. Exhaustive search over all 2³ = 8 assignments confirms all of it, and the figure re-derives it at load.

A richer instance shows the distribution the analysis is really about. A random 3-CNF on six variables with twenty clauses (seeded, and checked to be well-formed) has E[Y] = 7·20/8 = 17.5, and the exact distribution over all 2⁶ = 64 assignments is 14: 2, 15: 2, 16: 7, 17: 18, 18: 23, 19: 10, 20: 2 — mean 1120/64 = 17.5 exactly, matching the theorem, with a spread of six clauses around it. Two of the 64 assignments satisfy all twenty, so this formula is satisfiable and its optimum is 20; the randomised algorithm's expectation of 17.5 is 87.5% of that, and 4 000 seeded trials of the real routine give an empirical mean of 17.52.

Derandomisation by the method of conditional expectations

The randomised algorithm gives a guarantee in expectation. Converting it into a deterministic algorithm with the same guarantee takes one idea: the expectation, conditioned on some variables already fixed, is computable exactly in polynomial time; and since the two children average to the parent, at least one child is at least as good as the parent. Walk down the tree, always taking the better child, and the expectation never falls below where it started — 7m/8. At the leaf nothing is random any more, so the "expectation" is the number of clauses satisfied.

the conditional expectation, computed exactlygiven a partial assignment fixing x₁ … x_k and leaving the rest random, E[ Y | x₁ … x_k ] = ∑_{j=1}^{m} Pr[ Cⱼ satisfied | x₁ … x_k ] Pr[ Cⱼ | … ] = 1 if some fixed literal of Cⱼ is already TRUE = 1 − 2^(−fⱼ) otherwise, where fⱼ = how many of Cⱼ's literals are still FREE (fⱼ = 0 means the clause is already dead: 1 − 1 = 0) cost: O(m) per evaluation, O(n·m) for the whole walk. Exact rational arithmetic — no sampling, no estimation. GREEDY-CONDITIONAL-EXPECTATION(φ)for i = 1 … n: a ← E[ Y | x₁ … x_{i−1}, xᵢ = FALSE ] b ← E[ Y | x₁ … x_{i−1}, xᵢ = TRUE ] fix xᵢ to whichever of the two is larger (ties: either; this page takes TRUE) return the assignment the invariant, which is the whole proof: E[Y | x₁ … x_{i−1}] = ½·a + ½·b ← the parent is the AVERAGE of its two children so max(a, b) ≥ E[Y | x₁ … x_{i−1}] ← taking the better child never DECREASES the expectation hence E[Y | x₁ … x_n] ≥ E[Y | x₁ … x_{n−1}] ≥ ⋯ ≥ E[Y] = 7m/8 and with every variable fixed, E[Y | x₁ … x_n] = the actual number of clauses satisfied. ⟹ the returned assignment satisfies at least ⌈7m/8⌉ clauses. Deterministic, O(n·m). ∎

Run on the eight-clause instance, the walk is a straight line: every conditional expectation, at every step and for both values, is exactly 7 — the formula is perfectly symmetric, so no choice can do better or worse, and the invariant holds with equality throughout. The returned assignment satisfies 7 clauses, which is ⌈7·8/8⌉ = 7 and also the exhaustive optimum. Run on the twenty-clause random instance it is more interesting, and the invariant is visible in every row:

stepconditional expectation beforeE[Y | …, xᵢ = F]E[Y | …, xᵢ = T]average of the twotakesafter
——————17.500 = 7m/8
x₁17.50017.87517.12517.500 ✓F17.875
x₂17.87517.62518.12517.875 ✓T18.125
x₃18.12517.62518.62518.125 ✓T18.625
x₄18.62518.75018.50018.625 ✓F18.750
x₅18.75018.50019.00018.750 ✓T19.000
x₆19.00019.00019.00019.000 ✓T (tie)19.000

The expectation climbs 17.5 → 17.875 → 18.125 → 18.625 → 18.75 → 19 → 19, never falls, and the final value 19 is exactly the number of clauses the returned assignment x = (F, T, T, F, T, T) satisfies — checked directly, not inferred. The guarantee demanded ⌈17.5⌉ = 18 and the algorithm delivered 19; the exhaustive optimum is 20, so the achieved ratio is 20/19 = 1.053, comfortably inside the promised 8/7 = 1.143. Note what the derandomisation does not promise: it does not find the optimum, and on a satisfiable formula it can and here does miss a full assignment. It promises ⌈7m/8⌉, and that is all it promises.

The coin-flip algorithm's distribution, measured over thousands of runs, beside the derandomised walk that reaches the same expectation with no coins at all left: a histogram of clauses satisfied over the chosen number of seeded random runs, with the exact distribution over all 2ⁿ assignments drawn behind it, the computed expectation 7m/8 marked in amber and the exhaustive optimum in green; right: the conditional-expectation walk stepped variable by variable, showing both children, the average identity, and the running value; the readout checks empirical mean ≈ 7m/8, the monotonicity of the walk, and that the walk's final value equals the clauses actually satisfied

Why this is one of the most striking facts on the page

Look at what the threshold says next to the algorithm. Håstad's theorem: for every fixed ε > 0 it is NP-hard to find an assignment satisfying more than (7/8 + ε)·m clauses of a 3-CNF whose clauses have three distinct variables — and this holds even on instances promised to be satisfiable. The algorithm above: satisfies 7m/8 in expectation, deterministically after derandomisation, in O(nm) time, without using any structure of the formula beyond counting its clauses. The two meet exactly. There is no room between them — not a constant factor, not a lower-order term, nothing.

The consequence is worth stating plainly. For MAX-3-SAT, everything an algorithm could possibly learn by examining the formula is worth nothing, asymptotically, beyond what a coin already gives you — unless P = NP. All the propagation, all the clause learning, all the structural insight that makes the solvers of §30 devastating on real instances buys exactly zero guaranteed improvement in the worst case. That is the sharpest illustration available of the difference between worst-case approximability and practical performance, and it is the reason §28–§30 exist as separate sections rather than as footnotes to this one.

It also settles MAX-3-SAT's place in §20's taxonomy: it is in APX (ratio 8/7) and it has no PTAS unless P = NP (a (1 + ε)-approximation for small enough ε would beat 7/8). MAX-3-SAT is therefore the canonical APX-complete problem, and gap-preserving reductions from it are how most other inapproximability thresholds are obtained.

Neighbouring problems, each with its own number

  • MAX-SAT with clauses of arbitrary length is harder to approximate, not easier: a unit clause is satisfied by a coin with probability only 1/2, so the naive bound degrades to 1/2. Better algorithms — combining the random assignment with a linear-programming relaxation and randomised rounding, then taking the better of the two — reach 3/4 of the optimum in the classic treatment, and semidefinite relaxations push that to about 0.797; and since MAX-E3-SAT is a special case of MAX-SAT, nothing can beat 7/8 unless P = NP.
  • MAX-2-SAT: a random assignment satisfies each two-literal clause with probability 3/4. That is beatable — a semidefinite-programming relaxation with hyperplane rounding reaches about 0.940 — and the threshold is 21/22 ≈ 0.955, so a gap remains. Note the boundary this straddles: deciding 2-SAT is in P (§16), while maximising over 2-CNF is NP-hard. Optimisation versions can be hard when the decision version is easy.
  • MAX-Ek-SAT for k literals per clause: the same coin gives 1 − 2^(−k), and that too is exactly the threshold for every k ≥ 3. The trivial algorithm is optimal for the whole family — unless P = NP.
  • The method of conditional expectations is general. Any time a randomised algorithm's objective has a conditional expectation you can evaluate in polynomial time, the same walk derandomises it — it is not a trick specific to satisfiability. The obstacle in other settings is evaluating the conditional expectation, which is why pessimistic estimators (an efficiently computable bound that behaves like the expectation) exist.
  • The probabilistic method more broadly. "The average is at least t, therefore something is at least t" proves the existence of objects nobody can construct — Ramsey-graph lower bounds, error-correcting codes, expanders. This section is the case where the existence proof happens to be constructive with one extra idea, which is rarer than it looks.

25 The knapsack FPTAS: rounding the values

Knapsack has a dynamic program whose table is indexed by value and whose running time is Θ(n·V) — pseudo-polynomial, because V is exponential in the number of bits used to write the values down. Divide every value by K = ε·v_max/n and round down, and the table shrinks to O(n²/ε) columns while the answer loses at most n·K = ε·v_max ≤ ε·OPT: a (1 − ε)-guarantee in O(n³/ε) time, polynomial in n and in 1/ε together. That is a fully polynomial-time approximation scheme, the strongest class in §20's taxonomy, and this is the standard example of one. The mechanism is worth stating as a slogan, because it is the only known route to an FPTAS: an FPTAS is a pseudo-polynomial algorithm with the numbers scaled down. It follows that a problem has an FPTAS essentially only where §14 found a pseudo-polynomial dynamic program — which is why knapsack and subset sum have one and TSP, 3-colouring and vertex cover cannot (§20's strong-NP-hardness theorem).

Two dynamic programs, and why the second one is the useful one here

Dynamic Programming builds the weight-indexed table: m[i][w] = the best value achievable from the first i items within capacity w, filled in Θ(n·W) time. That is the one everyone learns, and it is the wrong one to scale, because the guarantee we want is about value and rounding the weights would break feasibility — a rounded-down weight can let a set into the knapsack that does not fit. So transpose the table.

the VALUE-indexed dynamic programitems 1 … n with weights wᵢ > 0 and values vᵢ > 0; capacity W. Let V = ∑ᵢ vᵢ (an upper bound on any value). m[i][z] = the MINIMUM TOTAL WEIGHT of a subset of items 1 … i whose values sum to EXACTLY z (∞ if none) m[0][0] = 0, m[0][z] = ∞ for z > 0 m[i][z] = min( m[i−1][z], m[i−1][z − vᵢ] + wᵢ ) the second term only when z ≥ vᵢ answer = max { z : m[n][z] ≤ W } table (n + 1)·(V + 1) entries, one min per entry ⟹ Θ(n·V) time, and Θ(V) space with one rolling row. ► measured on the five-item instance w = ⟨3, 5, 7, 4, 9⟩, v = ⟨20, 30, 35, 26, 50⟩, W = 15: V = 161, so the table is 6 × 162 = 972 states and the fill does 5 × 162 = 810 cell computations. It returns 85, from items 1, 2, 3, at weight exactly 15 — which an exhaustive search over all 2⁵ = 32 subsets independently confirms. ► this is PSEUDO-POLYNOMIAL, not polynomial: V is written in about log₂V bits (§02), so Θ(n·V) is Θ(n·2^(bits)). Doubling the number of digits in the values SQUARES the running time.

The scaling, and why it is safe

The pseudo-polynomial cost is driven by the magnitude of the values, and magnitude is exactly the thing we are willing to be sloppy about. Choose a granularity K, replace each value by how many whole K's it contains, and solve the smaller problem exactly. Each item loses less than K, so a set of at most n items loses less than n·K — and choosing K proportional to ε·v_max/n makes that loss a fraction ε of something we can bound below by the optimum.

KNAPSACK-FPTAS(w, v, W, ε)0. discard every item with wᵢ > W it can never be used; after this, OPT ≥ v_max 1. v_max ← max vᵢ ; K ← ε·v_max / n 2. v′ᵢ ← ⌊ vᵢ / K ⌋ for every i ROUND DOWN. Weights are untouched, so feasibility is exact. 3. run the value-indexed DP above on (w, v′, W), exactly 4. return the ITEM SET it chooses — and report its value under the ORIGINAL v, not under v′ step 4 matters: the guarantee below is about the TRUE value of the set chosen, and the true value is at least K·∑v′. the approximation guaranteewrite S = the set the scheme returns, S* = an optimal set, and note for every item K·v′ᵢ ≤ vᵢ < K·v′ᵢ + K. (1) S maximises ∑ v′ among feasible sets (the DP is EXACT on v′), and S* is feasible, so ∑_{i∈S} v′ᵢ ≥ ∑_{i∈S*} v′ᵢ (2) value(S) = ∑_{i∈S} vᵢ ≥ K·∑_{i∈S} v′ᵢ by the left half of the bracket ≥ K·∑_{i∈S*} v′ᵢ by (1) > ∑_{i∈S*} (vᵢ − K) by the right half = OPT − |S*|·K ≥ OPT − n·K (3) n·K = n · ε·v_max / n = ε·v_max ≤ ε·OPT ← needs OPT ≥ v_max, which step 0 guaranteed ⟹ value(S) > (1 − ε)·OPT i.e. relative error < ε, i.e. ratio OPT / value(S) < 1/(1 − ε). ∎ ► in §20's ρ ≥ 1 convention that is 1/(1 − ε), not 1 + ε. The two are interchangeable at a constant cost in ε: running the scheme with ε′ = ε/(1 + ε) gives ratio exactly 1 + ε, and multiplies the running time by (1 + ε) ≤ 2. This page states the guarantee as "relative error at most ε", which is the form the figure measures. ► step 0 is not a technicality. Without it v_max may exceed OPT, (3) fails, and the scheme has no guarantee. the running time — and what "fully polynomial" meanseach scaled value v′ᵢ = ⌊vᵢ/K⌋ ≤ v_max/K = v_max·n / (ε·v_max) = n/ε their sum V′ = ∑ v′ᵢ ≤ n · n/ε = n²/ε the table (n + 1)·(V′ + 1) = O(n³/ε) entries, each filled with one comparison TIME O(n³/ε) SPACE O(n²/ε) with a rolling row FULLY polynomial = polynomial in the input size n AND in 1/ε. Halving ε DOUBLES the work — a constant factor. Contrast a bare PTAS such as Euclidean TSP's, where halving ε can square the exponent; and contrast the exact DP, whose cost Θ(n·V) depends on the VALUES, which the scheme has removed from the bound entirely. ► note what disappeared: n³/ε contains no vᵢ, no W, no V. The scheme's cost depends only on how many items there are and how accurate you asked to be. That is the whole point of scaling.

Measured, on the five-item instance

With v_max = 50 and n = 5, the granularity is K = 10ε. At ε = 0.2, K = 2 and the values ⟨20, 30, 35, 26, 50⟩ become ⟨10, 15, 17, 13, 25⟩, summing to 80 instead of 161 — the table falls from 972 states to 486 and the fill from 810 cell computations to 405, an exact halving, and the scheme still returns items 1, 2, 3 for the true optimum of 85: relative error 0, against a permitted 0.2. The rounding only starts to cost something at ε = 0.6, where K = 6, the values become ⟨3, 5, 5, 4, 8⟩, and items 2 and 3 become indistinguishable at 5 apiece although they are really worth 30 and 35. The DP then prefers the lighter pair {2, 5} (scaled value 13 at weight 14) over {1, 2, 3} (scaled value 13 at weight 15) and returns a true value of 80 — relative error 5/85 = 0.0588, one tenth of the permitted 0.6, with the table down to 156 states and 130 cell computations.

the sweep, every number measured by running the routine under a counterfive-item instance (V = 161, exact table 972 states / 810 cells, OPT = 85) ε K scaled values V′ states cells value relative error permitted within? 0.1 1.0 20 30 35 26 50 161 972 810 85 0.0000 0.10 ✓ 0.3 3.0 6 10 11 8 16 51 312 260 85 0.0000 0.30 ✓ 0.5 5.0 4 6 7 5 10 32 198 165 85 0.0000 0.50 ✓ 0.6 6.0 3 5 5 4 8 25 156 130 80 0.0588 0.60 ✓ 0.8 8.0 2 3 4 3 6 18 114 95 76 0.1059 0.80 ✓ 1.0 10.0 2 3 3 2 5 15 96 80 80 0.0588 1.00 ✓ ten-item instance (seeded; OPT = 374 by the exact DP, confirmed over all 2¹⁰ = 1 024 subsets) ε cells value relative error permitted 0.05 11 320 374 0.0000 0.05 0.10 5 630 373 0.0027 0.10 0.20 2 790 373 0.0027 0.20 0.30 1 840 373 0.0027 0.30 0.40 1 380 373 0.0027 0.40 0.50 1 100 369 0.0134 0.50 0.70 770 369 0.0134 0.70 0.90 590 368 0.0161 0.90 ► the work falls roughly as 1/ε, exactly as the bound says (halving ε from 0.1 to 0.05 doubles the cells, 5 630 → 11 320); the error stays an ORDER OF MAGNITUDE below what the guarantee permits; and the error is NOT monotone in ε (the five-item run is worse at 0.8 than at 1.0) — the bound is one-sided, and nothing promises that a coarser grid always hurts more. The figure flags any row where measured error exceeds ε; none ever does.
Turn the accuracy dial: the value table shrinks, the work falls, and the measured error stays under the guarantee top: the scaled value axis drawn as cells, so you can see the table narrow as ε grows, with the cells actually computed counted under a counter; bottom: the achieved value against the exact optimum, and the measured relative error plotted against the ε line — a point above the line would be a violated guarantee and is flagged DISAGREE; the exact optimum is cross-checked against exhaustive search over all 2ⁿ subsets

What generalises, and what does not

  • Subset sum gets the same treatment in a slightly different dress: keep a list of achievable sums and repeatedly trim it, deleting any element within a factor 1 + δ of one already kept, with δ = ε/(2n). The list stays of length O((n/ε)·log(target)) and the answer loses at most a factor 1 + ε. Trimming and rounding are the same idea — collapse values that are close — and §14's pseudo-polynomial DP is the thing being collapsed in both cases.
  • The cheap alternative, when ε is not the question. A 2-approximation for knapsack needs no dynamic program at all: sort by value density vᵢ/wᵢ, take the greedy prefix that fits, and return the better of that prefix and the single most valuable item. One of the two is always at least half the optimum, because the fractional relaxation's optimum is the greedy prefix plus a fraction of the next item (Greedy Algorithms), and that optimum bounds OPT above. Use the FPTAS when the caller names an ε; use the greedy when they do not.
  • Why no FPTAS for TSP, colouring or vertex cover. §20's theorem: a strongly NP-hard problem with a polynomially bounded integer objective has no FPTAS unless P = NP, because running the scheme at ε < 1/OPT would make it exact in pseudo-polynomial time. Those three problems are strongly NP-hard — their hardness does not come from big numbers — so there is nothing to scale. Knapsack's hardness does come from big numbers, which is precisely §14's pseudo-polynomial observation read forwards.
  • The knapsack FPTAS is not how anyone solves large knapsacks. Branch and bound with the fractional relaxation as the bound (§26), or an integer-programming solver (§30), routinely handles instances with hundreds of thousands of items exactly. The FPTAS is the structural result — it locates knapsack in the easiest approximation class there is — and the algorithm you would actually deploy only when a hard accuracy contract has to be met with a provable bound.
  • Where the ε goes in practice. The running time O(n³/ε) is polynomial in 1/ε, but ε = 10⁻⁶ still means a million-fold multiplier. "Fully polynomial" is a statement about the shape of the dependence, not a promise that small ε is cheap. Measure before promising.

26 Exact exponential algorithms: branch and bound, DP over subsets

Giving up the polynomial bound is a design decision, not a surrender: an algorithm that is exponential in a small base, or that prunes most of the tree it could build, solves instances that brute force cannot look at — and it returns a provably optimal answer, which no approximation or heuristic on this page does. Three techniques carry almost all of the load. Backtracking walks the tree of partial solutions depth-first and abandons a branch the moment a partial solution is already infeasible. Branch and bound adds a computable lower bound on the cost of completing a partial solution and abandons the branch when that bound already reaches the best complete solution found so far. Dynamic programming over subsets replaces the n! orders of a permutation problem with the 2ⁿ subsets of a set, which is an enormous saving and also the standard illustration of the trade this section is about: Held–Karp turns the travelling salesman's Θ(n!) into Θ(n²·2ⁿ), and is still exponential. The section ends on the one thing that goes wrong silently, and the figure demonstrates it: a "lower bound" that is not a lower bound makes the search faster and the answer wrong, and nothing in the search notices.

Backtracking: depth-first search over partial solutions

A backtracking algorithm needs three components: a way to extend a partial solution by one decision, a test that looks at a partial solution and returns one of three verdicts — failure (no completion exists), success (this is already a solution), or uncertain — and a traversal order. The traversal is a depth-first search of the tree whose root is the empty partial solution and whose leaves are complete ones; the test is what makes it more than brute force, because a failure verdict at a node of depth d removes the entire subtree below it, and for 3-SAT that subtree contains 2^(n − d) assignments. All the intelligence lives in the test and in which decision to branch on next.

the backtracking skeletonBACKTRACK(P₀) S ← {P₀} the active subproblems while S ≠ ∅: choose P ∈ S and remove it WHICH subproblem: a policy expand P into subproblems P₁ … P_k WHICH decision to branch on: a policy for each Pᵢ: if test(Pᵢ) = SUCCESS: return Pᵢ if test(Pᵢ) = FAILURE: discard Pᵢ ← the whole subtree below Pᵢ vanishes else: add Pᵢ to S return "no solution" for SAT a subproblem IS a formula: plug the fixed variables in, delete satisfied clauses, strike out falsified literals. test = FAILURE if some clause is now EMPTY; SUCCESS if no clauses remain; UNCERTAIN otherwise. Choosing the subproblem with the SHORTEST clause and branching on a variable in it is the classical policy: if that clause is a singleton, one of the two branches dies at once. the worked instance — φ on four variables, unsatisfiableφ = (w ∨ x ∨ y ∨ z) ∧ (w ∨ ¬x) ∧ (x ∨ ¬y) ∧ (y ∨ ¬z) ∧ (z ∨ ¬w) ∧ (¬w ∨ ¬z) branch on w: w = 1 ⟹ (z ∨ ¬w) becomes (z) and (¬w ∨ ¬z) becomes (¬z) two singletons that contradict ⟹ one unit step gives the EMPTY clause: FAILURE, immediately w = 0 ⟹ (w ∨ ¬x) becomes (¬x) ⟹ x = 0 (x ∨ ¬y) becomes (¬y) ⟹ y = 0 (y ∨ ¬z) becomes (¬z) ⟹ z = 0 (w ∨ x ∨ y ∨ z) is now EMPTY: FAILURE both branches die ⟹ φ is UNSATISFIABLE. ► measured: exhaustive search examines all 2⁴ = 16 assignments. The backtracking search with unit propagation makes 1 branching decision, 4 unit propagations and 2 conflicts, over 3 calls. Without unit propagation the same search takes 5 calls, 5 decisions and 6 conflicts. ► the best any assignment can do is 5 of the 6 clauses — so this is also a MAX-SAT instance (§24) with optimum 5, and "unsatisfiable" and "5 of 6" are two true statements about the same formula.

That trace is the algorithm behind every SAT solver in production; §30 is the same procedure with twenty-five years of engineering bolted on. Two of its habits generalise immediately. First, propagate before you branch: any decision that is forced costs one step and never costs a factor of two. Second, branch where the constraint is tightest — in the shortest clause, on the most-constrained variable, at the vertex of highest degree — because that is where a failure verdict arrives soonest. The dual habit, branching where you are most confident of success, is the right one for a search that only needs one solution and knows a good heuristic, which is why solvers use both and switch.

Branch and bound: backtracking for optimisation

Backtracking's test is binary: a partial solution either can or cannot be completed. For an optimisation problem the corresponding question is quantitative — what is the cheapest completion? — and its exact answer is as hard as the original problem. So we replace it by a quantity that is cheap to compute and provably no larger: a lower bound on the cost of any completion. If cost(partial) + lowerbound(partial) ≥ the best complete solution already found, then no completion of this partial solution can beat the incumbent, and the whole subtree can go. Nothing else about the algorithm changes.

BRANCH-AND-BOUND, for a minimisation problemS ← {P₀}; best ← +∞; bestsol ← none while S ≠ ∅: choose P ∈ S and remove it expand P into subproblems P₁ … P_k for each Pᵢ: if Pᵢ is COMPLETE: if cost(Pᵢ) < best: best ← cost(Pᵢ); bestsol ← Pᵢ else if cost(Pᵢ) + lowerbound(Pᵢ) < best: add Pᵢ to S else: DISCARD ← the pruning return bestsol three knobs, and each one is worth more than the others in a different regime: the BOUND — tighter prunes more but costs more per node; the product is what matters the ORDER — depth-first keeps memory O(depth); best-first expands fewest nodes but stores them all the INCUMBENT — start from a good heuristic solution (§22's Christofides, §28's 2-opt) and the pruning bites from the first node instead of after the first leaf the CORRECTNESS condition — the only thing that must be proved lowerbound(P) ≤ cost of EVERY completion of P for every partial solution P That single inequality is what makes the discard safe. It is NOT a statement about the bound being good, or tight, or fast. A bound that violates it on even ONE partial solution can discard the branch containing the optimum — and the search then returns a suboptimal answer with no indication at all that anything went wrong. There is no run-time symptom. The output is simply wrong.

A lower bound for the travelling salesman, derived by counting edges

Fix the start city 0. A partial solution is a simple path 0 ⇝ cur that has visited a set of cities; write rem for the ones it has not. Its completion is a path cur → v₁ → … → v_k → 0 through exactly the cities of rem. The derivation is pure counting: that path uses exactly |rem| + 1 edges — one leaving cur, and one leaving each v ∈ rem (the last of them being the edge into 0). Bound each of those edges below by the cheapest edge that its tail vertex has available, and the sum is a lower bound.

the bound, and the edge count it rests onthe completion leaves cur exactly once, into rem: cost ≥ min { d(cur, u) : u ∈ rem } ≥ min { d(cur, u) : u ∈ rem ∪ {0} } the completion leaves each v ∈ rem exactly once, into another remaining city or into 0: cost ≥ min { d(v, u) : u ∈ (rem ∪ {0}) ∖ {v} } LB(cur, rem) = min_{u ∈ rem ∪ {0}} d(cur, u) + ∑_{v ∈ rem} min_{u ∈ (rem ∪ {0}) ∖ {v}} d(v, u) └────── 1 edge ──────┘ └──────────── |rem| edges ────────────┘ = |rem| + 1 ✓ each term is ≤ the edge the true completion actually uses at that vertex, and the terms are indexed by DISTINCT tail vertices, so no edge is double counted: LB ≤ cost(any completion). ∎ the first term takes its minimum over rem ∪ {0}, not rem, so that the same formula covers the last step (rem = ∅, where the only edge left is cur → 0); this is the bound the figure's code evaluates, and every count below comes from it. Dropping 0 from that minimum whenever rem ≠ ∅ gives a TIGHTER bound that is still valid (the edge out of cur really does go into rem); on this instance it enters 110 nodes, 41 of them unpruned, instead of 116 and 44, with the same optimum. cost: O(|rem|²) per node — cheap enough to pay at every node of the tree. the mistake — one extra term, and the bound stops being a bound"the completion must also come BACK to 0, so add the cheapest edge from rem into 0 as well": BAD(cur, rem) = min_{u ∈ rem} d(cur, u) + min_{v ∈ rem} d(v, 0) + ∑_{v ∈ rem} min_{u ∈ (rem ∪ {0}) ∖ {v}} d(v, u) └── 1 ──┘ └──── 1 ────┘ └──────── |rem| ────────┘ = |rem| + 2 the sentence is even TRUE — the completion does come back to 0 — but that edge is ALREADY one of the |rem| terms in the sum, because it leaves some v ∈ rem. Counting it twice makes BAD(cur, rem) exceed the true completion cost on most partial solutions, so the test "cost + BAD ≥ best" fires on branches that contain better tours, including the optimal one. Every individual term is a real edge cost, which is exactly why the error survives a reading; only the EDGE COUNT exposes it.

Measured, on the eight-city instance

All four searches below run on the same eight-city Euclidean instance, from the same start city, with children taken nearest-first. A node is one call on one partial path — pruned ones included; a node is unpruned when it survives the bound test, which makes unpruned = nodes − pruned: the internal nodes whose children are generated, plus the complete tours (leaves), which are never tested. The exhaustive optimum, over all 7!/2 = 2 520 distinct tours, is 32.1721.

four pruning rules, every count produced by running the search under a counter rule nodes unpruned pruned leaves answer correct? none — plain depth-first over 7! orders 13 700 13 700 0 5 040 32.1721 ✓ prune when cost alone ≥ incumbent 7 878 4 661 3 217 945 32.1721 ✓ prune on cost + LB (valid) 116 44 72 6 32.1721 ✓ prune on cost + BAD (invalid) 49 16 33 2 33.0944 ✗ WRONG ► the valid bound cuts 13 700 nodes to 116 — a factor of 118 — and still proves optimality. ► the invalid bound is TWICE AS FAST AGAIN (49 nodes) and returns a tour 2.87% worse than the optimum. It reports nothing. It has no failed assertion, no warning, no anomalous statistic.
