It just answers. ► why the invalid bound looks plausible in the run: it finds a complete tour, its incumbent decreases monotonically, its tree is small and tidy, and 33.0944 is a perfectly ordinary-looking number. ► the direct test, run over every partial path with ≥ 4 cities left (260 of them): the valid bound is ≤ the true cheapest completion on 260 of 260; the invalid bound is ≤ it on only 29 of 260 — it OVERSHOOTS on 231, by as much as 4.0036. That is the whole diagnosis, and it takes one loop.

The lesson is worth stating as a rule, because it is the one error on this page that a test suite does not catch: a branch-and-bound implementation should ship with a test that compares its bound against the true completion cost on small instances where the true completion can be computed exhaustively. Correctness of the search is not evidence; the search will happily return a wrong answer at speed. §33 lists this among the pitfalls, and it is the only one there whose symptom is better performance.

Branch and bound on eight cities: the tree shrinks with the bound — and the invalid bound shrinks it further and gets the wrong answer the search tree drawn to depth, one node per partial path, green where the incumbent improved and faded where the branch was pruned; every node count comes from running the real search under a counter, and the tour it returns is cross-checked against exhaustive search over all 2 520 tours — the "invalid bound" setting is expected to DISAGREE, and the bound-audit view shows exactly why

Dynamic programming over subsets: Held–Karp

Branch and bound attacks the constant in front of the exponential. Dynamic programming over subsets attacks the exponential itself, by noticing that a partial tour's future depends on the set of cities already visited and on the current endpoint, but not on the order in which the set was visited. That collapses (n−1)! partial paths into (n−1)·2ⁿ⁻¹ states, and is the oldest non-trivial exact exponential algorithm there is (Bellman 1962; Held and Karp 1962).

the recurrencefor S ⊆ {1 … n−1} and j ∈ S: g[S][j] = the cheapest path 0 ⇝ j that visits exactly S ∪ {0} and ends at j g[{j}][j] = d(0, j) g[S][j] = min { g[S ∖ {j}][i] + d(i, j) : i ∈ S ∖ {j} } for |S| ≥ 2 OPT = min { g[{1 … n−1}][j] + d(j, 0) : j ∈ 1 … n−1 } the table has (n − 1)·2ⁿ⁻¹ cells; the cells with j ∈ S — the only reachable ones — number (n − 1)·2ⁿ⁻² exactly, because for each of the n − 1 endpoints there are 2ⁿ⁻² subsets containing it. Each cell takes a minimum over < n predecessors. the cost — and two quantities that must not be put in the same column SPACE / STATES Θ(n·2ⁿ) cells a COUNT of table entries TIME Θ(n²·2ⁿ) a COUNT of elementary operations: Θ(n) work per cell these are DIFFERENT quantities and differ by a factor of n. At n = 8 the table has 896 cells and the time bound reads 8²·2⁸ = 16 384 operations. Listing "16 384" in a column headed "states" is the commonest slip in write-ups of this algorithm. ► measured at n = 8: 448 cells filled ( = (n−1)·2ⁿ⁻² = 7·64, exactly the reachable half of the (n−1)·2ⁿ⁻¹ = 896 the table allocates ) 1 344 transitions examined in the fill, plus 7 closing edges back to city 0 optimum 32.1721 on tour A-B-C-D-E-H-F-G — the same tour as the exhaustive search ► against the alternatives on the same instance: 2 520 tours enumerated exhaustively; 13 700 nodes for plain depth-first; 116 nodes for branch and bound with the valid bound. On EIGHT cities branch and bound wins. The point of Held–Karp is the asymptotics, not n = 8. what each growth rate can actually finish — arithmetic on a budget of 10⁹ operations per second for one hour, not a benchmark Θ(n!) permutations, no pruning n ≈ 15 Θ(n²·2ⁿ) Held–Karp n ≈ 31 ← but see the space column O(2ⁿ) subset enumeration n ≈ 41 O(1.3247ⁿ) a simple independent-set branching, n ≈ 102 analysed by counting vertices O(1.2210ⁿ) the SAME algorithm, measure and conquer n ≈ 144 O(2ᵏ) the §27 search tree for vertex cover k ≈ 41 O(1.3ᵏ) a refined vertex-cover branching k ≈ 110 ► Held–Karp's real ceiling is MEMORY, not time: at 8 bytes a cell, the (n−1)·2ⁿ⁻¹ table is ≈ 3.2 GB at n = 25 (≈ 1.6 GB if only the (n−1)·2ⁿ⁻² reachable cells are stored), and at n = 31 it is ≈ 258 GB (≈ 129 GB reachable) — large-server territory. On a 16 GB machine the allocated table stops fitting after n = 27 (≈ 14 GB), and the reachable-only layout after n = 28 (≈ 14.5 GB): a time bound alone would overstate the reach by three or four cities. ► the gap between bases is where all the engineering happens: 2ᵏ / 1.3ᵏ is 2.3 × 10⁹ at k = 50 and 5.1 × 10¹⁸ at k = 100. "Both exponential" is true and useless; the base is the whole subject.

Two more patterns, briefly, because they come up

  • Inclusion–exclusion. Counting Hamiltonian cycles, or deciding whether one exists, can be done in O*(2ⁿ) time and polynomial space. Fix a vertex v and let w(S) be the number of closed walks of length n from v to v that use only vertices outside S. Then ∑_{S ⊆ V} (−1)^|S| · w(S) counts exactly the closed walks that use every vertex — the Hamiltonian cycles, each counted a fixed number of times — because a walk missing a vertex u is counted once with each sign as S ranges over the subsets that do and do not contain u, and cancels. Each w(S) is an entry of an n-th matrix power, computable in polynomial time and polynomial space, so the algorithm stores nothing exponential — it only iterates over 2ⁿ subsets. That is the point of the technique: it trades Held–Karp's Θ(n·2ⁿ) memory for the same order of time, and memory is the binding constraint. The same idea counts proper colourings, set covers and perfect matchings.
  • Measure and conquer. Branching algorithms are analysed by a recurrence on a measure of instance size — and choosing a cleverer measure than "number of vertices" proves a better bound for the same algorithm. If a branching rule removes a vertex of degree d in one branch and d + 1 vertices in the other, the naive measure gives T(n) ≤ T(n−1) + T(n−d−1); weighting vertices by degree so that high-degree vertices count for more makes each branch reduce the measure by more, and the same code gets a smaller base. The technique's headline result is maximum independent set: a simple branching algorithm whose standard analysis — measure = number of vertices — gives O(1.3247ⁿ) was shown, by measure and conquer and without any change to the algorithm, to run in O(2^(0.288n)) ⊆ O(1.2210ⁿ) (for scale, 3^(n/3) ≈ 1.4423ⁿ is the number of maximal independent sets a graph can have, so it is the cost of enumerating them all); subsequent work combining better branching rules with the same style of analysis has brought the exponent below 1.2ⁿ. It is the clearest example in the field of a bound being a property of a proof rather than of a program — and the corresponding warning is that a published exponent tells you what someone has managed to prove, not what the code does.
  • Knapsack, with the fractional relaxation as the bound. Knapsack is a maximisation, so the roles flip and what the search needs at each node is an upper bound on the best completion; the natural one is the fractional knapsack's optimum on the remaining items, computed greedily by value density in O(n log n) once and O(n) per node (Greedy Algorithms). This is exactly the linear-programming relaxation of §30, and it is the reason knapsack instances with hundreds of thousands of items are solved exactly. Measured on the five-item instance of §25: the root fractional bound is 92.6667 against a true optimum of 85; the search visits 26 nodes and prunes 3 subtrees, against 2⁵ = 32 subsets for brute force — a modest saving at n = 5, and the same code at n = 60 is the difference between a second and a millennium.

Where this leaves you

  • Exact exponential search is the response of choice when n is genuinely small, when optimality is contractual, or when you need a certificate. It is the only one of §19's responses that produces a proof that the answer is optimal: the pruning argument, run to completion, certifies that no unexplored branch can do better. An approximation algorithm gives a ratio; a heuristic gives nothing; branch and bound gives the answer and the proof.
  • It composes with everything else on the page. Warm-start the incumbent from §22's Christofides tour or §28's 2-opt tour and the pruning bites immediately; use §27's kernelisation to shrink the instance before the search starts; use §30's LP relaxation as the bound. A production solver is all of these at once.
  • The bound is the algorithm. Everything else — the traversal order, the data structures, the incumbent — moves the running time by constant factors. The bound moves it by orders of magnitude, and it is also the only component that can make the answer wrong. Spend the effort there, and test it there.
  • Do not confuse "exponential" with "impossible". §19's framing is the right one: the hardness proof forbids a polynomial worst case, not a fast algorithm on your instances. §30's SAT and integer-programming solvers are exact exponential algorithms with enormous engineering inside the constant, and they solve instances that dwarf anything in this section.

27 Parameterised complexity: FPT, kernels, bounded search trees

Parameterised complexity refuses to measure an instance by a single number. It measures it by two — the size n and a parameter k that captures whatever is small in practice — and asks for a running time of the shape f(k)·poly(n), where f may be as monstrous as it likes but the dependence on n is polynomial with an exponent that does not involve k. A problem with such an algorithm is fixed-parameter tractable, FPT. The distinction this draws is not cosmetic: vertex cover is FPT with parameter "size of the cover", solvable in O(1.2738ᵏ + k·n) time, which is linear in n for every fixed k; clique with parameter "size of the clique" is not believed to be FPT — it is W[1]-hard, and the best known algorithms are n^O(k), where the parameter sits in the exponent of n. Both problems are NP-complete, both are mutually reducible in polynomial time (§11, §12), and they behave completely differently once a parameter is fixed. That is the whole subject: NP-completeness is a single equivalence class; parameterised complexity is a finer instrument applied inside it.

The two shapes, and the difference between them

FPT against XP — the placement of k decides everything FPT time f(k) · n^c c a CONSTANT independent of k e.g. 2ᵏ·n, 1.2738ᵏ + k·n, 2^(2^k)·n XP time n^f(k) the parameter is in the EXPONENT e.g. n^k, n^(k/2), n^(O(k)) at k = 10, n = 10⁶: 2ᵏ·n = 1.02 × 10⁹ finishes n^k = 10⁶⁰ does not, and never will at k = 30, n = 10⁶: 2ᵏ·n = 1.07 × 10¹⁵ a large cluster job n^k = 10¹⁸⁰ beyond the number of atoms in the universe FPT ⊆ XP, and the inclusion is PROVED STRICT — unlike almost every other separation on this page. So "is this problem FPT?" is a question with real content, and for some problems a real answer. the hardness theory, in one paragraph W[1]-hardness is to FPT what NP-hardness is to P: a problem is W[1]-hard if every problem in W[1] reduces to it by an FPT-REDUCTION — a map computable in f(k)·poly(n) time that sends parameter k to a parameter g(k) bounded by a function of k alone. CLIQUE, parameterised by the clique size k W[1]-COMPLETE INDEPENDENT SET, by the set size k W[1]-complete DOMINATING SET, by the set size k W[2]-complete (harder still) VERTEX COVER, by the cover size k FPT k-PATH (a simple path on k vertices) FPT, by colour coding, 2^O(k)·n SAT, by the number of variables n FPT trivially (2ⁿ·m) — a useless parameter FPT = W[1] would imply, among other things, an O(2^o(n)) algorithm for 3-SAT, contradicting the Exponential Time Hypothesis. So "W[1]-hard" means "no f(k)·poly(n) algorithm is known, and one would collapse a hierarchy that is widely believed not to collapse". Same epistemic status as §07's treatment of P vs NP, and the same discipline of phrasing applies.

Note where the reductions of §10–§15 fail here. The polynomial-time reduction from clique to vertex cover is a one-line complementation: a graph G has a clique of size k if and only if its complement has a vertex cover of size n − k (§12). That is a perfectly good polynomial-time reduction, and it is useless as an FPT reduction, because it sends the parameter k to n − k, which is not bounded by any function of k. This is exactly why parameterised complexity needs its own reductions and its own catalogue: the standard Karp reductions do not preserve parameters. A hardness proof that transfers in one theory need not transfer in the other, and §33 lists the temptation to reuse it as a pitfall.

The bounded search tree: vertex cover in O(2ᵏ·E)

The algorithm is three lines and rests on one observation. Take any edge (u, v) that the current partial cover does not cover. Every vertex cover must contain u or v — that is what "cover" means. So branch on those two possibilities, and each branch spends one unit of the budget k. The recursion depth is at most k, the branching factor is 2, and each node does O(E) work to find an uncovered edge.

VC-SEARCH(G, C, k) — does G have a vertex cover of size ≤ |C| + k containing C?1. if every edge of G is covered by C: return C SUCCESS 2. if k = 0: return FAIL budget exhausted 3. pick any uncovered edge (u, v) 4. return VC-SEARCH(G, C ∪ {u}, k − 1) or VC-SEARCH(G, C ∪ {v}, k − 1) CORRECTNESS. Step 3's edge must be covered, so u ∈ C* or v ∈ C* for every cover C* ⊇ C; the two branches are exhaustive. If a cover of size ≤ |C| + k exists, one branch keeps a cover of size ≤ |C| + k − 1 above the enlarged C, so induction on k carries it to a leaf. ∎ SIZE. A binary tree of depth ≤ k has ≤ 2^(k+1) − 1 nodes; each does O(E) work to find an uncovered edge. TIME O(2ᵏ · E) — FPT, since E ≤ n² and the exponent of n is 2, not k. SPACE O(k + n), the recursion stack and the partial cover: the tree is never materialised. measured on the seven-vertex instance of §12 and §21 G = a…g, 8 edges. Exhaustive search over all 2⁷ = 128 vertex subsets: the minimum cover has size 3 and is UNIQUE, {b, d, e}. The bounded search tree, branching on the first uncovered edge in the stored edge order: k = 0 FAIL 1 call ≤ 2¹ − 1 = 1 the empty cover leaves edge (a,b) uncovered k = 1 FAIL 3 calls ≤ 2² − 1 = 3 k = 2 FAIL 7 calls ≤ 2³ − 1 = 7 k = 3 FOUND 15 calls ≤ 2⁴ − 1 = 15 returns {b, d, e} — the unique optimum the bound is attained EXACTLY: the tree is FULL, because the successful leaf is the last one visited total over k = 0, 1, 2, 3: 26 calls, against 128 subsets for the brute force. ► reading the numbers honestly: 26 vs 128 is a factor of 4.9 on a seven-vertex graph, which is not an argument for anything. The argument is the SHAPE: 128 = 2ⁿ grows with the graph; 26 grows with k. At n = 10 000 with k = 20 the brute force is 2¹⁰⁰⁰⁰ and the search tree is about 2²¹ ≈ 2 × 10⁶ calls. ► the 2^(k+1) − 1 bound is on the SIZE OF THE TREE, not on the running time: the time is that many calls TIMES the O(E) spent in each. Two different quantities; keep them in separate columns.
Two axes, not one: the search tree grows with k while the brute force grows with n — and Buss's rule shrinks the instance before either starts left, the bounded search tree actually built at the chosen budget, each node labelled with the vertex it takes and the budget left, successful leaves green and budget-exhausted leaves faded; right, the kernel — the vertices Buss's rule forces into every cover and the edges that survive — with the measured call count against the 2^(k+1) − 1 bound, all cross-checked against exhaustive search over every vertex subset

Kernelisation: shrink the instance until only the hard core is left

The second FPT technique is more surprising than the first, and it is the one with practical consequences. A kernelisation is a polynomial-time preprocessing that maps an instance (I, k) to an equivalent instance (I′, k′) with k′ ≤ k and |I′| ≤ g(k) for some function g of the parameter alone. The size of the reduced instance does not depend on n at all. A ten-million-vertex graph with a cover of size 40 is reduced, in linear time, to a graph of at most a few thousand edges, and whatever exponential algorithm you like then runs on that. It is a theorem that a decidable problem is FPT if and only if it has a kernel, so kernelisation is not one technique among several — it is an equivalent characterisation.

BUSS's rule, with its proofCLAIM. If deg(v) > k then v belongs to EVERY vertex cover of size ≤ k. PROOF. Suppose C is a cover with |C| ≤ k and v ∉ C. Every one of v's deg(v) incident edges must be covered, and v is not covering any of them, so all deg(v) neighbours lie in C. Then |C| ≥ deg(v) > k, contradicting |C| ≤ k. ∎ RULE 1. While some vertex v has deg(v) > k: put v in the cover, delete v and its edges, set k ← k − 1. RULE 2. Delete isolated vertices (degree 0): they cover nothing and are in no minimal cover. RULE 3. If k < 0 at any point: answer NO. RULE 4. When Rules 1–3 no longer apply every remaining degree is ≤ k, so a cover of size ≤ k touches at most k·k edges. If MORE THAN k² edges remain: answer NO. Otherwise what remains is the KERNEL: ≤ k² edges and ≤ 2k² vertices, independent of n. TIME O(n + E) with degree counting. RESULT O(2ᵏ·k² + k·n) overall, by running the search tree of the previous subsection on the kernel rather than on G. The k·n term is the preprocessing; the exponential term no longer touches n at all. the rule applied to the seven-vertex instance at k = 3 — measured degrees: a 1, b 2, c 3, d 4, e 3, f 2, g 1 Rule 1: deg(d) = 4 > 3 ⟹ d is in EVERY cover of size ≤ 3. Take d; k ← 2; delete d's edges (c,d), (d,e), (d,f), (d,g). degrees now: a 1, b 2, c 2, e 2, f 1, g 0 — none exceeds the new budget 2, so Rule 1 stops. Rule 4: 4 edges remain — (a,b), (b,c), (c,e), (e,f) — and the limit is k² = 2² = 4. 4 ≤ 4, so the instance is NOT rejected; the kernel is those 4 edges on 5 vertices. the search tree then finds {b, e} on the kernel, and {d} ∪ {b, e} = {b, d, e} ✓ — the unique optimum found independently by enumerating all 128 subsets. ► note what Rule 4 does NOT say. "More than k² edges" ⟹ NO; "at most k² edges" ⟹ nothing at all, and the search still has to run. It is a rejection rule, not an acceptance rule. ► the sharper kernel: the Nemhauser–Trotter theorem gives a kernel with at most 2k VERTICES, via the half-integral LP relaxation of vertex cover, whose optimal basic solutions have every variable in {0, ½, 1}: the 1-variables go into the cover, the 0-variables go out, and only the ½-variables survive — at most 2k of them. That is the kernel real implementations use.

Choosing the parameter is the modelling step

The theory is only as useful as the parameter, and choosing it is where the judgement goes. A parameter is worth having when it is small on your instances and the problem is FPT with respect to it — two independent conditions, and either can fail.

parameterwhat it measuresproblems that become FPTwhen it is small in practicecaution
solution size khow big the answer is allowed to bevertex cover O(1.2738ᵏ + k·n); k-path 2^O(k)·n; feedback vertex set; d-hitting setconflict resolution, minimal repair sets, small seed setsfails for clique and independent set — W[1]-hard, so the same parameter is useless there
treewidthhow far the graph is from being a treealmost every NP-hard graph problem: independent set, colouring, Hamiltonicity, dominating set — all f(tw)·n by dynamic programming over a tree decompositionseries-parallel circuits, control-flow graphs, many road and utility networksf is 2^O(tw) for independent set, and — though the straightforward dynamic programme for Hamiltonicity, which tracks how partial paths pair up a bag's vertices, gives 2^O(tw log tw) — also 2^O(tw) for Hamiltonicity, by Cut & Count (randomised, 2011) and then deterministic rank-based methods; computing treewidth is itself NP-hard (though FPT)
number of distinct valueshow much of the input is actually variedscheduling with few distinct job lengths; ILP with few variables (Lenstra: f(d)·poly in fixed dimension d)configuration problems, bin packing with a small catalogue of item sizesthe f here is often doubly exponential, which is FPT and unusable
distance from trivialityhow many vertices/edges must be deleted to reach an easy classalmost-2-SAT, almost-bipartite, vertex cover above the LP bounddata that is nearly clean: a bipartite graph with a few odd edgesrequires knowing which easy class you are near, which is itself a modelling claim
the maximum degree Δlocal densitymany colouring and packing problems become polynomial or FPTphysical networks, molecular graphs, grid-like data3-colouring stays NP-complete on graphs of maximum degree 4 — a small Δ is not automatically a small parameter

Two failure modes deserve naming. The first is an FPT algorithm with an unusable f: a running time of 2^(2^(2^k))·n is FPT by the definition and useless at k = 4. The classification is qualitative; the constant still has to be checked. The second is a parameter that is not small: vertex cover is FPT in the cover size, and on a graph whose minimum cover is half the vertices that is worth precisely nothing. The question to ask of your data is never "is this problem FPT?" but "is there a number in my instances that stays below about 30 while n goes to millions?" — and if the answer is yes, the follow-up is whether that number is a parameter the literature has an algorithm for.

Where it sits among the other responses

  • Against approximation (§20–§25). FPT keeps exactness and pays in generality; approximation keeps generality and pays in exactness. They are not rivals, and they even combine: a parameterised approximation algorithm relaxes both, and for some problems that are both W[1]-hard and hard to approximate, relaxing both is the only thing that works.
  • Against exact exponential search (§26). The bounded search tree is a branch-and-bound search — its "bound" is the budget k — so the two sections describe one family of algorithms from two directions. The difference is what the exponent is measured against: §26 counts 2ⁿ in the input size, §27 counts 2ᵏ in the parameter, and kernelisation is the step that makes the second honest by removing n from the exponential term entirely.
  • Against special cases (§16). A structural restriction — planar, bipartite, interval, chordal — is the extreme case of a parameter: the parameter is zero. Bounded treewidth is the graded version, and the reason it is the single most productive parameter in the literature is that "small treewidth" holds approximately for a great many real graphs while "is a tree" holds for almost none.
  • Where it touches the rest of these notes. The tree-decomposition dynamic programme is the same table-filling discipline as Dynamic Programming's tree DP, with the bag of a decomposition node in place of a vertex's children; the k-path algorithm's colour coding is a randomised technique — like §24's coin flip — but it is derandomised differently: not by conditional expectations but by a k-perfect family of hash functions, an explicit, small set of colourings under at least one of which every k-subset of vertices gets k distinct colours; and the half-integrality that gives vertex cover its 2k kernel is the LP relaxation §30 uses as a branch-and-bound bound.

28 Local search, 2-opt and simulated annealing

Local search abandons the guarantee altogether: start from any solution, define a neighbourhood of small modifications, and keep moving to a neighbour that is better until none is. What it returns is a local optimum — a solution superior to everything within one move — and there is in general no theorem relating that to the global optimum at all. The design decision that matters is the neighbourhood, and it is a trade with two sides: a larger neighbourhood has fewer bad local optima and costs more to search, a smaller one is cheap per step and gets stuck more often. Local search has no place in a taxonomy of guarantees, and it is nevertheless the top performer on a broad range of optimisation problems, routinely landing within one or two per cent of optimal on travelling-salesman instances with millions of cities. This section defines the mechanism precisely, measures it, and is careful to distinguish what was observed on one instance from what is proved — because for local search those two sets barely overlap.

Neighbourhoods, local optima, and the two things that go wrong

the procedure, and the definitions it needsa NEIGHBOURHOOD is a map N : solutions → sets of solutions. It is IMPOSED by the designer; nothing in the problem statement supplies it. LOCAL-SEARCH(s₀): s ← s₀ while ∃ s′ ∈ N(s) with cost(s′) < cost(s): s ← s′ ← WHICH s′: a policy (below) return s s is a LOCAL OPTIMUM ⟺ cost(s) ≤ cost(s′) for every s′ ∈ N(s) s is a GLOBAL optimum ⟺ cost(s) ≤ cost(s′) for every feasible s′ a PLATEAU is a connected set of neighbours of EQUAL cost — no strict improvement exists, and the search halts although it may be adjacent to an improving move two steps away the two failures, and they are different: (a) the search stops at a local optimum that is much worse than the global one (b) the search does not stop soon enough — the number of improving steps can be exponential the trade the neighbourhood makes |N| large → fewer local optima (every local optimum must beat MORE competitors) each step costs more to search in the limit N = everything: one step, and it is the original NP-hard problem |N| small → each step is cheap more local optima, and typically worse ones the usable middle: |N| polynomial, searchable in O(|N|) or better with incremental evaluation. For TSP: 2-change |N| = Θ(n²), 3-change |N| = Θ(n³), Lin–Kernighan a variable-depth compromise.

An aside that matters for reading the literature: finding a local optimum is itself a computational problem, and for many neighbourhoods it is hard in a precise sense — the class PLS ("polynomial local search") collects the problems where a solution's neighbourhood can be searched in polynomial time, and TSP with the k-change neighbourhood is PLS-complete. A PLS-complete problem has instances and starting points from which every improving path to a local optimum is exponentially long. So "run local search to convergence" is not a polynomial-time algorithm in the worst case, even though it is one in every run anyone has ever performed. Smoothed analysis explains the gap: under tiny random perturbations of the input, the expected number of 2-opt improving steps is polynomial.

2-opt for the travelling salesman, defined exactly

Two tours cannot differ in exactly one edge — removing one edge from a cycle leaves a Hamiltonian path whose only closure is the edge just removed — so the smallest interesting difference is two edges. The 2-change neighbourhood of a tour is the set of tours obtained by removing two edges and adding two others. For a tour written as a sequence, that operation has a clean form: reverse a contiguous segment.

the 2-opt move, and why its cost change is a four-term expressiontour t = ( t₀, t₁, …, t_{n−1} ), read cyclically. A move is a pair (i, k) with 1 ≤ i < k ≤ n−1. before: … t_{i−1} → t_i → t_{i+1} → … → t_k → t_{k+1} … after: … t_{i−1} → t_k → t_{k−1} → … → t_i → t_{k+1} … the segment t_i … t_k REVERSED removed edges: ( t_{i−1}, t_i ) and ( t_k, t_{k+1} ) added edges: ( t_{i−1}, t_k ) and ( t_i, t_{k+1} ) every other edge of the tour is unchanged — those inside the reversed segment are traversed backwards, and the instance is SYMMETRIC, so they cost the same. Δ(i, k) = d(t_{i−1}, t_k) + d(t_i, t_{k+1}) − d(t_{i−1}, t_i) − d(t_k, t_{k+1}) evaluating a move is O(1); APPLYING it is O(k − i) for the reversal. |N| = Θ(n²) pairs. ► on a DIRECTED (asymmetric) instance this is false: the reversed segment's edges change cost, and the move is no longer O(1) to evaluate. Asymmetric TSP needs a different neighbourhood (Or-opt, 3-opt variants that preserve orientation). Quoting the symmetric analysis for an asymmetric instance is a real and common mistake. two policies for choosing among improving moves FIRST improvement — scan (i, k) in some order and take the first move with Δ < 0. cheap per step; the scan order is part of the algorithm, not an implementation detail BEST improvement — evaluate all Θ(n²) pairs and take the most negative Δ. "steepest descent"; more work per step, FEWER steps neither dominates the other. Best improvement is not the better policy; it is the greedier one, and greed over a descent trajectory is not an optimisation criterion. The measurement below shows it losing on this instance, and that is not a fluke worth generalising either — it is a single instance.

Measured, on the eight-city instance — including the part that is luck

2-opt from the nearest-neighbour tour, every count from the real routine under a counter start: nearest neighbour from city A → tour A-B-H-F-G-E-D-C, cost 38.8502 (ratio 1.2076) exhaustive optimum over all 7!/2 = 2 520 tours: 32.1721 on A-B-C-D-E-H-F-G policy moves (i, k) pairs evaluated final tour cost ratio first improvement 2 40 A-B-C-D-E-H-F-G 32.1721 1.0000 ← the optimum best improvement 2 63 A-B-H-C-D-E-F-G 33.0944 1.0287 ► first improvement reached the GLOBAL optimum here. That is a fact about this instance and this starting tour. It is NOT a bound, it is not typical, and 2-opt has no approximation guarantee of any kind — see the theory box below. Reporting "2-opt found the optimum" as evidence for 2-opt is the benchmark-on-a-favourable-instance error of §33. ► best improvement did MORE work (63 pairs against 40) and finished WORSE (33.0944 against 32.1721). Steeper descent, worse basin. On another instance the comparison reverses. ► 200 random restarts, each a uniformly random starting tour followed by first-improvement 2-opt: the run reaches exactly TWO distinct local optima on this instance — 32.1721 and 33.0944 — and lands on the optimum 117 times out of 200. So a single 2-opt run has roughly a 59% chance here, and three independent restarts take the failure probability to about 7%. That is the whole theory of restarts. ► simulated annealing on the same neighbourhood from the same start (T₀ = 3, T_end = 0.01, 3 000 proposals, geometric cooling): reaches 32.1721, having ACCEPTED 185 worsening moves and 110 improving ones and rejected 2 705. The 185 are the mechanism; a descent that accepts none of them is exactly the first-improvement run above.
What is actually proved about 2-opt. (i) For general TSP it has no constant-factor guarantee, and neither does anything else: §22's gap reduction shows a constant-factor approximation for general TSP implies P = NP. (ii) For metric TSP the worst-case ratio of a 2-opt local optimum is exactly √(n/2), a tight bound — proved, not conjectured, and growing with n (the earlier Ω(log n / log log n) lower bound was the first proof that it is not constant) — and for Euclidean instances it is Θ(log n / log log n), sharpening an older O(log n) upper bound. None of these is a constant. (iii) The number of improving steps is exponential in the worst case, and polynomial in expectation under smoothed analysis. So the honest summary is: 2-opt has no approximation guarantee, has a proved super-constant lower bound on metric instances, and is excellent in practice — three statements that are all true simultaneously, and the third is not evidence for weakening the first two.
Step the 2-opt descent, or turn the temperature up: the improving move, the cost, and the gap to the exhaustive optimum in descent mode, each step highlights the two edges leaving the tour in red and the two entering in green and counts the (i, k) pairs evaluated; in annealing mode the temperature is yours and the accepted worsening moves are counted separately — the mechanism that distinguishes annealing from descent; the exhaustive optimum over all 2 520 tours is drawn as a line in the cost panel and every cost shown is recomputed from the tour it is drawn on

Escaping local optima: randomisation, restarts, annealing

Three devices, in increasing order of sophistication and of the effort needed to tune them.

  • Randomise the start, and restart. If a single run reaches a good local optimum with probability p, then O(1/p) independent runs find one with high probability; with r restarts the failure probability is (1 − p)^r. Measured above: p ≈ 0.585 on this instance, so three restarts fail with probability about 0.07 and five with about 0.012. Restarts are embarrassingly parallel, need no tuning, and are the first thing to try. They stop working when p itself shrinks exponentially with instance size, which is the usual situation on large instances.
  • Randomise the move. When several improving moves are available, pick among them at random rather than by a fixed rule. This alone changes which basin the trajectory falls into and turns a deterministic algorithm with one outcome into a distribution over outcomes, which restarts can then exploit.
  • Accept worsening moves: simulated annealing. Instead of only descending, propose a random neighbour and accept it even if it is worse, with a probability that falls as the damage rises and as the search proceeds.
SIMULATED ANNEALING, with the Metropolis rule written outs ← any starting solution; T ← T₀ repeat: choose s′ ∈ N(s) uniformly at random Δ ← cost(s′) − cost(s) if Δ < 0: s ← s′ always take an improvement else: s ← s′ with probability e^(−Δ/T) and sometimes take a worsening one T ← cool(T) until T is small or the budget is spent; return the BEST solution ever seen (not the last one) the acceptance probability, read carefully: T → 0 e^(−Δ/T) → 0 the procedure degenerates to plain descent T → ∞ e^(−Δ/T) → 1 the procedure degenerates to a random walk, ignoring cost Δ = 0 e^0 = 1 a plateau move is ALWAYS accepted — which is how annealing crosses plateaux big Δ exponentially unlikely — the rule is forgiving of small damage, not of large COOLING SCHEDULES: geometric T_{i+1} = α·T_i with α ≈ 0.95 … 0.999 (what everyone uses) logarithmic T_i = c / log(i + 2) (what the theorem needs) ► THE THEOREM, and its uselessness: with a logarithmic schedule and enough time, annealing converges in probability to a global optimum. The time it needs to do so is typically WORSE than enumerating the whole search space. The theorem certifies that the method is not fundamentally broken; it justifies no run you will ever perform. Every practical schedule is geometric and has NO guarantee. ► the price: many more moves than plain descent — 3 000 proposals against 40 pair evaluations on the eight-city instance — and a schedule to tune, which is genuinely an art.

Local search for satisfiability: GSAT and WalkSAT

The same machinery applies verbatim to SAT, with a different neighbourhood. A solution is a complete truth assignment; the neighbourhood is "flip one variable" (so |N| = n); the objective is the number of unsatisfied clauses, to be driven to zero. GSAT flips the variable that reduces the unsatisfied count the most. WalkSAT improves on it with a targeted random walk: pick a currently violated clause, and flip one of its three variables — with probability p a random one, otherwise the one that breaks fewest other clauses. Restricting attention to a violated clause is the key idea: flipping a variable outside every violated clause cannot help, so the neighbourhood is pruned to the moves that could possibly matter.

  • These solvers are incomplete. They find satisfying assignments; they never prove unsatisfiability. Running WalkSAT for a million flips and failing tells you nothing — the formula may be satisfiable and unlucky. A complete solver (§30's DPLL/CDCL) can answer both ways. This is the sharpest instance on the page of "what guarantee does this algorithm carry?", and the answer for a local-search SAT solver is: a yes-answer is certified by the assignment it returns; a no-answer is not an answer at all.
  • They win on the instances CDCL loses. Randomly generated satisfiable instances near the §30 threshold, and large structured instances with many solutions, often fall to WalkSAT in seconds where a systematic search flounders; industrial instances with deep implication structure go the other way. Portfolio solvers run both.
  • The connection to §24. A uniformly random assignment already satisfies 7m/8 clauses of a 3-CNF in expectation. WalkSAT starts from exactly that and walks. So the local search begins with a proved 7/8-approximation for MAX-3-SAT and discards the guarantee the moment it takes its first flip.

What carries over, and to where

  • The continuous cousin. Gradient descent is local search with an infinitesimal neighbourhood and a derivative to pick the direction; local minima, plateaux (saddle points and flat regions), restarts (random initialisation), and annealing (noise injection, learning-rate schedules) all have exact counterparts. Optimization develops the continuous theory, including the convexity condition under which every local minimum is global — which is precisely the condition that makes local search exact and which combinatorial problems almost never satisfy.
  • When local search is exact. It sometimes is, and the cases are worth knowing because they mark the boundary. The simplex method is local search over the vertices of a polytope with the edge neighbourhood, and it is exact because the feasible region is convex. Local search on spanning trees with the single-edge-swap neighbourhood is exact, and the reason is the matroid exchange property that Greedy Algorithms proves. Where an exchange argument exists, local search is an algorithm with a theorem; where it does not, local search is a heuristic. §22's discussion of greedy rules that look like approximation algorithms is the same distinction.
  • Graph partitioning, the other standard example. Split V into two halves minimising the weight crossing the cut; the neighbourhood is "swap a pair, one from each side", of size Θ(n²), which keeps the halves balanced. This is minimum bisection, which is NP-hard — although minimum cut without the balance constraint is in P by max-flow (Shortest Paths, MST & Flow), so the balance requirement is exactly the kind of one-constraint change that crosses §16's boundary — and the Kernighan–Lin heuristic — a variable-depth local search over exactly this neighbourhood — is still the workhorse in circuit layout and sparse-matrix ordering.
  • Measure, do not assume. The single most useful property of local search is that its quality is measurable on your own instances: run it against an exact solver on small cases, against a bound (the MST bound for TSP, the LP relaxation for an ILP) on large ones, and you obtain an empirical gap that is worth more than a worst-case ratio you will never meet. What you must not do is publish that gap as a guarantee. §33.

29 Population heuristics: genetic algorithms

A genetic algorithm keeps a population of candidate solutions rather than one, scores each by a fitness function, and produces the next population by selecting fitter candidates, recombining pairs of them, and perturbing the results at random. It carries no guarantee of any kind — not optimality, not an approximation ratio, not a bound on running time, not even a proof that it converges — and it is the last thing on this page one should reach for. It earns a section because it earns its keep in one specific situation, which is common enough to matter: when the objective can be evaluated but not analysed — a simulation, a physical measurement, a trained model's score — so that no gradient, no relaxation, no lower bound and no exchange argument is available, and the only thing the algorithm is allowed to do is ask "how good is this one?". In that setting the competition is not Christofides or branch and bound; it is random search, and a population method beats random search. Everywhere else, the sections above are better, and §32 routes accordingly.

The pieces, and the one that is actually hard to get right

the generational loop1. ENCODE choose a chromosome: a data structure encoding one candidate solution 2. INITIALISE generate a population of N chromosomes, usually at random 3. EVALUATE compute the fitness of each ← the ONLY contact with the problem 4. repeat until a stopping rule fires: a. SELECT choose parents, biased towards higher fitness b. CROSSOVER with probability p_c, recombine two parents into a child; else copy a parent c. MUTATE with probability p_m, perturb the child d. EVALUATE the child's fitness e. REPLACE form the next population from children (and, with ELITISM, the best few parents) 5. RETURN the best chromosome ever seen — NOT the best of the final population, which may be worse the stopping rule is itself a choice with no theory behind it: a generation budget, an evaluation budget, no improvement in the best for g generations, or the population's fitness variance collapsing. the parameters, and what each one trades N population size diversity ↔ evaluations per generation. Too small: premature convergence. p_c crossover probability 0.6 … 0.95 typical. p_c = 0 makes it parallel mutation hill-climbing. p_m mutation probability often 1/L per gene, L the chromosome length. Too high: a random walk. tournament size / selection pressure high: fast convergence to a possibly bad optimum; low: slow progress, but the population keeps its variety elitism how many of the best survive untouched. Without it the best solution can be LOST between generations — which is why step 5 says "ever seen". ► there is no theory that sets any of these. They are tuned by experiment on instances like yours, and the tuning does not transfer between problems. That tuning cost is the real price of the method and is almost never counted when a GA is compared against an algorithm that has no parameters.

The component that most often decides whether the method works at all is the representation — the choice of chromosome — because crossover and mutation are defined on it, not on the problem. A representation is good when small changes to the chromosome correspond to small changes in the solution, and when the pieces that crossover exchanges are meaningful pieces of a solution. A representation is bad when a single mutation produces an infeasible object, or when the two halves that crossover glues together have no relationship to each other. The travelling salesman is the standard cautionary example.

Selection: four rules, and what each one is for

  • Roulette-wheel (fitness-proportional) selection. Give each chromosome a slice of a wheel proportional to its fitness and spin once per parent: P(i) = fᵢ / ∑ⱼ fⱼ. Simple, and awkward in three ways. It needs non-negative fitness, so a minimisation objective such as tour length has to be transformed — f = C − cost or f = 1/cost — and the choice of transformation silently changes the selection pressure. It is dominated by outliers early on, when one lucky chromosome can take a large share of the wheel and flood the population. And it stops discriminating late on, when all fitnesses are nearly equal and the wheel is nearly uniform. Fitness scaling patches these, and adds parameters.
  • Rank selection. Sort the population and assign selection probabilities by rank rather than by raw fitness. Immune to the scale of the objective and to outliers, and it keeps a usable pressure when the fitnesses have converged. The cost is a sort per generation and the loss of the information in the fitness gaps.
  • Tournament selection. Draw t chromosomes uniformly at random and take the best. The selection pressure is set by t alone — t = 1 is no pressure at all, t = 2 is mild, t = 5 is strong — it needs no sorting, no scaling and no non-negativity, and it works unchanged on a minimisation objective. This is the practical default and the one the figure uses.
  • Elitism. Copy the best e chromosomes into the next generation unchanged. Not a selection rule but a replacement rule, and it removes a real failure mode: without it, the best solution found so far can be destroyed by crossover or mutation and never recovered. With it, the best fitness is monotone non-increasing across generations — the only monotonicity a GA has, and it is there by construction rather than by any property of the search.
  • Stochastic universal sampling. Roulette with N equally spaced pointers on one spin instead of N independent spins: the same expected counts with far less variance, so a chromosome with expected share 2.4 is selected 2 or 3 times rather than possibly 0 or 6 times. A cheap variance reduction on top of proportional selection.

Crossover, and why the ordinary one destroys a tour

the bit-string operators, on chromosomes of length L ONE-POINT pick a cut c ∈ 1 … L−1; child = parent₁[1…c] ++ parent₂[c+1…L] preserves contiguous runs; can never separate two adjacent genes; strongly positional TWO-POINT pick c₁ < c₂; the child takes the middle from one parent and the ends from the other treats the chromosome as a RING, so the "ends" are one segment; less positional bias UNIFORM each gene independently from either parent with probability ½ maximal mixing, no positional bias at all, and maximally destructive of long patterns ► the choice is a claim about where the useful structure lies. One-point assumes it is contiguous; uniform assumes it is scattered. Neither assumption is checkable in advance. what one-point crossover does to two TOURS — measured parent₁ = A-B-C-D-E-F-G-H parent₂ = A-D-B-H-F-C-G-E cut after position 4 child = A-B-C-D ++ F-C-G-E = A-B-C-D-F-C-G-E city C appears TWICE; city H appears NOT AT ALL. The child is not a tour, not a permutation, and not a point of the search space. The operator did not produce a bad solution — it produced a non-solution. Repairing it (delete duplicates, append the missing cities) is possible and destroys exactly the structure crossover was supposed to transmit. ► the general rule: when the chromosome carries a CONSTRAINT — a permutation, a partition, a tree, a schedule with precedences — the textbook operators violate it, and the operator must be redesigned around the constraint. This is the representation problem, and it is where the work is. ORDER CROSSOVER (OX) — a crossover that respects a permutation1. choose two cut points i ≤ j 2. copy parent₁[i … j] into the child at the SAME positions 3. walk parent₂ from position j+1 CYCLICALLY; skip any city already in the child; place the rest into the child's empty slots, also from position j+1 cyclically parent₁ = A-B-C-D-E-F-G-H, parent₂ = A-D-B-H-F-C-G-E, i = 2, j = 4 (positions counted from 0) step 2: child = _ _ C D E _ _ _ the run C-D-E of parent₁, copied in place step 3: parent₂ from position 5 cyclically: C, G, E, A, D, B, H, F → drop C, D, E → G, A, B, H, F fill from position 5 cyclically: child = H-F-C-D-E-G-A-B VERIFIED a permutation what it transmits: a contiguous RUN of visits from one parent, and the RELATIVE ORDER of the rest from the other — which are the two things a tour is actually made of. A tour's meaning is in the adjacencies, not in the absolute positions, and OX is built around that. ► PMX (partially matched crossover) is the other standard permutation operator: it takes a matching section and repairs the collisions by following the exchange chain it induces. Both are valid; which is better on a given problem is an empirical question with no general answer.

Mutation is easier. On bit strings it is a flip; on permutations the standard operators are swap (exchange two positions), insertion (move one city elsewhere) and inversion (reverse a segment) — and inversion is exactly the 2-opt move of §28, which is the clearest possible statement of what a GA on TSP is doing: it is running a population of local searches and occasionally splicing them. Mutation's role is not to improve; it is to reintroduce alleles the population has lost, so that a converged population does not become a fixed point. A GA with p_m = 0 converges to a population of clones and then stops moving entirely, whatever its crossover rate.

Generational or steady-state, and the schema story

Generational replacement builds an entire new population and discards the old one (with elitism as the escape hatch). Steady-state replacement produces one or two children per step and inserts them by displacing the worst members, so the population changes continuously; it converges faster and loses diversity faster. The choice is another tuning parameter with no theory.

The traditional explanation of why any of this works is the schema theorem and its corollary, the building-block hypothesis. A schema is a pattern over the alphabet extended with a wildcard — 1**0* matches every chromosome with a 1 first and a 0 fourth — and the theorem gives a lower bound on the expected number of copies of a schema in the next generation: short, low-order schemata whose average fitness exceeds the population average receive exponentially increasing trials, provided crossover and mutation do not disrupt them too often. The building-block hypothesis then asserts that a GA works by assembling such short, low-order, above-average "building blocks" into better and better solutions.

Read that as intuition, not as a theorem about performance. The schema theorem is a correct statement about expected counts over one generation of a specific selection scheme; it does not say that the GA finds good solutions, and it cannot, because it says nothing about the generations after the first. The building-block hypothesis is a hypothesis, and the literature contains explicit counterexamples: schemata whose average fitness is high because of a few outstanding members that the GA will never see, and schemata whose average is dragged below the population average by a few poor members although they contain the optimum. Competing explanations of the same empirical successes exist and fit the evidence about as well — that crossover acts as a macromutation operator (replace a contiguous block by a random one), under which a population is not needed at all and a "macromutational hill-climber" matches the GA on problems without clean building blocks; and that crossover is an adaptive mutation whose step size shrinks automatically as the population converges. The honest position is that genetic algorithms are an empirical technique with a suggestive biological metaphor and no performance theory, and this page will not pretend otherwise.

Head to head, on the same eight-city instance

everything measured by running the real routine; optimum 32.1721 from exhaustive search over 2 520 tours method work measured result guarantee exhaustive search 2 520 tours enumerated 32.1721 OPTIMAL, proved branch and bound, valid lower bound (§26) 116 search-tree nodes 32.1721 OPTIMAL, proved Christofides (§22) one MST + one matching 32.2175 ≤ 3/2 · OPT, proved nearest neighbour (§22) n² distance scans 38.8502 none 2-opt, first improvement, from NN (§28) 40 O(1) move evaluations 32.1721 none 2-opt, best improvement, from NN (§28) 63 O(1) move evaluations 33.0944 none simulated annealing, from NN (§28) 3 000 proposals, 185 worse accepted 32.1721 none genetic algorithm, 40 × 60, seed 3 2 320 FULL tour evaluations 32.1721 none genetic algorithm, 40 × 60, seed 5 2 320 FULL tour evaluations 33.0944 none — it STALLED ► the units differ and the difference is the point. A 2-opt move evaluation is four distance lookups (§28's four-term Δ); a GA fitness evaluation is a full tour, eight lookups. In lookups: 2-opt spent about 160 to reach the optimum and the GA about 18 560 — a factor of roughly 116 for the same answer. ► over 50 seeds the GA reaches the optimum on 43 (86%), mean final tour 32.3012; with the population cut from 40 to 12 it reaches it on 28 of 50 (56%) — diversity is what the population is buying. ► 2-opt from 200 random starting tours reaches the optimum on 117 of them (58.5%), for 15 940 move evaluations in total — and the BEST of the 200 is the optimum. Restarted local search is the population method's most honest competitor, and it has no parameters to tune. ► on a scrambled eight-city instance none of this decides anything. The comparison is offered to make the UNITS and the STALL visible, not to rank the methods; a ranking needs a benchmark family, many instances and error bars, and even then transfers only to instances like those.
A real GA run: best and mean fitness per generation, against the exhaustive optimum — watch it converge, and watch it stall order crossover, tournament selection and elitism on the eight-city instance; the curves are the best and the mean tour length in each generation, every value produced by evaluating real tours under a counter, and the flat line is the optimum computed by exhaustive search over all 2 520 tours; change the seed until you find one on which the population converges to something worse and never leaves — a GA has no mechanism that forbids this and no way to detect it

When to use one, and when not

  • Use one when the objective is a black box. If fitness comes from a simulation, a physical experiment, a rendering, or the validation score of a trained model, then there is no gradient, no relaxation to bound with, no exchange argument and no structure to exploit — the only permitted operation is evaluation. Everything else on this page needs structure; a population method needs only a fitness function and an encoding. This is also the honest description of most reported GA successes.
  • Use one when the search space is a strange object. Mixed discrete/continuous parameters, variable-length structures, programs, network topologies, designs. Writing a neighbourhood for local search is often as much work as writing crossover and mutation, and if the space is that irregular, neither has a theory, so take the one that is easier to implement correctly.
  • Do not use one when a guarantee is available. If the problem is vertex cover, take the 2-approximation (§21) — it is three lines and it is a theorem. If it is metric TSP, take Christofides (§22). If it is knapsack with a stated accuracy, take the FPTAS (§25). If n is small or a parameter is small, take §26 or §27 and get the exact answer. A GA that lands near a solution you could have proved near is strictly worse than the proof.
  • Do not use one as a first resort on a problem with an obvious local move. Restarted local search (§28) is simpler, has fewer parameters, is easier to instrument, parallelises trivially, and on combinatorial problems with a natural neighbourhood it is usually at least as good. The measurement above is one data point in that direction and the literature has many more. If a GA is chosen over local search, the comparison should have been run.
  • Beware the comparison that was never made. A GA is almost always reported against random search or against no baseline at all. The baselines that matter are: a good constructive heuristic, local search from that heuristic, local search with restarts, and — if the instance is small enough — an exact solver, so the gap is a measured gap rather than a relative one. §33.
  • Where this touches the rest of these notes. Evolutionary and population search appears in machine learning as one of the black-box strategies for hyperparameter tuning, alongside random search and Bayesian optimisation, and the case for it there is exactly the case made above: the validation score is a black box. Its continuous relatives — evolution strategies, CMA-ES, and the finite-difference gradient estimators — are derivative-free optimisation, next to the gradient methods of Optimization; the trade is the same one, an exact gradient where one exists against a sampled estimate where none does.

30 SAT solvers, ILP, and the phase transition

NP-complete problems are solved to optimality, routinely, at industrial scale — satisfiability instances with millions of variables, integer programmes with hundreds of thousands of binary variables — and nothing in §07 is violated, because NP-completeness is a statement about the worst case over all instances and the instances people actually have are not the worst case. Two technologies do most of this work: conflict-driven clause-learning SAT solvers, which are §26's backtracking search with four decades of engineering around the conflict; and integer-programming solvers, which are §26's branch and bound with the linear-programming relaxation as the bound and cutting planes to tighten it. The third thing this section owes the reader is an explanation of why random instances are usually easy: the satisfiable fraction of random 3-CNF formulas falls sharply as the clause/variable ratio crosses a threshold, and instances are hard only in a narrow band around the crossing. The figure measures that curve by running a real solver on freshly generated formulas — and the caption is careful, because the number the curve shows at a small number of variables is not the asymptotic threshold.

From DPLL to CDCL: what each mechanism buys

The search of §26 — assign a variable, simplify, recurse, backtrack on an empty clause — is the Davis–Putnam–Logemann–Loveland procedure, DPLL, and on its own it is a competent exponential search. A modern solver is DPLL plus five mechanisms, each of which addresses a specific waste.

  • Unit propagation (Boolean constraint propagation). When simplification leaves a clause with a single unassigned literal, that literal is forced: assign it and simplify again, to a fixed point. This costs one step and saves a branching factor of two per forced variable, and in practice 90% or more of all assignments in a solver run are propagated rather than decided. The implementation trick that makes it fast is two watched literals per clause: a clause can only become unit when one of its two watched literals is falsified, so an assignment only has to visit the clauses watching it, and — crucially — nothing has to be undone on backtrack.
  • Conflict analysis and clause learning. When a clause becomes empty, plain DPLL flips the last decision and forgets what happened. A CDCL solver instead walks the implication graph back from the conflict to a cut (the first unique implication point) and derives a new clause that is logically entailed by the formula and that excludes the assignment pattern that caused this conflict. That clause is added to the formula permanently. The effect is that the same conflict cannot recur anywhere else in the tree: the solver converts one dead end into a constraint that prunes the whole remaining search. This is the single mechanism that separates modern solvers from 1990s ones.
  • Non-chronological backtracking (backjumping). The learned clause says which decision levels were actually responsible for the conflict. If the culprits are levels 3 and 17, the solver jumps straight back to level 3 rather than undoing level 40, level 39, and so on. Whole regions of the tree are skipped without ever being visited — which is why a CDCL solver's trace is not a depth-first traversal of anything.
  • Activity-based branching (VSIDS). Every variable carries an activity score; variables appearing in recently learned clauses get bumped, all scores decay geometrically, and the solver branches on the highest score. The effect is that branching follows the part of the formula where conflicts are currently happening, so the search concentrates on the region that is actually constrained. It is a cheap, self-tuning heuristic with no theory behind it and an enormous measured effect.
  • Restarts. Periodically abandon the current assignment trail and start again from the top — keeping every learned clause. Search-cost distributions for these solvers are heavy-tailed: a run has a substantial chance of dropping into a region that costs orders of magnitude more than typical, and restarting cuts that tail off while the learned clauses ensure that nothing is really lost. Modern schedules (Luby, or glue-based) restart very frequently.
  • Preprocessing and inprocessing. Before and during the search: eliminate variables by resolution, subsume clauses that imply others, strengthen clauses by self-subsuming resolution, detect equivalent literals. These are §27's kernelisation applied to formulas, and they routinely cut industrial instances by large factors before any search happens.
DPLL measured — the four-variable unsatisfiable formula of §26φ = (w ∨ x ∨ y ∨ z) ∧ (w ∨ ¬x) ∧ (x ∨ ¬y) ∧ (y ∨ ¬z) ∧ (z ∨ ¬w) ∧ (¬w ∨ ¬z) UNSATISFIABLE exhaustive search over all 2⁴ = 16 assignments 16 assignments, 6 clauses each DPLL WITH unit propagation 3 calls, 1 decision, 4 propagations, 2 conflicts, depth 1 DPLL WITHOUT it 5 calls, 5 decisions, 0 propagations, 6 conflicts, depth 3 ► one decision instead of five. On four variables that is a curiosity; the same ratio at n = 10⁶ is the difference between a solver and a rumour. ► and on §09's satisfiable φ the solver makes 2 decisions and returns 110 (x₁ = x₂ = TRUE, x₃ = FALSE), one of the 5 satisfying assignments that exhaustive search over all 8 finds — checked in the figure.

Integer programming: branch and bound over a linear relaxation

An integer linear programme asks to minimise cᵀx subject to Ax ≤ b and x integral; with x ∈ {0,1}ⁿ it is a 0-1 ILP, and it is NP-hard — indeed §14's subset sum, §12's vertex cover, §23's set cover and §13's TSP all have two-line ILP formulations, which is exactly why the technology is worth building. Dropping the integrality requirement leaves a linear programme, which is in P, and that is the whole lever.

the relaxation, and why it is a bound ILP: min cᵀx s.t. Ax ≤ b, x ∈ ℤⁿ LP: min cᵀx s.t. Ax ≤ b, x ∈ ℝⁿ every feasible point of the ILP is feasible for the LP, so the LP minimises over a LARGER set: LP-OPT ≤ ILP-OPT always, for a minimisation that inequality is the branch-and-bound lower bound of §26, computed by an algorithm that is polynomial in theory (ellipsoid, interior point) and fast in practice (simplex). If the LP optimum happens to be INTEGRAL it is the ILP optimum and the node is done; otherwise pick a variable xⱼ with fractional value v and BRANCH: xⱼ ≤ ⌊v⌋ and xⱼ ≥ ⌈v⌉ both children exclude the fractional point, neither excludes any integer point. Recurse. the INTEGRALITY GAP = ILP-OPT / LP-OPT measures how much the relaxation throws away. It approaches 2 for vertex cover's standard LP — on the complete graph Kₙ every xᵥ = ½ is feasible, so LP-OPT ≤ n/2 against ILP-OPT = n − 1, a gap of 2 − 2/n (§21's tight examples, a single edge and K_{n,n}, are bipartite, where this LP is integral and the gap is 1) — Θ(log n) for set cover's (§23), and 1 for any problem whose constraint matrix is totally unimodular — which is why maximum flow, bipartite matching and shortest paths have integral LPs and are in P (Shortest Paths, MST & Flow: max-flow / min-cut is the textbook LP duality pair, and its integrality is exactly the reason augmenting-path algorithms return whole units of flow). branch and CUT — what a modern MIP solver actually runsat each node: 1. solve the LP relaxation the bound 2. if integral → a new incumbent; if LP-OPT ≥ incumbent → PRUNE §26's rule, verbatim 3. otherwise ADD CUTTING PLANES: inequalities valid for every INTEGER point of the feasible region but violated by the current fractional LP optimum. Re-solve. Repeat while the bound improves. • Gomory mixed-integer cuts, derived from the simplex tableau — general purpose • knapsack cover cuts: if ∑_{i∈C} aᵢ > b then ∑_{i∈C} xᵢ ≤ |C| − 1 • clique, flow-cover, MIR, and dozens of families a commercial solver rotates through 4. if no cut helps, BRANCH on a fractional variable plus: PRESOLVE (fix variables, tighten bounds, remove redundant rows — often halves the model), PRIMAL HEURISTICS (rounding, local search, feasibility pump) to get an incumbent EARLY, because §26 showed that a good incumbent is what makes the bound prune, and the MIP GAP (incumbent − best bound) / incumbent — reported continuously, so the user can stop at "within 0.1% of optimal" with a PROOF of that statement, which no heuristic on this page offers.

That last line is the practical answer to §19's question "what guarantee do you need?". A MIP solver run to completion returns a proved optimum. A MIP solver stopped early returns a solution and a bound, hence a proved bound on how far from optimal it might be — on this instance, which is far more useful than a worst-case ratio over all instances. It is not the only instance-specific guarantee on this page — §21's matching size is one for vertex cover, and §26's branch and bound certifies its answer — but it is the general one: it is available for any problem you can write as an ILP.

The phase transition: why random instances are usually easy

Generate a random 3-CNF formula by drawing m clauses independently, each on three distinct variables with independent uniform signs, and ask how the probability of satisfiability depends on the ratio α = m/n. With few clauses almost every formula is satisfiable and almost any assignment works; with many clauses almost none is, and a solver proves unsatisfiability quickly because contradictions appear near the top of the tree. Between those regimes the satisfiable fraction falls, and the instances that sit in the fall are the hard ones: the formula is almost satisfiable, so the search must look everywhere before concluding anything.

what is known, stated with its epistemic status the CONJECTURED asymptotic threshold for random 3-SAT: α₃ ≈ 4.267 — from the non-rigorous cavity method of statistical physics, and matched by large-scale experiment what is PROVED for k = 3: 3.52 ≤ α₃ ≤ 4.4898 — a lower bound by analysis of a greedy algorithm, an upper bound by a first-moment argument what is PROVED in general: a SHARP threshold sequence exists for every k (Friedgut), and for LARGE k the threshold's value is known exactly (Ding–Sly–Sun, 2015). k = 3 is still open. ► so "the threshold is 4.267" is a conjecture with strong numerical support, not a theorem, and this page writes it that way. ► and the threshold is an ASYMPTOTIC statement, about n → ∞. At any finite n the curve is a smooth sigmoid, not a step, and the ratio at which it crosses one half is NOT 4.267. measured — the real solver on freshly generated formulas, 200 per ratio, seed 12345, m/n swept from 2.00 to 7.00 in steps of 0.25 with 4.267 inserted percentage of the 200 formulas found SATISFIABLE m/n n = 14 n = 20 n = 30 n = 40 2.000 100.0% 100.0% 100.0% 100.0% 3.000 100.0% 100.0% 100.0% 100.0% 4.000 86.0% 81.0% 84.0% 86.0% 4.267 71.5% 72.5% 66.0% 56.0% 4.500 61.5% 53.5% 45.0% 43.5% 5.000 41.5% 28.5% 21.0% 8.5% 6.000 7.5% 5.5% 2.0% 0.0% 7.000 4.0% 0.0% 0.0% 0.0% the MEASURED 50% crossing, by linear interpolation between the two straddling ratios: n = 14 4.721 n = 20 4.583 n = 30 4.445 n = 40 4.379 the mean SEARCH-TREE SIZE in nodes per formula, at its peak and in the under-constrained regime: n = 14 peak 10.78 at m/n = 6.00 against 6.96 at m/n = 2.00 — 1.55 × n = 20 peak 16.16 at m/n = 5.00 against 9.21 at m/n = 2.00 — 1.75 × n = 30 peak 28.71 at m/n = 4.50 against 13.17 at m/n = 2.00 — 2.18 × n = 40 peak 49.18 at m/n = 4.75 against 16.85 at m/n = 2.00 — 2.92 × ► THE CURVE IS NOT THE THRESHOLD. The measured crossing is 4.721 at n = 14 — well above 4.267 — and it DESCENDS towards the conjectured value as n grows: 4.721, 4.583, 4.445, 4.379. It has not arrived at n = 40 and it is not supposed to have; the threshold is an n → ∞ statement. Reading "4.267" off a small-n picture is the finite-size error §33 names: the number in the sentence is not the number in the picture — and here BOTH numbers are correct, because they answer different questions. ► the EASY–HARD–EASY pattern is in the effort rows and it SHARPENS with n: the peak is 1.55 × the under-constrained cost at n = 14 and 2.92 × at n = 40. At these sizes it is a hump, not a spike; the dramatic version in the literature needs n in the hundreds and a solver that is not being re-run inside a page load. Note also that the effort peak lies above 4.267 at every n measured (6.00, 5.00, 4.50, 4.75) and, like the crossing, drifts down as n grows — consistent with the hard instances sitting where satisfiability is genuinely in doubt, which at finite n is not 4.267. On a 0.25-step grid with 200 formulas per ratio, the data cannot locate the peak more precisely than that, nor show it coinciding with the crossing. ► these are Monte-Carlo estimates from 200 samples. The standard error of a fraction near one half is √(0.25/200) = 3.5 percentage points, so a ±7-point wobble between columns is ordinary noise. The figure draws FRESH formulas at whatever settings you choose and will report slightly different numbers; that is sampling variation, not a disagreement. The trend ACROSS n — far larger than 7 points — is the only thing worth reading off the table.
The satisfiable fraction against the clause/variable ratio — every point measured by solving fresh random formulas, with the solver's effort on the same axes for each ratio the figure generates trials fresh random 3-CNF formulas on n variables and runs the real DPLL solver on each, plotting the fraction found satisfiable and the mean search-tree size; the dashed line at 4.267 is the conjectured asymptotic threshold and the solid marker is the measured crossing at this n — raise n and watch the second walk towards the first without arriving

Why industrial instances are not random instances

The phase transition explains why random formulas are easy away from the threshold; it does not explain why industrial formulas with millions of variables are easy, because those sit nowhere near the random model. The observed explanation is structure: real formulas have highly non-uniform variable degrees, a community structure that partitions them into loosely coupled clusters, and a small "backdoor" set of variables whose assignment makes the rest collapse under unit propagation. Clause learning exploits all three — it discovers the couplings between clusters and records them, and once the backdoor variables are set the remaining formula falls over. Random formulas at the threshold have none of this structure, which is why a few hundred random variables at α ≈ 4.27 can defeat a solver that dispatches a hardware verification instance with ten million.

  • The practical consequence is a workflow. Model the problem as SAT, MaxSAT, SMT or ILP and hand it to a solver; do not write a bespoke search unless the solver has been tried and has failed. The encoding matters far more than anything else — a good encoding of cardinality constraints, or of an all-different constraint, can change the running time by orders of magnitude — and encoding skill is the real expertise in this area.
  • Know which tool answers which question. SAT answers satisfiability. MaxSAT optimises the number of satisfied (soft) clauses and is the direct home of §24's problem. SMT adds theories — arithmetic, arrays, bit-vectors — so you need not encode integers into bits by hand. ILP optimises a linear objective over linear constraints. Constraint programming offers rich global constraints with specialised propagators. They overlap heavily and their relative performance is problem-specific.
  • The guarantee, stated precisely. A complete SAT solver that returns SAT hands you an assignment — a certificate anyone can check in linear time (§05). A complete solver that returns UNSAT can, if asked, emit a resolution proof, which is a certificate of the co-NP fact (§17) and can be enormous. An ILP solver returns an incumbent and a bound, so its answer is always accompanied by a proved optimality gap. Local-search solvers (§28's WalkSAT) return only the first kind and never the second.
  • None of this weakens §07. Every one of these solvers has a worst case that is exponential, and adversarial instances of very modest size — pigeonhole formulas, random parity constraints, crafted combinatorial benchmarks — defeat all of them. The theorem says such instances exist; the engineering says yours probably are not among them; and the only way to find out which is true for your problem is to run it.

31 NP-hardness in machine learning

Machine learning is one of the largest consumers of the coping strategies in this half of the page, and it is not obvious from the outside, because the field almost never says "this problem is NP-hard" — it says "we train with stochastic gradient descent", "we grow the tree greedily", "we run Lloyd's algorithm until convergence", "we use the ℓ₁ relaxation". Each of those is a response to a hardness result, and knowing which result it responds to tells you exactly what the method is and is not promising. This section lists the hardness theorems that actually bite in practice, attributes each correctly, and names the standard response beside it. The recurring shape is: the exact combinatorial problem is NP-hard; the field uses a convex relaxation, a greedy rule, or a local search; and the guarantee — where there is one — is conditional on an assumption about the data rather than universal.

Training

  • Training a three-node neural network is NP-complete (Blum and Rivest, 1992). The problem: given labelled examples in {0,1}ⁿ, do there exist weights for a network with two hidden linear-threshold units and one output unit that classifies every example correctly? That is as small as a network gets, the data are binary, and deciding consistency is already NP-complete — by reduction from set splitting. The result is about exact empirical risk minimisation, and it is the reason nobody looks for an algorithm that provably fits a network: backpropagation is a local search (Neural Network Training) over a non-convex surface, with no guarantee of reaching a global minimum and no claim that it does. What makes it work in practice is a separate and much-studied phenomenon — over-parameterisation, which changes the geometry of the loss surface (Neural Networks) — and not a repeal of the theorem.
  • Learning is hard in a second, different sense. Even where the optimisation is easy, proper PAC learning of some concept classes is NP-hard: finding the smallest consistent k-term DNF, or the smallest consistent decision tree, is hard even though a hypothesis from a larger class can be found in polynomial time. This is why the field so often learns an improper hypothesis — a bigger, messier model that predicts well — rather than the smallest one that fits, and why "find the simplest explanation" is a modelling aspiration rather than an algorithm. The bias-variance account of why a bigger hypothesis class is not automatically worse is Bias–Variance Trade-off.

Model structure

  • The minimum-size decision tree consistent with a sample is NP-hard (Hyafil and Rivest, 1976) — and so are most of its variants, including minimising the expected number of tests. The universal response is the greedy one: choose the split that maximises an impurity reduction, recurse, then prune. Decision Trees gives the splitting criteria and the pruning that follows them, and it is explicit that this is a heuristic with no optimality claim: greedy splitting can miss a small tree that requires a first split which looks useless in isolation, exactly the failure mode Greedy Algorithms analyses. Two footnotes worth carrying: the greedy rule has no approximation guarantee for tree size, and modern MIP-based optimal-tree solvers (§30's technology) now build provably optimal trees for small depths on moderate datasets — one of the clearest cases where a solver has moved the frontier of what "we must be greedy here" means.
  • Learning the structure of a Bayesian network is NP-hard (Chickering, 1996), and remains so with in-degree bounded by 2. Score-and-search methods (greedy hill climbing over DAGs with add/delete/reverse moves — local search, §28) and constraint-based methods (conditional-independence tests) are the standard responses; exact solvers based on dynamic programming over subsets (§26's Held–Karp pattern, applied to variable orderings) or on ILP (§30) handle a few dozen variables exactly.
  • Feature selection is subset selection. Choosing the best k features, or the sparsest regression with a given error, is NP-hard — this is the ℓ₀-constrained problem, hard by reduction from exact cover (Natarajan, 1995). Two responses dominate, and they have completely different guarantee structures. Convex relaxation: replace the ℓ₀ count by the ℓ₁ norm and solve a convex programme — the lasso — which recovers the true support under conditions on the design matrix (restricted isometry, irrepresentability) and gives no guarantee when those conditions fail; Regularization shows the geometry that makes ℓ₁ produce exact zeros. Greedy with submodularity: when the objective is monotone and submodular — coverage-like, with diminishing returns — greedy forward selection is within 1 − 1/e ≈ 0.632 of optimal (Nemhauser, Wolsey and Fisher, 1978), and that factor is optimal unless P = NP. This is §23's set-cover analysis in maximisation form, and the caution is the same: the guarantee needs submodularity, which for a criterion such as validation accuracy is an assumption, usually a false one.

Clustering

  • k-means is NP-hard — in general dimension even for k = 2, and in the plane for general k. Lloyd's algorithm is a local search, and saying so explains everything about its behaviour: it alternates two exact minimisations (assign points to the nearest centre; move each centre to its cluster's mean), each of which weakly decreases the objective, so it converges — to a local minimum whose quality depends entirely on the initialisation, which is why it is restarted and why k-means++ seeding exists. k-means++ is the one piece of this with a theorem: its seeding gives a solution within O(log k) of optimal in expectation, before Lloyd's iterations even begin. Clustering has the algorithm and the failure modes; §19's "give up optimality" is precisely the door being used.
  • k-centre has a clean 2-approximation and it is tight. Minimise the largest distance from a point to its assigned centre: the farthest-first traversal — repeatedly open a centre at the point furthest from all current centres — is a 2-approximation under the triangle inequality, by an argument in the same shape as §21's and §22's, and no (2 − ε)-approximation exists unless P = NP. This is the clustering objective for which the approximation theory is cleanest, and it is worth knowing that it exists, because the k-means objective's corresponding results are far weaker.
  • Correlation clustering, hierarchical objectives, and choosing k. Most alternative formulations are NP-hard too, and the responses are the same three: LP or SDP relaxation with rounding, greedy with a proved ratio where the objective is submodular, local search where it is not. Single-linkage hierarchical clustering is the exception that proves the rule: it is Kruskal's algorithm stopped early (Clustering), it is polynomial, and it is polynomial because the objective it optimises — maximising the minimum inter-cluster spacing — has a matroid-flavoured exchange argument behind it.

Inference

  • Exact MAP inference in a general graphical model is NP-hard, and computing marginals is #P-hard — a counting class above NP (§03). The tractable islands are exactly the ones §27 predicts: inference is polynomial when the graph has bounded treewidth, by dynamic programming over a tree decomposition, and the chain case of that is the Viterbi algorithm on a trellis, which is the DAG shortest-path algorithm in disguise (Hidden Markov Models & CRFs).
